#10423. 5 smart home devices that save you money (and 3 that are a waste)

The secret to smart home technology isn’t making your house feel like a sci-fi movie—it’s focusing strictly on your return on investment.

A handful of smart devices will pay for themselves within a year or two through lower utility bills and prevented disasters. Others, however, are expensive novelties that will never recoup their cost.

Here are five smart home upgrades that deliver genuine financial savings, along with three overengineered gadgets you should skip.

Smart devices that actually save money

Smart thermostats

Heating and cooling account for over half of the average home’s energy consumption. Traditional programmable thermostats fail because nobody wants to manually reprogram a clunky digital interface every time their schedule changes.

Smart thermostats like Nest and Ecobee use geofencing and motion sensors to know when you’ve left the house, automatically easing back the HVAC. Most households save around 10% on heating and 15% on cooling.

As a bonus, many electric utility companies offer rebates just for installing one.

Smart water leak sensors

An unnoticed slow drip behind a washing machine or under a water heater can ruin floorboards, rot drywall, and breed mold long before you spot water pooling in the hallway. The average residential water damage claim costs more than $10,000.

A three-pack of basic Wi-Fi leak sensors costs less than $50. Tucking them under sinks, behind toilets, and next to your water heater ensures you get an instant push notification on your phone the second a drop of water hits the sensor contacts.

Plus, some home insurance carriers offer discounts on your annual policy premium if you install connected leak detection hardware.

Smart plugs with energy monitoring

Even when turned off, big-screen TVs, game consoles, audio receivers, and desktop computers continuously pull “phantom power”—or “vampire draw”—from wall outlets 24 hours a day.

Smart plugs featuring live energy monitoring show you exactly how many kilowatt-hours an outlet is consuming in real time. You can set auto-cut routines that completely sever wall power to media centers or home office desks overnight, saving you some cash in idle electricity costs.

Smart sprinkler controllers

Traditional irrigation timers are notoriously dumb. They fire off lawn sprinklers at 5:00 a.m. regardless of whether it rained an inch at midnight or a downpour is forecast for two hours later.

Connected irrigation controllers such as Rachio sync directly with hyperlocal weather data to skip watering cycles automatically when rain, wind, or freezing temperatures occur. Homeowners with large lawns routinely cut their outdoor water usage by up to half during spring and summer months.

Smart light switches (not bulbs)

Replacing every light bulb in your house with a $15 smart bulb gets expensive fast, and if someone flips the physical wall switch off, the bulb dies anyway. Smart in-wall switches replace the wall controls directly.

Installing smart switches or motion-sensing switches in high-traffic zones (hallways, basements, kids’ bedrooms) ensures lights turn off automatically after periods of inactivity. Pairing them with LED fixtures keeps lighting costs down to pennies per month.

3 smart devices that are a financial waste

Smart refrigerators with touchscreens

Paying an extra $1,000 to $2,000 for a giant tablet built into your refrigerator door is one of the worst tech investments you can make.

The internal computer and display will stop receiving software updates in 5 years, while the rest of the fridge is supposed to last 15. Furthermore, the internal cameras meant to “show you what’s inside while at the grocery store” are routinely obscured by milk cartons and leftovers. You’re paying a huge premium for a screen that will inevitably lag long before the compressor breaks down.

Motorized smart blinds

Motorized window shades that open and close automatically via app control sound luxurious, but the math behind them doesn’t add up.

Outfitting a typical home with custom motorized blinds costs between $2,000 and $5,000. While keeping shades drawn during hot summer afternoons does reduce solar heat gain slightly, the energy savings amounts to maybe $20 or $30 a year. It would take more than a century of energy savings to recoup the initial hardware investment.

Connected appliance dongles and ‘smart’ small gadgets

From Wi-Fi-enabled trash cans that order garbage bags to smart soap dispensers and connected toaster ovens, the consumer market is flooded with microappliances that add app connectivity just to raise retail prices.

These devices solve nonexistent problems while introducing security risks and unnecessary app clutter. They offer zero energy efficiency gains and carry higher repair failure rates than their simple, mechanical counterparts.

#10422. The biggest mistake people make when trying to persuade others

Here’s the bad news: The deeper your understanding of an issue and the stronger your conviction about the solution, the worse you often become at persuasion.

It’s due to a cognitive trap that the fields of behavioral economics and neuroscience have spent decades documenting: the curse of knowledge. In other words, your knowledge has fundamentally altered your perception of what you believe others are experiencing. It applies to your persuasion—especially when you are the well-informed expert in the room.

Here’s how this curse blindsides you in persuasion. You’ve spent weeks developing a proposal to solve a complex problem. You’re now completely convinced of the way forward. By the time you present your proposal to decision-makers, you’re already deep in the curse of knowledge. You cannot accurately imagine what your proposal sounds like to anyone who’s encountering it for the first time.  

Now you can start to understand the baffling experience all of us have had: presenting what feels like an obvious and clear case for an idea and watching it hit a brick wall. From our perspective, everything is clear, logical, and persuasive. To further complicate matters, we become invested in a solution—our solution.

Pride of ownership deepens our commitment. When stakeholders start to raise challenging questions, we say to ourselves: They must not be listening carefully. They must be resistant to change or protective of the status quo. The possibility that we’ve failed to build a bridge from their knowledge to ours doesn’t even occur to us, because we cannot see the gap.

How to vanquish the curse of knowledge

There is an antidote to the curse of knowledge. You must abandon your mental model of persuasion as first and foremost a solution-centric activity. Masterful persuasion starts with problem definition, not solution definition.

It’s not an easy perspective to accept. After all, you want to solve a problem efficiently and quickly. And who can resist the ego rewards of “pride of ownership” and the public recognition of how clever you are? Instead, with problem-centric persuasion, you’ll experience a different kind of reward: meaningful collaborations with colleagues in solving truly thorny problems. But this approach requires far more from your curiosity and skills of engagement, and, at the start, far less from your expertise and advocacy skills.

Truly effective persuasion begins with curiosity, not certainty. It begins with questions, not answers. It begins with exploring problems, not advocating solutions. At a certain point later in the process, you will make a pitch and advocate for a particular solution. But this will occur only after you’ve gathered intelligence and built a rich understanding of the problem through others. Then you absolutely need to advance a clear recommendation. But the sequence matters profoundly. Questions first, pitches second.

Not so surprisingly, one of the trademark traits of leaders who are masterful at persuasion is that they’re instinctually curious. Before they try to change your mind, they work to understand it. They ask about your concerns, your priorities, your constraints, your resources, your values. Foundational to their curiosity is their capacity to listen.

What can we conclude from our exploration into the curse of knowledge and its associated traps?

First, resist the temptation to go solo in researching the problem you’re confronting. At the start, make it a collaborative process. Engage a breadth of stakeholders, both decision-makers and implementers. Before you formulate your pitch, formulate your questions. Design them to go deep—to understand the problem from multiple angles and the implications for all your stakeholders. Ask yourself: What do I need to understand about this person’s situation, preferences, values, identity, resources, and constraints? Then go ask them.

This discovery process will at times feel like a setback; your original perspective on the issue or a solution you’re contemplating just became irrelevant. That’s progress. You’ve avoided wasting your persuasive capital on an argument that would have fallen flat. And you’ve conducted the reconnaissance needed to see the problem more fully and to craft a solution that will be enthusiastically supported and implemented.

That’s the paradox at the heart of persuasion: The fastest path to changing someone’s mind is to first spend time truly understanding it.

#10420. Anthropic is printing money, but AI isn’t paying off outside tech

Good morning. Today I’m reading about Netflix’s bloody week, playing with Apple Music’s fun new 404 page, mourning Arc’s untimely demise, and devouring Maggie O’Farrell’s new novel, Land.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

Anthropic is going public, and thanks to its S-1, we now have access to some of the mouthwatering numbers behind the IPO. In the first half of the year, the company reported $16.3 billion in revenue, up from $1.2 billion for the same period of 2025. If you’ve been following the AI phenomenon at all, you’ll know why: Anthropic’s Claude Code kicked off the AI coding boom in early 2026, causing every company from Google to OpenAI to largely pivot to the enterprise. By the summer we were talking about “token-maxxing” as Claude Fable 5.1 ate billions of tokens every day.

With a rumored valuation approaching $1.5 trillion, Anthropic’s IPO, when it does happen, will be the largest of all time, eclipsing SpaceXAI’s by billions of dollars. The interesting thing about the numbers, at least to me, is how much cash is on hand: of the $141 billion raised so far, it has over $112 billion remaining.

However, Anthropic has committed to spending hundreds of billions of dollars with Google, Amazon, Microsoft, and SpaceX for cloud infrastructure, and expects to be compute-constrained over the next year. All of the major hyperscalers, including Google, have are prioritizing their own AI workloads over competitors’, meaning that Anthropic will be on the hunt for newer and potentially less reliable providers, with data centers largely powered by natural gas to keep costs down.

The data in this prospectus are truly outrageous. Anthropic did not exist until 2021 and was largely seen as an ideological research outfit until fairly recently. The notion that it would be one of the hottest stocks of ever was laughable, but here we are.

Conversely, a viral post by Apollo chief economist Torsten Slok last week is making the argument that except for tech, AI is not moving the needle.

In a number of compelling charts, Slok outlines the fact that while the Magnificent 7 are propping up the US economy, the rest of the country’s largest businesses — the S&P 493, as he calls them — are not seeing rising profits or meaningful productivity gains.

“Despite massive investments and widespread hype around artificial intelligence,” it begins, “the technology has delivered zero financial payoff to corporate America outside of tech. There are still no signs of AI boosting productivity growth.”

Perhaps most concerning is where AI spend is concentrated: 80% of the top 1% of all AI spenders in the US are VC-backed companies, reinforcing the idea that frontier models are still the darlings of Silicon Valley more than Main Street.

Ultimately, both things can be true: the AI labs are minting money, but most other businesses haven’t seen the returns.

Welcome to Cc:, where I ask interesting people in the community about what’s making them happy both inside and outside the world of tech. This week, I’m speaking with C. Scott Brown, a video creator at Android Authority and on his own channel.

What do you do for work?I’m a video creator at Android Authority and on my solo channel, C. Scott Brown (clever name, I know).

What do you do for fun?I enjoy cycling and hiking quite a bit. I spend a lot of time with my partner watching movies, too, as we’re both big film buffs.

What is your relationship like with technology these days?Technology has always been a huge part of my life. I really love it. I love when a piece of technology makes my life better/easier, even if only in a small way. I genuinely believe that technology is what moves humanity forward.

What are three apps you can’t live without and why?The Homey app is so important to me because my entire smart home runs through my Homey Pro, and I don’t even know what my life would look like without it. I’m also addicted to Reddit, and the Relay app is my way of accessing it, so I open it about 400 times a day. Finally, the Tautulli app keeps me hooked into what’s going on with my Plex server, so I check that a lot each day, too.

What is a gadget that you love right now?I just got done reviewing the Fitbit Edge. I really like the Edge, but while reviewing it, I had to set my Pixel Watch 5 aside for the week. Now that the Edge review is done, I am very, very happy to have my Pixel Watch 5 back!

How does AI fit into your life right now?I use AI to solve many practical problems in my life. Being a video creator involves a lot of small, mind-numbing tasks, and AI helps me do them faster or, in some cases, remove them entirely from my plate. However, I will never use AI to take over any creative aspects of my job. That’s the fun stuff!

What’s a book you recently read and loved?The YouTube Formula by Derral Eves. I’m still learning so much about YouTube and how to improve my videos, and this book has been very helpful. I just wish it was written a bit better (sorry, Derral).

What’s an album (or two) you recently listened to and loved?My two favorite albums of 2026 so far are Get Mean by Boy Harsher and Fantasia by Slift. I love the minimalist simplicity of Boy Harsher’s music. They’ve locked into a vibe and just beat it into the ground. They’re like the AC/DC of synthwave. Meanwhile, Slift is such an amazing band, and it’s so refreshing to hear loud rock music that isn’t overproduced, radio-friendly, or trying to fit into a modern scene. Fantasia is just guitars cranked up to 11, screaming vocals, pounding drums, and everything else I’ve ever loved about loud music.

What’s a TV show you recently watched and loved?I mean, the only answer in 2026 is Widow’s Bay. It’s literally perfect. Perfect cast, perfect storylines, funny when it needs to be, just enough scares, terrific drama…there’s nothing to dislike about it.

What’s a movie you recently watched and loved?Everyone Is Lying to You for Money was a banger. It was preaching to the choir for me because I’ve always known crypto was a giant sham, but even if we set that aside, the film was immensely enjoyable and way funnier than I expected.

What’s a game you recently played and loved?Dungeons of Hinterberg was one of my recent discoveries. It was like Persona meets Zelda with a millennial going through an existential crisis. It sounds awful when I write it out like that, but I thought it was terrific!

What’s something offline you do every day that brings you joy?Drink a cup of tea. Or three or four cups. It is the simplest and most effective way to make me happy.

Here’s a less cheerful follow-up to last week’s foldable excitement: some iPhone Duo owners are reporting cracked inner screens. Teardowns have raised possible explanations involving pressure from the hinge mechanism, which JerryRigEverything tries to prove in his latest video.

Apple has now acknowledged the issue is affecting “a small number” of units and says owners should contact AppleCare for a replacement. Still, however small the number, it’s likely to tarnish Apple’s big launch in some way, and it certainly won’t help dispel the belief that foldables are just too fragile for the mainstream.

I’m not surprised about this change; anonymized Gmail and Photos data is like crack for advertisers, and Google has finally found a way to justify utilizing it. Yes, if a service is free, then you’re the product, but Google is also asking you to pay a lot of money for Gemini, so the bargain doesn’t feel as fair anymore.

#10419. 9 Checks Before Shipping a Product Email: A Workflow for Developers and Designers

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A product email can look correct in a design file and still fail its reader. The primary action may disappear when images are blocked. A long workspace name may break the layout. A staging link may survive into production. Even when the message renders correctly, the application may send it twice.

These failures cross design, frontend, backend, and delivery boundaries. A useful release process checks each boundary independently and records what the tests actually establish.

This article uses a workspace invitation as an example. The recipient needs to understand who invited them, identify the workspace, and open a valid acceptance link. The same workflow applies to account notifications, activity summaries, and many other product emails.

Write a short behavioral contract before designing the template:

This separates visual preferences from required behavior. A rounded button is a design choice; a usable invitation link is a requirement.

Build fixtures for a short workspace name, a long name, an accented inviter name, missing optional information, and a translated version. Use synthetic recipients and tokens.

Designers should review these fixtures early. A layout approved only with ideal copy leaves developers to invent overflow behavior later.

MJML provides email-oriented components that compile to responsive HTML. React Email offers a component-based workflow suited to teams already using React.

Choose according to the team’s maintenance needs. Introducing both usually adds complexity without helping a single template.

Regardless of authoring tool, review the generated HTML. The compiled document is what the sending pipeline passes onward, and it can differ substantially from the source component.

Check that the output contains the expected heading, action URL, image alternatives, language information, and footer. Confirm that production rendering uses the same dependency versions as the reviewed build.

SitePoint’s HTML email coding guide provides useful background on email markup. Treat older examples as starting points and verify compatibility against the clients your audience actually uses.

Avoid adding application JavaScript to an email template. Put interactive workflows on a web page and use the email to explain and link to the next action.

Browser support and email-client support are different things. A layout working in Chrome does not establish that it works in every Outlook or Gmail environment.

Use Can I Email to investigate the HTML and CSS features your design depends on. Read the support notes and test dates, not only the headline support percentage.

Define a small client matrix using available audience evidence. Record the specific client and platform; “Outlook” alone is too broad to describe a test environment.

For the invitation example, the baseline should preserve:

SitePoint’s HTML email tips and tricks offers additional implementation context.

Design the baseline first. Decorative effects can improve the experience, but they should not carry information that disappears when a client ignores a CSS rule.

Use visible text for the essential message. A large image containing the entire invitation fails when images are unavailable and gives readers less control over text presentation.

Give meaningful images appropriate alternatives, and use empty alternative text for purely decorative images. Choose descriptive link labels such as “Accept your workspace invitation” instead of repeating “Click here.”

Two useful tools address different questions:

Neither establishes accessibility across every email client. A client may modify markup or colors after delivery.

Manually inspect reading order, text enlargement, keyboard access to links, and the message with images unavailable. Check the delivered email with relevant assistive technology when that is part of your supported environment.

For dark mode, examine the actual resulting colors. Changing a browser preview’s preferred color scheme does not reproduce every email client’s color transformations.

A template preview tests the template. It does not verify that the application selects the right recipient, subject, language, or invitation token.

Route development email to Mailpit, a local SMTP testing tool, and trigger the real application flow using synthetic data.

Mailpit also provides HTML compatibility and link-checking features. Review what a link checker will request before using it on action URLs. An automated request should not consume a single-use invitation or trigger a state-changing action.

A successful capture proves that the development application generated a message. It does not prove that a production mailbox provider will accept it or place it in the inbox.

A perfectly rendered email with a localhost link is still broken.

For a specific invitation flow, an explicit destination validator can catch common mistakes before rendering. The following example assumes invitations must use HTTPS, a single approved hostname, an exact path, and a token:

Run this standalone example with node --test invitation-url.test.mjs. Replace the hostname and route with your application’s documented contract before integrating it.

This checks URL shape, not token authenticity, expiry, or authorization. The receiving application must enforce those properties.

Apply the validator before inserting the URL into a template, and use context-appropriate escaping during rendering. Inspect the final delivered link too, because a sending provider may rewrite it for tracking.

Playwright is useful for repeatable checks against a rendered HTML preview. Test narrow widths, long content, missing optional fields, and the presence of essential text and links.

A browser test can assert that the action has the expected accessible name and destination. It can also capture screenshots for human review.

Keep the limitation explicit: browser automation does not emulate an email client’s HTML rewriting or all of its rendering behavior.

Use an email-client testing service such as Litmus, or controlled test accounts in the clients you support, to inspect the delivered result. Include light and dark appearances where relevant.

Record the template version and fixture alongside each preview. Otherwise, a designer and developer may approve screenshots from different builds.

Do not accept screenshot updates automatically. A changed image may reveal a genuine regression, including truncated text or a button that no longer looks actionable.

Email generation often happens in a background job. That means the release process must account for retries and partial failures.

Give the business event a stable identifier. Record which invitation event produced which send attempt, and use your queue or provider’s documented deduplication mechanisms where available.

Do not promise exactly-once sending merely because a database flag says “sent.” A crash between provider acceptance and saving that flag creates an ambiguous state.

Preserve enough event history to investigate that ambiguity without logging raw invitation tokens. Also separate “accepted by the sending provider” from “delivered to the receiving system,” and from “seen by the user.”

Rendering quality and delivery behavior require separate evidence. Review authentication, bounce handling, complaint signals, suppression behavior, and provider feedback before increasing production volume.

Start with the relevant mailbox provider’s requirements. For example, Gmail’s sender guidelines explain authentication and other requirements, with additional obligations for bulk senders. Check the requirements for your actual sending pattern rather than treating one checklist as universal.

For vendor research at this stage, treat vendor-published comparisons as vendor-authored material: evaluate their claims against provider policies and your own delivery evidence.

Warmup activity is not a substitute for appropriate recipient expectations, correct authentication, or operational monitoring. It also cannot establish that a template is accessible or that an invitation token is valid.

Choose tools according to the question being investigated. A compatibility lookup explains feature support; an SMTP capture shows message construction; a client preview shows rendering; provider events describe delivery outcomes. Keeping those responsibilities clear makes failures easier to diagnose.

A release is ready when the team can explain what the message does, demonstrate the important failure cases, and identify which evidence supports each conclusion.

#10418. Gen Z is challenging ‘performative availability.’ Why aren’t Millennials and Gen X doing the same?

For decades now, American workers have adhered to an unspoken deal that, in order to be successful professionally, some sacrifices need to be made. Whether it be checking work emails after dinner, taking work calls while on vacation, or being willing to be available to your boss on a Sunday, these behaviors became commonplace demonstrations of professional commitment. No one ever said any of it out loud, but through osmosis, employees came to accept that behavior as the standard in order to glean the professional rewards that they were conditioned to value.

But what if all those sacrifices weren’t worth it?

A new report from Software Finder, a software comparison platform, poses some interesting questions about the deal we’ve made with the workplace. In a survey of 1,006 American workers, 71% of respondents felt pressured to be available outside of working hours. Millennials and Gen X reported slightly higher levels of pressure, with 72% stating they felt pressure to respond to work after hours, compared with 65% of Gen Z. However, the real takeaway might be that 69% of respondents felt that their after-hours availability had not meaningfully impacted their careers, and just 8% directly linked a promotion to being available outside of working hours.

We’ve grown accustomed to the narrative that Gen Z is breaking workplace norms, often speculating that they lack the ambition or commitment of their predecessors. However, these numbers make us think: Rather than wonder why Gen Z seems less willing to conform to a workplace culture that expects them to be always on, maybe we should be wondering why older generations are still conforming to a workplace culture that seems to offer little reward.

The workplace bargain we inherited

Millennials and Gen X might’ve come into the workforce under different circumstances, but they both found themselves in an environment that seemed to demand a certain kind of professionalism. Someone who was dependable, loyal, and even willing to do more than what was asked. Technology enabled those behaviors to extend beyond the office. Instead of working late, you could work at home.

Now, is there anything wrong with occasionally making yourself available outside working hours? Of course not. Some responsibilities require flexibility, and different stages of our careers come with different demands. The issue isn’t with occasionally being willing to be available outside working hours. The issue is when that willingness becomes an unofficial barometer of professional commitment in an environment where no one has ever really defined what you’re expected to do and what you can expect in return.

Which brings us back to these generational differences. Gen Z hasn’t shown that they don’t value hard work, and the survey certainly can’t support the claim that they’re resisting workplace norms. However, younger workers may be coming into the workplace with a different set of assumptions about professional commitment and personal time. Having seen the effects of burnout and economic instability on older generations, they may be more likely to question whether constant availability is a fair exchange for professional success.

Performative availability

There’s another angle to this conversation that I think is critically important. In many workplaces, responsiveness has become synonymous with responsibility. If you respond to a message at 9 p.m., you’re a dedicated employee. If you respond at the start of the next workday, you’re lazy.

I call this performative availability, the compulsion to demonstrate professional commitment based on visibility instead of the value or importance of your contributions.

This matters because organizations don’t always recognize the behaviors that they claim to value. Managers will tell you that it’s not about the hours, it’s about the results. However, they might also tell you that you’re a dedicated employee because you respond to messages late at night. Another manager might tout work-life balance, only to send employees emails on the weekend and expect immediate responses. These contradictions create expectations that aren’t written down, but that can be remarkably influential.

Software Finder’s survey responses complicate matters further. While most respondents admitted that their after-hours availability had not meaningfully impacted their careers, some employees who identified as always on reported that their constant availability had meaningfully helped them in their careers. This suggests that the rewards might not be nonexistent, just uneven.

Are older generations compounding the issue?

The most intriguing possibility is that millennials and Gen X aren’t just experiencing these workplace expectations. In fact, as they ascend to supervisory or leadership roles, they might be compounding these expectations.

Imagine a manager who spent their career answering emails on weekends, saying yes to last-minute requests, and making personal sacrifices in order to be professionally successful. Even if they now recognize the cost of those behaviors, they might still equate availability with commitment because that was what they were taught throughout their career. Unintentionally, they’re asking younger employees to demonstrate professional commitment in the same way.

Again, this isn’t necessarily hypocritical behavior. This is how workplace norms survive. Employees observe what behaviors are rewarded and, over time, internalize those expectations in order to pass them on to others. Practices that weren’t formally expected become an organization’s definition of professionalism.

In my own research on Gen Z leadership preferences, this emphasis on fairness, structure, and clear expectations has come up time and again. These qualities are important because employees shouldn’t have to read between the lines to understand whether looking at messages during dinner will impact how they’re evaluated by their supervisor. Employees shouldn’t have to make personal sacrifices in order to demonstrate that they care about their work.

Leaders can start to fix this by differentiating between actual performance needs and performative expectations. They can set guidelines for after-hours communication, stop rewarding performative availability, and start rewarding employees for their actual contributions to the organization instead of how often they make themselves available.

A question for every generation

Workplace disagreements can easily devolve into generational disagreements. Gen Z won’t just abide by workplace norms, millennials are burned out, and Gen X just learned to deal with the demands of professional life. However, these broad brushstrokes miss the forest for the trees. Employees of all generations are dealing with workplace expectations that were formed by organizations, not employees.

Gen Z may not have uncovered a game-changing approach to work. Younger workers may simply be questioning workplace norms that previous generations didn’t have the opportunity, or willingness, to question.

And maybe that’s the opportunity. Perhaps organizations can start questioning some of the workplace norms that employees are expected to conform to.

Because if employees are being evaluated on how available they are, and not on what they’re actually capable of contributing to an organization, it’s not a generational issue.

It’s a managerial issue.

#10416. Inside KFC’s big plan to win the chicken wars

On a busy street in Dubai, there’s a building that’s impossible to miss. Its bright red facade glows with electric white lights. Three giant archways offer passersby a glimpse of a two-story screen playing constant editorial-style videos. Inside, a stark, almost brutalist construction is offset by two-tone red furniture and a neon wall mural. 

It’s not a club or a luxury store—it’s a KFC.

We’re in the middle of a modern chicken war, and KFC wants to reclaim its icon status. Chicken—more specifically, the chicken tender—is undeniably having a moment, even as the rest of the fast-casual food space continues to languish in a post-pandemic, cost-of-living-induced slump. Rising stars like Raising Cane’s, Dave’s Hot Chicken, and Hangry Joe’s Hot Chicken & Wings are doing major sales and social media numbers by wooing young consumers with their signature sauces and chunky pickles. Meanwhile, legacy chicken purveyor KFC has faded into the background.

Now, KFC is ready to throw its drumstick back into the ring. After testing new chicken concepts, beverages, and brand approaches for the past several years, KFC is undertaking a major transformation to reclaim chicken supremacy—including, starting on October 31, testing out what it calls a restaurant concept of the future. 

Catherine Tan-Gillespie, a KFC veteran who has been with the brand since 2015, took over as president of KFC U.S. in April 2025. Upon stepping into the role, she noted: “It was so obvious that there’s this huge latent love for the brand. We’re an American icon.” At the same time, it was equally clear that while people still hold fond memories of the chain, the challenge lies in keeping it current. In 2025, Raising Cane’s bumped it out of the top three U.S. chicken restaurants by sales, a list still led by Chick-fil-A and Popeyes.

The market share loss, Tan-Gillespie recalls, “was the catalyst for, ‘Okay, we’ve got to do something urgently around making KFC more relevant, more easy, and more distinctive.’”

KFC’s comeback includes a refreshed menu, an entirely new brand platform, and an advertising campaign that brings back Colonel Sanders. The brand’s latest move, though, is perhaps its most ambitious yet. Through a collaboration with the global agency JKR, KFC is reimagining the design of its restaurants from the ground up. 

The state of play: A primer on the 2026 chicken wars

If you were online in the late 2010s, you probably remember the chicken sandwich showdown that saw industry giants Popeyes and Chick-fil-A duking it out for fans’ favor. In 2026, though, the chicken war playing field looks quite a bit different—in large part due to an influx of hot new contenders. 

Over the past several years, chicken has become a boon for the fast-casual space. According to a report from the market research company Circana, total restaurant traffic for all quick-service restaurant (QSR) brands for the year ending in September 2026 was down 4% from 2019, while chicken spots were up 20%. Per the food-and-beverage trend tracker Datassential, chicken was the strongest-performing segment across all QSR and fast-casual restaurants in 2025, growing total units by 4.6% and sales by 4% across 28 brands.

This supercharged boost has been spearheaded by underdogs like Hangry Joe’s, which was Datassential’s fastest-growing chain in America in 2024 by unit count; Raising Cane’s, which has been on a months-long store-opening tear; and Dave’s Hot Chicken, which Datassential notes expanded its store count by over 34% in 2025 and its sales by nearly 45%.

As new chicken restaurants begin to carve out their own market share, legacy players like Popeyes and KFC are scrambling to pick up the slack. In an interview with MarketWatch, Mark Kalinowski, CEO of Kalinowski Equity Research, noted that in 1999, KFC’s U.S. market share stood at 39.7% of the limited-service chicken segment. By 2025, Chick-fil-A was far and away the leader, with around 45% market share. Popeyes had 11.5%, Raising Cane’s had 11%, and KFC had just 10%.

From around early 2024 to mid-2025, KFC saw a sales slump that added up to seven straight quarters of falling U.S. same-store sales. That figure finally ticked into the green in the third quarter of 2025 and has remained positive since, including a 2% gain in the second quarter of 2026, which ended in June. 

It’s looking like KFC’s comeback is finally gaining a bit of headway—and the brand has a plan to keep it going.

Phase one of the chicken turnaround

KFC’s turnaround plan was in the works for years before it started showing up in the numbers. On the product side, KFC has invested in figuring out how to elevate its menu from a nostalgic memory into something more modern and craveable.

In 2024, it launched Saucy, an Orlando-based concept restaurant focused around chicken tenders and sauces. The eye-wateringly neon pink restaurant—designed to appeal to younger generations—tested out 11 new sauce flavors, including cayenne barbecue, chimichurri ranch, and sweet teriyaki. 

Since the first Saucy location opened, the spin-off has scaled to 16 additional restaurants. KFC describes them as a “learning lab” to figure out what today’s guest wants from the brand, and several of its custom sauces have become part of KFC’s “global sauce pantry.”

Christophe Poirier, KFC’s chief concept officer, says Saucy’s success has resulted in several key takeaways: “One, you can explore—the world is ready. Second, if you stick to what is expected, that’s the highway to the graveyard of brands. And third, new generations of consumers are very much mavericks in the ways they experience brands.”

Clearly, the test worked: This June, KFC announced a next-generation global menu featuring a lineup of over 20 sauces and new “Dunked” tenders, wings, and sandwiches, all of which come “drenched in sauce.” It also debuted “Kwench,” a beverage platform boasting drinks like boba refreshers, iced coffees, and “Krunch Shakes” as an obvious play to capitalize on Gen Z’s “little treat” drink culture. (McDonald’s, Starbucks, and Taco Bell are just a few other chains that are currently fine-tuning their beverage offerings for younger consumers.)

As KFC modernizes its menu, it has simultaneously used nostalgia to get fans back into stores. In August 2025, the brand announced the return of its cult-favorite potato wedges and hot and spicy wings, tweeting simply: “HERE, DAMN.” The post has more than 80 million views and nearly 500,000 likes. A press release called reviving these items part of KFC’s fight “back to icon status.”

While KFC declined to share specific numbers on the launch’s success with Fast Company, the brand noted that “we went on to see the first of four consecutive quarters of same-store sales growth after seven consecutive quarters of negative same-store sales growth, so we’re encouraged by that progress.”

Into the “Bucketverse”

If the essence of KFC’s new menu is “nostalgia meets modern,” its refreshed brand approach is something similar. This April, the brand reinvested in its marketing with a campaign called “Finger Lickin’ Machine.” The 90-second spot—which accompanied the launch of KFC’s $7, $9, and $11 Box Feast meals—shows Colonel Sanders busting a move in a boardroom meeting as the gloomy executives surrounding him complain about the brand’s low prices. 

The campaign is clearly intended to emphasize KFC’s renewed focus on value amid the ongoing consumer spending slowdown, but it serves the dual purpose of recentering KFC’s advertising around its most iconic brand asset, Colonel Sanders. The character is now reimagined as less of a wise old man and more of an athletic, swaggering, chicken purveyor for the people.

“It’s about making sure that we remain distinctively KFC: We have a feisty edge, an entrepreneurial edge,” Tan-Gillespie says. “The colonel was the ultimate showman, and the bucket is also super important.”

That bucket—the iconic red-and-white striped vessel—became the centerpiece of KFC’s recent rebrand, which was designed by JKR and unveiled in June. The new look includes an updated logo, typography, packaging, and app design. Threaded through it all is the image of the bucket, which has been reimagined as a frame to hold food photography, illustration, and campaign messaging. It’s a system that will give KFC much more flexibility in how it shows up on social media, in marketing, and in the stores themselves. 

“Now it’s much more kinetic; it’s also much more immersive,” Poirier says. “This is why we call it the ‘Bucketverse.’”

With a revamped menu, an updated marketing plan, and a global brand refresh, KFC was ready for the most ambitious part of its comeback plan: building the chicken restaurant of the future. 

The fast-food restaurant of the future

At the same time that JKR was crafting KFC’s rebrand, the agency was thinking about how it would show up in KFC’s restaurants. It was tasked with designing a modular restaurant concept that epitomized the next chapter of the brand, which could also be broken down into smaller parts and applied to existing and future stores. Dubbed project “Muse,” the first concept store opened on October 31 in Dubai, and two others are currently underway in Seoul and the U.K. Eventually, insights from the concept stores could find their way into KFC’s global fleet of 34,000 locations (and counting). 

Sam Smith, an executive business director at JKR, describes Muse as “the best physical expression of the brand we’ve created for KFC.” While conceptualizing the space, JKR didn’t look at other chicken restaurants—or even other restaurants, period. Instead, Smith’s team decided that the best way to imagine a chicken restaurant of the future was to take inspiration from outside the fast-food category.

“How do you connect with people in the real physical world when so much is digital now?” she says. “How could we really use our restaurant spaces, which are the heart of the brand, to create this? We looked everywhere—from hospitality to fashion, entertainment, technology, and luxury.”

KFC says that Dubai, in the United Arab Emirates, was chosen as the flagship Muse site because it’s “one of the world’s most demanding, cosmopolitan, and hospitality-centric markets—a place where guests already expect world-class design and experience.” That combination made Dubai ideal to try out an experimental format that might not immediately land elsewhere. 

Indeed, the first Muse location looks more like a modern art museum or sneaker shop-turned-nightclub than your childhood KFC. The restaurant is two stories tall with a stark red exterior, three tall archways, and a giant, illuminated outline of a bucket. Through its glass windows, a massive screen is visible even from the outside of the space. Smith calls this screen the “chicken theater.” Rather than displaying promotional material, which is the typical fare for QSR advertising, the chicken theater is constantly cycling through imagery of KFC dishes displayed like “a fashion shoot.” 

“The content whets your appetite at different times of day with different product ranges,” she adds. “At lunchtime, it’s obviously focusing on lunch. Then it changes to more snacks, and you get fed with more beverage information during the afternoon. And then it dials up again for the evening.”

The actual vibe of the restaurant shifts depending on the time of day, too. In the morning and afternoon, the music is kept mellow and the lighting is soft, but as night approaches, the lights come up, the music builds, and the on-screen content gets more intense. 

In an era when other quick-service restaurants are rethinking their stores after overemphasizing a design centered on mobile orders and takeout, JKR decided to throw out any notion of what a fast-food restaurant should look like and invent a new one. When it comes to service, the flow of Muse was designed from the ground up.

Muse can accommodate a range of different diners—from those who want to pick up an order to groups who might want to have a traditional restaurant experience. “We’re not following any of the traditional QSR design codes,” Smith says. “We’re definitely not designing furniture-first because, obviously, there are multiple routes: You could be in a drive-through, you could go in person, you can order online, all of these things.”

When visitors first enter the Muse location, they place their order at a kiosk zone (the kiosks have been custom-designed for this new store), which is overseen by an employee who can answer any questions. From there, they can head to a pickup zone to grab their food, or opt to have their food delivered directly to their table. In the seating area, different setups—like sections for solo diners versus groups—are set apart with their own materials and colors. The zone receiving table service is called the “Crave Circle.” Smith says that as you move through the restaurant, from the kiosk to your seat, it should feel like a journey with multiple touchpoints.

The finished restaurant is meant to represent what Smith calls KFC’s transition from a QSR to a QXR—“quality experience retail.” KFC’s vision of the future fast-food restaurant turns grabbing a chicken sandwich into a literal production, complete with lights, screens, and mood-setting.

“Kentucky-fried comeback”

Obviously, not every KFC location can be a Muse. (The cost of two-story bucket screens for 34,000 stores alone boggles the mind.) But all of the details that went into Muse are intended to stand on their own—so that in the future, global franchisees will be able to pick and choose which elements could elevate their own locations. 

“We designed it holistically so it could work in any site, any place, any format,” Smith says. “They have stores that work in malls, the drive-throughs, the flagships, the tiny versions that appear in transport hubs, the delivery-only locations. Across the globe, there are lots of different formats and they’re also very, very different in terms of the square footage they take. So, we wanted a design that really focused on disrupting the category a bit.”

Other locations might choose to implement specific elements, such as Muse’s ever-changing lighting, audio, and content platform; its “Crave Circle” design; or its lobby kiosk and host system.

In the U.S., KFC is currently testing another concept location called “Open House,” which is similar to Muse but on a slightly smaller scale and tailored to an American audience. It features a mezzanine level, beverage bar, and merch station, as well as long, family-style tables for in-house service. Like KFC’s new brand and marketing, it’s a novel approach mixed with a blast from the past. 

“Dining, I think, has been overlooked in the last few years,” Tan-Gillespie says. “If you go to a restaurant, you expect to be waited on. One of the premises of Open House is that it’s a fast-casual experience without the fast-casual price tag—so we’ve got table service.”

Right now, Tan-Gillespie says that Open House is a testing ground: It will have to prove out, through its own sales results, which of its strategies are working and which need improvement. Eventually, though, the goal is to begin applying learnings from Open House (and globally from Muse) and rolling them out on a broader scale. 

“This is an invitation for all of America, whatever generation you belong to, to try KFC again,” Tan-Gillespie says. “We like to say it’s the ‘Kentucky-fried comeback’ made real in one restaurant.”

#10415. Going to the gym just got easier for residents at this Paris homeless shelter

At the nonprofit Emmaüs Solidarité homeless shelter in Paris, it’s not uncommon for new residents to have sleepless nights. But since the summer, residents have had a new way to cope with the stress of a restless evening: head to the courtyard and do a few circuits on their new outdoor gym. 

Through a partnership between the city, the nonprofit, and Omnigym, a Finnish fitness company, this new installation of outdoor exercise equipment seeks to not just help provide access to fitness gear, but build community and self-esteem. Consisting of four pieces of workout gear set up in the garden next to the shelter, this small public gym provides a fitness option for all, but is specifically located to assist those who don’t have the money or wouldn’t feel welcome in a private gym. It also addresses broader issues of physical inactivity and social isolation. 

“It’s a natural gathering place, and people from different socioeconomic backgrounds start to meet because they exercise,” says Omnigym’s CEO, Kirsti Laasio. “It’s a very egalitarian sort of situation.” 

The company, which has more than 800 locations across 18 countries, believes there’s both a business case and social value in building new gyms and workout spaces for disadvantaged communities. Laasio says that outdoor gyms can become part of city planning, since they can serve as modes of social contact that also help activate the streetscape and encourage preventive healthcare.  

The company’s People’s Gym initiative has been installing equipment for free at spaces such as prisons to demonstrate the impact this equipment can have on communities. Public sector clients, like park districts, make up most of Omnigym’s clientele. The Emmaüs Solidarité site includes four pieces of equipment: lat pulldown, squat, incline bench press, and low row.

The company had been working with the city of Paris, where it has two other outdoor gyms. When Laasio was told there was a shelter seeking to try something like this, Omnigym decided to participate in a trial, donate the equipment, and study the impact. The gear was installed in mid-July, and sensors on the equipment have found the gym gets consistent use.

One finding showed that people at the Emmaüs Solidarité location tend to work out more later in the day and overnight, as opposed to early mornings, suggesting an atypical routine compared with other gym users. The company uses this anonymous user data to make the case for more locations, and better tailor the array of machines at any particular site. 

A survey taken a month into the gym’s existence found that 80% of Emmaüs Solidarité residents reported improved physical fitness, while 90% reported that it helped them meet or talk to other people. 

Abdellah Bounkit, director of Emmaüs, said the conversation and socialization has been the “biggest victory.” Previously, those who wanted to exercise or play sports would walk to the nearby park and often play five-on-five soccer matches. Having a new, easily accessible space to work out and socialize was a big shift. It’s also reduced the number of fights that occasionally break out in the shelter. 

“When people arrive here, they are often isolated, so it can take a long time to build relationships,” says Bounkit. “Sports and exercise can really help.”

Health has been a persistent challenge for the unhoused community, which often gets shortchanged in terms of access to facilities. According to the recent UCSF Benioff Homelessness and Housing Initiative study of homelessness in California, nearly half of unhoused in the state rate their health as fair or poor; 60% report at least one chronic health condition such as asthma or hypertension. 

Numerous barriers—poor diet, irregular schedules, lack of funds, and a stable address—often stand in the way of starting and staying with a workout routine. Studies have found that being able to access equipment and facilities would have significant impacts with mental and cognitive health and improve sleep, areas of great concern for the unhoused community. In addition, shelter populations can shift and change rapidly, making the addition of regular activities and places for informal conversation valuable to a fluid population. 

There are growing examples of groups and organizations trying to provide more fitness opportunities for the community. Back on My Feet, which operates in 14 U.S. cities, encourages running, and in San Diego, Father Joe’s Village, a service provider, hosts a regular cycling ride for the unhoused. The Homeless World Cup supports soccer teams in 75 countries. 

While teams and clubs can provide a sense of purpose and belonging, even something as small as regular access to gym equipment can make a difference. 

“I love the fact I can meet new people,” says Wilfried, a two-year Emmaüs resident, via an interpreter. He favors the machine for biceps curls. “I used to never talk to certain people, now we meet at the equipment, and we do sports together,” he says.

“It gives me a lot of hope,” says Binate, another man staying at the shelter. ”When you train every day with the same people, the trust is growing.”

#10414. What this CEO learned about business from detasseling corn for $1.50 an hour

I don’t consciously think about my first job when I meet with leaders in the electronics industry to talk about trade policy. But my young days in an Illinois cornfield will always shape how I view, and do, work.

My hometown in western Illinois had maybe 10,000 people if Western Illinois University was in session. I grew up with a single mom and things were tight. If I wanted a little spending money as a kid, I had to earn it; a deep and early seeded lesson in the value of work.

Detasseling corn for $1.50 an hour over the summer was my first job at the ripe age of 11. My friends and I, sack lunch and water jug in hand, would hop on the school bus that waited for us near the town square at 5:30 every morning. The workday ahead was not easy, but it was a common Midwest ritual.

For 10 to 12 hours, we walked down the corn rows towering over our heads in the dead of summer heat, pulling off the feathery tassel part at the very top of each plant. (Farmers do this to prevent the plants from self-pollinating and encourage cross-pollination.) Despite the heat, you wore long sleeves because the rows were lined with dew-covered leaves sharp enough to slice right through exposed skin.

The Field Looks Endless

Like the objectives we try to accomplish for industry, a cornfield seems impossibly large when you’re looking down that long row. The idea of detasseling even one acre of corn just doesn’t compute in a helpful way. Achieving a technical or legislative goal for the electronics industry, and all the industries that depend upon it, can feel like a similarly Herculean task; seemingly endless agendas and implications that interconnect in very complex ways.

In the cornfield you have to stick to it. You tell yourself if you can go down and back 12 times, that gets you to lunch. You take it one tassel at a time. That’s the only way to finish that row—and field.

One Tassel at a Time

This mindset is just as effective when helping electronics manufacturers advance and embrace the future. A few years ago, there was a new wave of automation adoption at our member companies. Most plants run a mix of equipment brands, each with their own software and protocol; it’s great if you bought everything from one brand, but no one did. Manufacturers were saddled with time-consuming and costly middleware to make it all work together.

Our organization, the Global Electronics Association, creates and implements technical standards and solutions used across electronics manufacturers. We enthusiastically proposed a communications standard that would radically minimize time and cost for manufacturers; invest 20 hours, and you’re done. The Connected Factory Exchange, or CFX, standard was born. But our initial “Let’s detassel this whole cornfield!” ambition landed with a thud, especially with the suppliers that wanted to sell their own software solutions. Our initial meeting attracted one company.

Eventually, we got one OEM to fall in love with CFX and we had our first tassel in hand. Together, we worked our way down that row one industry player at a time. Two years later, 200 companies gathered in a room to put the finishing touches on a standard we built together.

CFX was practically free, manufacturers could connect all of their equipment, and the standard allowed equipment providers to layer in their innovations. By putting in the hard work we were eventually able to step back to see that we had accomplished our mission.

You Can Always Have Fun

A second lesson I learned from my adventures in the cornfields is that you can always have fun. I pretty much insist on it. That’s true of our work at the association, too. We focus on serious and highly complex issues. In government affairs, we understand that how effectively we advocate for one legislation or government action can have very real consequences. It can help determine whether some companies succeed or fail, whether natural resources are protected for future generations, or whether there are decent jobs for people to support themselves. I take our mission very seriously and I know my team takes it personally, too.

The cornfield was grueling and mind-numbing work. But you were close enough to your friends to hear each other. My friend Randy and I were big Steve Martin fans, and we would get down that row by trading lines from his routines, cracking each other up. 

I don’t look back and think that I became a great detasseler. But I do recognize that Randy and I memorized every comedy routine on Martin’s early comedy albums. We found a way to bring joy into our very mundane task, which made it bearable in the hot summer sun to finish that long row!

In a leadership meeting not long ago, someone mentioned they always have a song pop into their head that applies to the situation at hand. That notion really resonated with everyone, with people asking what song we were in—“I Wanna Be Sedated” by the Ramones came up—and cracking each other up with comments. For me, it was Steve Martin all over again.

Now it’s traditional. Someone will always pipe up, “So what song are we in?” It energizes us if we lose steam and provides a much-needed ice breaker in a tense moment. I truly value working alongside people who appreciate that while the work can be challenging, having fun along the way makes it all worthwhile.

Finishing the Row

Those two lessons from the cornfield never really left me. Break the impossible task down until it’s just the next tassel, the next conversation—and have fun while you’re doing it. I’ve built a career, and I hope meaningful progress for the electronics industry, on those two ideas an 11-year-old figured out over a summer of blistered hands and “Wild and Crazy Guy” bits. Turns out they go a long way to get almost anything done.

#10412. Customer obsession ‘done with a twinkle in your eye’

Hello and welcome to Modern CEO! I’m Stephanie Mehta, CEO and chief content officer of Mansueto Ventures. Each week this newsletter explores inclusive approaches to leadership drawn from conversations with executives and entrepreneurs, and from the pages of Inc. and Fast Company. If you received this newsletter from a friend, you can sign up to get it yourself every Monday morning.

When I was a tech reporter in the early and mid-2000s, T-Mobile was the runt of the telecom litter. The carrier was one of the smallest and arguably the scrappiest player, embracing its challenger status by marketing itself starting in 2013 as “the un-carrier” as it rolled out streamlined wireless service plans aimed at grabbing share from bigger rivals. 

Thanks in part to its 2020 merger with Sprint, T-Mobile is no longer the little guy. Last week, the company announced stronger-than-expected third-quarter earnings, raised profitability and cash flow guidance, and said it now expects to add approximately 1.0 million postpaid accounts in 2026. The company reported total revenue of $88.3 billion in 2025, up 8.5% annually, and ended last year with 142.4 million customers across all its services. T-Mobile’s current market capitalization is about $167 billion, behind Verizon at about $186 billion but ahead of AT&T at roughly $144 billion. 

Spunky at scale 

But Srini Gopalan, who became CEO of T-Mobile a year ago after an eight-month stint as chief operating officer, says the company has no intention of shedding its spunky mentality—or its quest for growth—despite its scale. In fact, he says, after five years of merger integration and network improvements, “this is the right time for us to reignite that thing which we will hold constant, which is our culture, which is ‘the un-carrier.’” 

For Gopalan, that means “disruption for the sake of the customer” and “putting our front line and our people first.” He’s expanding a program called Frontline Connect, in which T-Mobile leaders spend two days in a retail store or care center, previously for vice presidents and above, to include leaders at the director level and up. Executive stock-based compensation is contingent upon an exec’s personal commitment to the front line and completion of the program. He’s also announced a new, onetime performance-based $12,000 stock grant for every eligible employee, starting in January 2027. 

Spotting new growth opportunities

The company continues to roll out new pricing plans and offers. T-Mix, piloted in October and launching broadly this month, lets customers mix and match add-on services or “picks” such as streaming services or phone upgrades, and they can switch picks four times a year rather than annually. 

Gopalan aspires to growth beyond traditional wireless plans. He sees opportunities to sell more home broadband services and to push further into the business-to-business segment. He also sees promise in financial services, pointing to a credit card T-Mobile has issued with Capital One. 

The CEO’s shorthand for the culture he wants to nurture is “unmistakably T-Mobile.” He defines it this way: “To me, it is an obsession with the customer, an obsession with the front line, but done with a twinkle in your eye, done in a cheeky, irreverent manner, done in a surprising and memorable manner.” 

Connection issues 

Competitors say T-Mobile’s irreverence goes too far. Last year, for example, AT&T sued T-Mobile over its “Easy Switch” feature, an AI-powered tool that asked rivals’ customers to share their logins and passwords in order to create a personalized wireless offer. AT&T said T-Mobile used those customer credentials to scrape data, and it secured an injunction in a federal court barring parts of the original Easy Switch feature.

AT&T has since filed a new complaint alleging that T-Mobile’s “Switching Made Easy” plan, which promises to let customers change carriers in 15 minutes, is misleading. “T-Mo has been playing fast and loose with the truth for so long, they’ve forgotten what it is like to be the adult in the room,” an AT&T spokesperson says. “We’re going to keep calling it like we see it, because we think consumers and the courts know the difference between right and wrong.” 

T-Mobile maintains that its tool is aimed at helping consumers. “[AT&T’s] CEO is talking about how AI agents can help customers ‘get insight as to what works for them,’” a spokesperson said. “We thought so too, and that’s why we built Easy Switch. Customers deserve simple ways to understand their options and choose what’s best for them. We continue to welcome collaboration with AT&T and others to make that easier, even when customers choose to switch.” 

The challenger becomes the challenged

Meanwhile, investors are worried that the challenger may become the challenged. In early October, the day after Elon Musk’s Starlink agreed to acquire cellular spectrum licenses in a bid to build a national wireless network, shares of T-Mobile, Verizon, and AT&T all fell at least 8%. 

When I ask Gopalan if he’d rather compete against other telecom companies or Musk’s Starlink, he seems to shrug off the threat. “Bring them on. From our perspective, we love competition. We think it makes the industry better. It forces us to be better,” he says. On Starlink specifically, he says: “They may build a network. It’s hard to tell because in the last six months they’ve said a few different things. If they do, it’s going to take a long time.” 

He says T-Mobile, meanwhile, will continue to invest in its network and employees. “We’re going to love our front line more than we’ve ever loved them before because that is part of our secret sauce,” he says. “That combination of things is our moat. People launch whatever they want, but that’s [what] we’ll focus on.” 

Modern CEO of the year

Modern CEO is seeking nominations for the 2026 Modern CEO of the Year. If you or a CEO you know has had an exceptional year driving innovation, transforming a business, or futureproofing a company, please share their story via this form. The deadline for submissions is December 4, and the CEO or CEOs of the year will be unveiled on December 28.

  • Challenger brands can win by sweating the details  
  • The powerful branding trick Guinness, Corona, and Spotify share 
  • How upstart brands like Hoka and On can tackle giants like Nike

#10411. El Niño 2026 could combine with these separate phenomena to bring intense flooding sooner than you think

Throughout the last week of October, intense tides caused record-high water levels across the Southern California coast. Ocean water flooded the Redondo Beach boardwalk. Tide levels in San Diego topped 8 feet above baseline. Water level readings were among the highest in more than a century, according to the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration.

This year’s record-hot Super El Niño helped push those water levels to the extreme. But El Niño also combined with a natural phenomenon called king tides. More king tides later this year are expected to bring even more intense, damaging flooding to the coast. 

And working underneath both of these events is climate change, which has caused sea level rise and intense marine heat waves.

All these factors are compounding and overlapping, experts say, in a way that will likely make this a dangerous winter for those along the coast. Here’s why that’s happening.

What are king tides?

King tides aren’t a new phenomenon. “We’re used to these every year,” says Marc Alessi, a climate scientist with the Union of Concerned Scientists.  

The term refers to exceptionally high tides, which bring water levels above what an average high tide would be. They occur normally along the coastline, often causing flooding. 

King tides form because of the way the sun, moon, and Earth align, and when the moon is at its closest to the Earth. That certain alignment of the three heightens the gravitational pull on the ocean. As gravity pulls more on that water, it raises tidal heights. 

Because king tides are affected by that celestial alignment, they can be predicted. The next major round of king tides is forecast for just a few weeks from now: November 22 to 28, around Thanksgiving, and then again December 22 to 26, around Christmas.

King tides can occur on both the Atlantic and Pacific coasts. Florida also experienced king tides in October, which flooded multiple areas. In Fort Lauderdale, the city’s chief resilience officer, Nancy Gassman, told reporters that at the end of October, water levels rose about 6 inches higher than during previous king tides. 

How does El Niño affect king tides?

Though king tides can impact both U.S. seacoasts, El Niño doesn’t affect these coasts in the same way. 

For California, El Niño causes another phenomenon called Kelvin waves, because of the way El Niño changes wind patterns. 

During a non-El Niño year, winds blow from east to west over the Pacific Ocean, essentially “pushing water up against Indonesia,” Alessi says. But during an El Niño year, those winds weaken. Without the winds pushing the water, it “sloshes” back toward the other side of the Pacific. 

“Imagine a bathtub, and you’re sloshing a big volume of water to the other side,” Alessi says. “But in this case, it’s the Pacific Ocean.”

Kelvin waves are very long, but they move slowly. They also travel underneath the water, which means they don’t break on the shore. When a Kelvin wave hits South America, it splits, and one part travels up toward the California coast. This raises sea levels because it brings a “bulge” of warm water.

One of the main characteristics of an El Niño is above-average sea surface temperatures in the Pacific. But separate from El Niño, the Pacific has been dealing with an intense marine heat wave off the California coast. Kelvin waves then bring even more warm water to that area. 

And because warm water expands, that raises sea levels even more—6 to 10 inches on top of king tides, and on top of sea-level rise, Alessi says. This is another example of how climate change and El Niño are combining to turbocharge their impacts. 

Florida doesn’t experience Kelvin waves. But El Niños still impact the Southeast, primarily by bringing more rain throughout the winter. More precipitation means a higher risk of flooding across the state. 

The impact of climate change

All these climate events are happening on top of fossil fuel-driven climate change, which has already added about 8 inches to the average sea level height over the last 100 years. 

“Climate change is this accelerator,” Alessi says. “It’s not just the king tides. We have climate change fueling these marine heat waves, which are expanding the water off the coast. We have it influencing the El Niño and the Pacific Ocean, which would not have been as strong without the background warming already existing there. All of these factors combining are contributing to these record high tidal levels.”

This year’s El Niño is already the strongest on record—and it hasn’t even reached its peak yet. Forecasters expect El Niños to reach their maximum intensity from November to about January, before weakening into the spring. 

Whether climate change has amped up this El Niño’s strength, or to what extent, is something scientists are still researching. But they do know that El Niño amplifies the impacts of climate change. 

El Niño essentially lies on top of a world that is already warmer because of our burning of fossil fuels. That gives us a glimpse of what a future with even more intense climate change could look like. 

Or as António Guterres, the United Nations secretary-general, put it: “El Niño conditions will pour fuel on the fire of a warming world.”

We are “just getting into the thick” of this El Niño, Alessi says, and already, California has declared a state of emergency, intense rain in the Four Corners region of the Southwest has caused extreme flooding, and the Pacific had one of its busiest hurricane seasons. 

With the king tides expected in November and December, on top of El Niño, it could cause “record high tidal heights this winter,” Alessi adds. 

“This is a wakeup call,” he says. “The fact is, we live in a fundamentally different climate system than 100, even just 50 years ago. Every part of our climate system is impacted by fossil fuel-driven climate change in some way. We need to stop the burning of fossil fuels.”

#10409. He got a religious exemption from using AI at work. Maybe you can too

As the University of Kentucky began encouraging employees to use AI—from Copilot for meeting notes to tools like Claude and ChatGPT for everyday tasks—David Durman, a research administrator, worried about what he would lose by outsourcing his own thinking to machines.

So in August, Durman, who identifies as a humanist, took an unusual step. He sought a religious exemption from using artificial intelligence at work. With help from the American Humanist Association, a nonprofit that advocates for the rights of humanists and atheists, he submitted a letter to the university arguing that relying on AI to perform tasks that require human judgment, listening, and personal responsibility violated his deeply held beliefs. Doing so, he wrote, would diminish human dignity and amount to an abdication of “human moral agency.”

On September 25, the university granted his request, determining that the accommodation would not impose an undue burden on his department.

The decision offers a potentially powerful new avenue for workers who object to AI, allowing them to invoke federal religious-accommodation protections to opt out of a technology that employers are increasingly embracing. And as Durman’s case illustrates, workers don’t necessarily have to believe in God to qualify.

Why humanists are pushing back on AI

Critical thinking is a core tenet of humanism, a nontheistic belief system that emphasizes human dignity, reason, and our responsibility to care for one another. Last year, an international coalition of humanist groups adopted a declaration outlining principles for AI, including that the technology should never replace human judgment and should benefit society broadly rather than enrich a privileged few.

There’s no single humanist viewpoint about exactly how to use AI, or whether it should be used at all. “You can be a humanist and believe different things about AI,” says Fish Stark, executive director of the American Humanist Association, who left the tech industry himself because of his own concerns about the risks of AI. What unites humanists, he says, is a belief that the technology should advance human flourishing, not undermine it. “We think the thing that makes people special isn’t an immortal soul; it’s conscience and the ability to think for ourselves,” Stark says.

In a press release, Durman also cited his concern about the broader impacts of AI, from the potential for job loss to environmental damage—also things that obviously conflict with his values as a humanist.

Under Title VII of the Civil Rights Act, employers generally must accommodate employees’ sincerely held religious beliefs, including certain nontheistic beliefs, unless doing so would impose an undue hardship on the business. While accommodations for religious observances, such as not working on the Sabbath, are well established, requests to avoid using AI are of course a much newer development.

Durman isn’t the only employee who has successfully taken this approach. Earlier this year, a software engineer who is a Unitarian Universalist also won an exemption from using AI at work, citing her concerns with the environmental impact of AI use. After Pope Leo XIV wrote about the potential risks of AI—including the potential for loss of human judgment and independent thinking—some Catholics may also begin seeking exemptions.

A new way to opt out

The American Humanist Association is now encouraging others to follow Durman’s example. On its website, the organization has posted an open invitation offering legal assistance to humanists interested in seeking workplace AI exemptions. “In a world where people need and don’t have more control over the development of AI, one of the few tools that we have is the ability to withdraw our consent and our participation,” Stark says.

You don’t need to have officially called yourself a humanist in the past to be able to potentially take this route. “Humanism isn’t necessarily about showing up to a church and practicing; instead it’s about what you believe and what you do in your life,” Stark says. “So we think probably about 10%-15% of American adults are humanists and many of them don’t use the term. To attain a religious accommodation, the standard is that you have to show that you have a sincere and deeply held belief in that religious tradition. Whether you’ve called yourself a humanist for a long time [or not], if you’ve believed what humanists believe and you believe it deeply, then that meets the standard.”

In religious accommodation cases, employees need to prove that their objection is religious and not political or coming from some other motivation, says Dallan Flake, a professor at Gonzaga University who studies employment law. If the objection is religious, employers have to offer the accommodation as long as it doesn’t cause the company or organization undue hardship. In the past, it was fairly easy for employers to claim that any hardship was too much, but that changed with a 2023 Supreme Court case. “It’s a much higher burden,” says Flake. Now, if an employer wanted to prove that an employee avoiding AI would cause them hardship, they’d have to show that the change caused substantial increased cost or loss of productivity.

For most jobs, that’s probably difficult to prove—especially since it hasn’t been long that AI was in widespread use, and people have been able to do their work without it. “I do think in the future, as we become more dependent on AI and it becomes more integrated into our job duties and as employers expect more fluency in using AI, at that point they might have a stronger claim that, hey, if we exempt this employee from using AI, that’s really going to harm us,” Flake says.

From a humanist perspective, beyond the choices that people make about their own work, Stark argues that those who are concerned about AI should be pushing for collective action. “I think that people should be coming together to demand better AI governance from our government,” he says. “Ultimately AI has the potential to do tremendous good or tremendous harm to humanity.”

Its development, he argues, shouldn’t be controlled by “a small cabal of billionaires.” Instead, it should be subject to democratic oversight.
