#10408. This election forecast tool updates every 10 minutes. Here’s what it’s predicting about Democrats’ chances

With control of the U.S. Senate on the line, a new tool is tracking the battle in near real time.

Throughout the day, the platform pulls data from the prediction markets Kalshi and Polymarket, and then uses statistical models to simulate a million versions of the midterm elections, with outcomes for each of the 35 races in play.

“Every 10 minutes, I’m running a gigantic simulation,” says Thomas Miller, the faculty director of the master’s in data science program at Northwestern University, who created the approach.

He calls it “NowCasting.” Prediction markets change very quickly, and so do the forecasts. “There’s value in that they’re very responsive to the news,” he says.

Some research suggests that prediction markets can be more accurate than polls. Still, the price that traders are betting on a candidate is not the same as a probability—if it costs 60 cents to bet that a Republican candidate will win, that’s not the same as a 60% chance of winning. For one thing, a large bet from a single trader could theoretically distort the price.

Platform fees and other pricing dynamics also affect the numbers. The prices of bets on all candidates in an election can add up to more than $1, even though their probabilities of winning should add up to 100%.

“I’m a data scientist . . . not a pollster”

Miller’s approach adjusts the prices and combines them with statistical modeling used in finance and econometrics. “It’s data driven—I’m a data scientist, not a politician, not a pollster,” he says. “I’m using the data, and using models that are trusted and that have been used for many years.”

Miller has been working with data from betting markets since 2020, when he correctly forecasted that Joe Biden would win using data from a market called PredictIt.

In January 2021, in the runoff election for Senate seats in Georgia, he correctly predicted that Jon Ossoff and Raphael Warnock would win. In 2024, when some other forecasters predicted that Kamala Harris would win the presidential election, he predicted that Donald Trump would win.

What’s the outlook for Tuesday?

At the point that we spoke on Friday, the latest model suggested that Republicans only had a 17.5% chance of keeping Senate control, down a couple of percentage points from earlier the same day, and a steep drop from the day before.

On Sunday, after surprise news that Michigan’s Democratic congresswoman Haley Stevens had endorsed the Republican candidate for Senate, Mike Rogers, over Abdul El-Sayed—Rogers’s chances ticked up slightly on the platform, but only temporarily, and nowhere near enough for a win.

By early Monday morning, with Election Day (Tuesday, November 3) just one day away, the platform predicted that Democrats had a roughly 90% chance of gaining control of the Senate.

#10406. Tech earnings season has been brutal. Can Apple turn things around today?

Autumn’s tech earnings season hasn’t been a good one for investors so far. A few outliers aside (looking at you, Microsoft), Wall Street has reacted pretty negatively to the financial results from major tech players in the past week.

One big thing that’s upsetting investors is the negative free cash flow from giants like Google and Amazon, driven largely by capital expenditures on AI data centers. The impact of AI has also driven down the stock prices of Reddit and Intel after they announced earnings. 

And today, it’s Apple’s turn to report its latest quarterly results. Here’s what to keep an eye out for.

Apple reports Q4 earnings after the closing bell

Today, the most valuable company in the world announces its latest quarterly results. iPhone maker Apple Inc. (Nasdaq: AAPL) will reveal its fourth-quarter financials after the closing bell. 

Apple’s Q4, which ended on September 30, also closes out its fiscal year. But it’s notable for another reason this year, too: it’s the first quarter in which Apple’s new CEO, John Ternus, ran the show—at least partly.

Ternus replaced longtime CEO Tim Cook at the beginning of September.

Historically, Apple’s Q4 is its most important—at least when it comes to product launches. It’s when Apple traditionally releases its new iPhones (albeit just a few weeks before the quarter ends).

But this year was a little different. Instead of launching the entire iPhone 18 family lineup during the quarter, Apple only announced the iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone 18 Pro Max. It’s saving its updated iPhone 18 and iPhone Air 2 for Q3 next year.

What to expect—and not to—from Apple’s Q4 earnings

Investors will want to know how much the new, staggered iPhone release schedule impacted iPhone sales for the quarter, so any word from Ternus or chief financial officer Kevan Parekh on September comparables to last year will likely be closely watched.

During Q4, the company also announced its first foldable, the iPhone Duo. However, that device did not go on sale until October, after the quarter ended.

That means the Duo had no impact on the company’s Q4 revenue. Still, given it’s Apple’s most radical iPhone redesign ever, investors will be looking for any hints from Apple’s leadership on how early sales of its foldable are going.

Apple will also likely give an update about the impact AI is having on its business. Unlike its Big Tech contemporaries, Apple has (it now appears, wisely) refrained from heavily investing in AI data centers, which is what has caused giants like Google and Amazon to post those negative free cash flows that have freaked investors out.

But AI-related costs are affecting the company—mainly through higher memory prices—and analysts will want an update on how those higher component costs are likely to affect Apple going forward.

But perhaps most importantly, investors will be keen to hear Apple’s guidance for its current Q1 2027. This is the quarter that will include Apple’s holiday sales—typically the biggest quarter of the year for Apple. Strong guidance could boost investor confidence and send the stock up after hours. Weaker guidance could have the opposite effect.

What Wall Street expects from Apple’s Q4

According to Bloomberg analyst consensus estimates (via Yahoo Finance), Wall Street expects the following Q4 results:

  • Total revenue: $113.46 billion
  • EPS: $2.00
  • iPhone revenue: $56.48 billion
  • Services revenue: $31.58 billion
  • Mac revenue: $9.69 billion
  • Wearables revenue: $8.99 billion
  • iPad revenue: $6.83 billion

To put those two topline numbers into context, last year Apple reported $102.46 billion in total revenue for its Q4 2025 and an EPS of $1.85 for the same quarter.

Apple has been the best-performing Magnificent 7 stock this entire year and is largely seen as a safe haven from the AI trade thanks to the company’s relatively conservative approach to AI spending compared to its contemporaries.

For the year, AAPL stock is up nearly 27% as of Friday’s close. That’s compared to less than 10% for Nvidia, 8% for Microsoft, and just over 4% for Amazon. Alphabet (Google’s owner), Meta, and Tesla are all underwater around 11%, 11%, and 6.8% as of Friday’s close.

Recently, Apple became the most valuable company in the world, leapfrogging Nvidia and becoming only the second company ever to close with a market cap above $5 trillion.

Apple’s financial call is expected at 2 p.m. PT / 5 p.m. ET today.

#10405. How AI data centers could find a regulation-free haven at the end of the world

Data centers have become one of the most polarizing issues in the world. Argentina’s president wants to lure as many of them as he can get.

As residents and policymakers in the United States and Europe fight the facilities that power artificial intelligence, President Javier Milei is pitching Argentina as a haven, promising a hands-off approach to draw foreign investment his crisis-prone country needs.

Like his ally, U.S. President Donald Trump, Milei frequently posts AI-generated images on social media. He has proposed creating “nonhuman corporations” run entirely by AI agents.

“We are not going to regulate artificial intelligence,” Milei told the U.S. Chamber of Commerce in Washington last month. “We are going to leave it free.”

Visitor logs obtained by The Associated Press show a steady stream of tech figures meeting with Milei and his aides in recent months, from venture capitalist Peter Thiel to representatives of OpenAI, Amazon, Google, Oracle and data center companies. Foreign Ministry records show that data center developer Crusoe, which helped build OpenAI’s flagship campus in Texas, sought talks this year with Argentine energy officials about U.S. government-backed investments.

Milei’s hopes rest as much on geography as on ideology. Patagonia offers vast, open plains where noisy server farms can be built far from population centers, a chilly climate to cool the machines, powerful winds to generate electricity and one of the world’s largest shale deposits in Vaca Muerta to fuel power plants.

“In densely populated parts of the U.S. and Europe, there’s a lot of fighting over access to the grid and who should get the electricity,” said Marta Zlotkowska, head of international development at Green Capital, a Polish renewable energy company that plans to build a $3 billion data center in Patagonia to lease to big tech firms for AI computing, though it has yet to secure a customer.

“We compared potential locations for the project around the world and Argentina — well, Patagonia — came out on top in every category,” she said.

Patagonia’s AI promise

For Argentina’s energy industry, already booming as the Iran war hikes oil prices, data centers represent a tantalizing new market. Pampa Energía, one of the country’s biggest power companies, has announced plans to supply immense amounts of gas-fired power to a data center near Vaca Muerta and is seeking developers.

“Being at the end of the world used to be a disadvantage,” said Rubén Etcheverry, a top official in Neuquén, the Patagonian province home to Vaca Muerta. “But today, faced with energy shortages and rising costs, investors are looking at us.”

To win over companies wary of Argentina’s chronic economic instability, Milei is pushing an incentive scheme that would slash the corporate income tax rate to 15% from 25% for projects of at least $1 billion in AI and other emerging industries.
The measure is awaiting Senate approval.

The payoff for Argentines remains unclear. Data centers employ relatively few people. Milei’s proposed incentives don’t require companies to reserve computing capacity for local consumption or to disclose their energy and water use.

“We’re not talking about data centers designed to strengthen Argentine technological sovereignty,” said Alan Rocha Varsanyi, a researcher at Observatorio Petrolero Sur, an Argentine environmental group. “The computing capacity will be exported wherever OpenAI or the other companies need it.”

Blueprints, not yet bulldozers

For all Milei’s hype, Argentina lags behind in the race to become a regional data center hub, as its neighbors tout cheap land, renewable power and expanding fiber-optic connections to U.S. tech giants.

Milei insists his calls for unfettered AI give Argentina an edge at a time when the European Union and a growing number of U.S. states have adopted new regulations. Critics warn he is pushing to deploy AI in Argentina without safeguards against risks ranging from mass surveillance to cyberattacks.

“Without rules, Argentina will become a laboratory — like when companies go to developing countries to test strange drugs they can’t test in major developed countries,” said Nicolás Wolovick, a computer science professor at the National University of Córdoba.

Milei may hear similar concerns next week when Pope Leo XIV, who has warned of AI’s threats to humanity, visits Argentina on his Latin American tour.

Even some in Milei’s own government acknowledge that his anti-regulation stance complicates efforts to build a clear legal framework, with one senior official not authorized to brief media saying companies are waiting for Argentina to settle rules on training AI with copyrighted works and sending personal data abroad.

Some investors are holding back until next year’s potentially tight presidential election, worried that if Milei loses, the next government could unravel his free-market reforms.

“One of our major challenges is getting foreign investors to understand that Argentina remains a friendly place for investment under different political parties,” said Andrés Arbeletche, cabinet coordination secretary for Chubut, a Patagonian province courting several AI mega-projects.

Farms of servers and sheep

In remote ranching outposts across the treeless plateau of Chubut province, life revolves around tending flocks on steppes and shearing sheep in sheds. With the wool industry in decline after decades of drought, overgrazing and soaring production costs, some ranchers are betting on a different kind of shed — filled not with fleece but with blinking racks of servers.

“Sheep farmers are looking very hard at what alternatives can be found to bring in more income,” said Francisco Sáenz, an agricultural engineer and fourth-generation sheep farmer whose family is among 14 landowners who have leased a combined 130,000 hectares (about 321,000 acres) to Green Capital for its planned data center. The ranchers expect to keep grazing sheep alongside their colossal future neighbor.

Construction isn’t expected to begin until around 2028. If operated continuously at its projected peak demand, the finished campus would use about 2.7 times as much electricity in a year as the entire city of Buenos Aires consumed in 2025, according to an AP calculation.

Green Capital promises to generate all its own electricity from wind turbines, solar farms and gas-fired generators rather than strain Argentina’s congested grid. Provincial officials are counting on the project to deliver billions of dollars in investment, highly skilled jobs and a market for surplus wind power.

“For us, it is all a benefit, and we give nothing up,” Arbeletche said.

But some residents of Trelew, a dust-blown city near the proposed site, say they have no way to judge those assurances. Environmental studies are still underway, permits are pending and the company hasn’t specified how much water the project would need or how much of its power would come from gas.

“There’s just so little information,” said Cristina Gibón, 46, who fears the environmental toll of data centers. “Maybe we’ll have water until we’re old. But afterward, our children will still be here, and they won’t have those resources.”

Worries like hers are feeding a wider backlash against Milei’s courtship of Big Tech. In Trelew, a small group of residents has rallied online and in the main square under the banner “Trelew Free of Data Centers.” In Congress, where lawmakers recently accused Milei of selling off the country at a hearing on billionaire Peter Thiel’s interests in Argentina, the opposition has forced the president to gut a bill easing restrictions on foreign land ownership and has stalled his plan to let AI agents control companies.

Back on his family’s farm, Sáenz sees a familiar Argentine reflex.

“Whenever a foreign company wants to make a major investment in Argentina, there’s a lot of criticism. Many people’s first reaction is to say, ‘No, they’re terrible people coming from the outside to take our resources,'” he said. “I’m not saying big companies are the best thing ever. I’m just saying that when I look at other countries, I see stable economies.”

—Isabel Debre, Associated Press

#10402. How Fanatics and the NHL used cryptic marketing to hype 32 new special-edition jerseys

Fanatics’ newest collaboration with the NHL is a collection of 32 custom jerseys that use font, color, and symbolism to pay homage to the history of each team’s home city.

The collection, called Hometown Remix, is a leaguewide special edition uniform, apparel, and headwear program designed in-house by the global sports platform Fanatics. It includes a bespoke jersey for each NHL team that will be worn at multiple points throughout the season, as well as a matching line of fan gear.

Every jersey was made through a series of workshops between Fanatics and the team to land on a look that represents each club’s local culture. The first jerseys will hit the ice when the Florida Panthers, Chicago Blackhawks, and San Jose Sharks play on November 2, and the fan apparel is available now across the Fanatics network of sites and in team stores.

Since becoming the NHL’s on-ice uniform partner in 2024, Fanatics has designed several special edition uniforms for marquee events like the 4 Nations Face-Off and NHL Winter Classic. With Hometown Remix, though, the brand is making its first foray into a leaguewide jersey program—and its strategy demonstrates how Fanatics and the NHL are using design to drum up anticipation throughout the season.

How leaguewide custom jerseys took off

For devoted NHL fans, the concept of a leaguewide jersey program isn’t new. The league first tried something similar back in 2020, when it launched a jersey line called Reverse Retro in partnership with its then-official outfitter, Adidas. The program created uniforms for each club inspired by a specific era in its history, but with a modern spin. It was so successful that, in 2022, Adidas brought the concept back—this time, to a surge of fan theories and mock-ups.

At the time, Dan Near, senior director at Adidas hockey, told ESPN, “We’re excited about the speculation. I think if you look back at the first time we launched in 2020, it came out of nowhere. Nobody knew what it was.” The jersey launch created its own cult following of NHL devotees, and, clearly, the NHL immediately recognized it as an ongoing marketing opportunity. 

“When we partnered up with the NHL, there was always a conversation around, ‘Is there going to be a leaguewide initiative? Is that something that Fanatics can bring to the table?’” says Dom Fillion, vice president of creative design and innovation at Fanatics. “We certainly heard about it from fans on socials and in conversation as well.”

In 2024, Fillion’s team sent out a series of surveys to fans to gauge their interest in a leaguewide program, and got a resounding “yes.” Once Fanatics had committed to taking on the challenge, the next hurdle was deciding on a central theme. It had to be scalable across all 32 teams while also allowing each club to take ownership of its own design. 

“We didn’t necessarily want to create a platform that was only looking backward and telling old stories, but really allowing this platform to create something new,” Fillion explains. “This is how Hometown Remix came to life—it connects the team, the city, and the fans together and gives opportunities for a team to tell stories that they haven’t had the opportunity to do so at that scale.”

Fans follow the breadcrumbs

On social media, teams have spent the weeks leading up to the launch playing into speculation by dropping hints to their fandoms. Every team posted their own version of a Hometown Remix-themed riddle for fans to solve. The Florida Panthers posted a color swatch from their jersey; the Seattle Kraken made a teaser video with all of the design details obscured; and the Edmonton Oilers dropped a moody trailer ahead of the launch. 

The Boston Bruins have taken the program one step further and invented an entire fake college, Causeway University, to play off of their jersey’s collegiate theme. Andrea Mazzarelli, vice president of marketing for the Bruins, says the campaign includes Causeway-themed posts on socials, an elaborate fake website, and, alongside the jersey drop, a “college orientation” event for fans.

“We didn’t connect [Causeway University] overtly to Hometown Remix with our launch, but there are some people who are already figuring it out based on rumors going around,” Mazzarelli says. “That’s what’s made it so fun and so cryptic, is that people are waiting for more.”

Across the league, the breadcrumbs are having their intended effect. Fans have created YouTube videos dissecting the roll-out and made Reels comparing the content across teams. By turning the launch into a cat-and-mouse game with fans, Fanatics and the NHL have manufactured the exact kind of fan dialogue that they need to turn Hometown Remix into a hit as soon as it debuts.

How Fanatics designed 32 city-inspired jerseys

To create each jersey, Fanatics went back and forth with all 32 teams to discuss color schemes, fonts, team identity, the storytelling behind the design, and potential easter eggs to include for fans. Making every design successful, Fillion says, required identifying every club’s “secret sauce”—or the special points of connection that they’ve built with their individual fandoms. 

“We went in with individual lanes on, ‘Hey, this could be concept one, two, and three,’ allowing different creative perspectives on how that hyperlocal narrative could come to life and the marks and the colors,” Fillion says. From there, he adds, each team either selected a preferred design and offered additional input, or suggested an entirely new direction. “They know their fans best. They work with these fans every single day at a granular level,” he says.

With the Bruins, for example, Fillion’s designers brought the team concepts based on the city’s nautical culture and the Boston Tea Party. Ultimately, though, the team opted for a look to honor its collegiate history. The brown and yellow uniform nods to the Bruins’ first season in 1924, marking the first time that the team has worn a brown kit in nearly 100 years. Other details, like the blocky serif wordmark, shield on the shoulder patch, split-color design, and classic striping on the sleeve mimic traditional campus wear. As an added easter egg for locals, a brick pattern on the hem resembles Boston’s Freedom Trail, a 2.5-mile historic walking trail in the city.

A similar level of detail appears across the full collection. Filigree detailing on the Nashville Predators’ uniform echoes the city’s country stagewear; the Los Angeles Kings’ look uses a Hollywood-inspired serif font; and the St. Louis Blues get a debossed wave pattern on the sleeves to evoke the Mississippi River. 

As the jerseys start appearing on ice, Fillion says, an “entire suite of marketing content” will accompany them to give viewers a behind-the-scenes look at the design and to point out some of the more subtle, city-specific details. The whole launch is designed to make fans feel like they’re part of something exclusive. 

“This is, to me, a great example of the power and the influence of the Fanatics brand,” Fillion says.

#10401. Midterm elections 2026: AI data centers could decide races in these states

Americans are flocking to the polls in record numbers ahead of nationwide midterm elections on November 3.

More than 54 million voters have already cast their ballots—voting early in-person and by mail. And as of late October, the early voter turnout numbers were higher than in both 2018 and 2022 at the same point in the election cycle—with predictions saying that early voting could make up two-thirds of all ballots cast.

Now, with just 1 day until the election, AI data centers have emerged as one of the hot topics in the midterms. As Brad Carson, head of Public First Action, told Fast Company, “There’s hardly a race in the country right now where data centers aren’t playing a role in some form.”

It is a key issue in both Ohio’s governor race and the Senate races in the Buckeye state, as well as in neighboring Michigan.

Ohio is one of 13 U.S. states that currently have AI data center-related measures on the ballot in multiple elections through the end of 2026. Some 84% of those 49 measures will be decided in the November midterms. Ohio has the most measures (21), trailed by Illinois (7), Maine (4), Wisconsin (3), Kansas (3), California (2), Arkansas (2), New Hampshire (2), Florida (1), Michigan (1), Nevada (1), New Jersey (1), and North Dakota (1).

Adding to that: In another 20 gubernatorial races (out of a total of 36), candidates are calling for a moratorium on AI data centers, according to Multistate, a policy tracking firm.

To understand why, you need only look at the latest polling: A staggering two-thirds of Americans oppose building new AI data centers, and 57% say artificial intelligence is bad for society—with one-third of those calling it “very” bad, according to The Washington Post.

And it’s not just Democrats that are running on anti-AI platforms. A number of Republican candidates are also trying to put some distance between themselves and President Donald Trump’s pro-AI policies, the Post reported.

That’s likely in part because 67% of Americans, both Republicans and Democrats, do not think the government is doing enough to regulate AI, according to the latest Washington Post-ABC News-Ipsos poll.

#10398. Passwordless Authentication and Identity Theft: Can Passkeys Make the Web Safer?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most identity theft online starts with a password. Someone reuses one, a site gets breached, a fake login page collects a few more, and suddenly a stranger is inside an email account resetting everything else.

Passkeys are the industry’s attempt to remove that weak link. Apple, Google, and Microsoft all support them now, and plenty of big sites already offer them at sign-in. But do they actually make identity theft harder, or do they just move the problem somewhere else?

The honest answer is a bit of both. This article looks at how passkeys work under the hood, which attacks they shut down, which ones they don’t touch, and what you need to get right if you’re adding them to your own site.

Passwords have one basic flaw. They’re a shared secret. You know it, the server knows it (or a hash of it), and anyone who gets a copy can use it from anywhere.

That one flaw feeds most of the account takeover attacks you hear about:

Two-factor authentication helps, but not as much as people think. SMS codes can be intercepted or stolen through SIM swaps. One-time codes from an app can still be phished, because a fake site can simply ask for the code and pass it along in real time.

A passkey is a pair of cryptographic keys made for one specific website. It’s built on the WebAuthn standard from the W3C and the FIDO Alliance.

Here’s the short version of what happens:

Nothing secret ever crosses the network, and nothing secret is stored on the server. That small change has big consequences.

This is the biggest win. Every passkey is tied to the domain it was created for. Your browser won’t use a passkey for example.com on examp1e-login.com, no matter how real the page looks. That protection only holds if the attacker can’t fall back to a password or a weak recovery path, which is why the rollout advice later in this article matters.

With a password, the user is the one who has to spot the fake. With a passkey, the browser checks the domain for them. People get tired and click bad links. The browser doesn’t.

Each passkey is unique to one site, so there’s nothing to reuse. An attacker can’t take a passkey from one breach and try it somewhere else, because there’s no shared value to try.

If an attacker dumps a site’s database, they get public keys. A public key can check a signature, but it can’t make one. So the stolen data can’t be used to sign in as anyone.

This doesn’t make a breach harmless (more on that below), but it does remove the part that usually leads straight to account takeovers.

This is where a lot of articles get too optimistic. Passkeys protect the login step. Identity theft is a much bigger problem than that.

Every site needs a way back in for people who lose their phone. If that recovery path is an SMS code, an email link, or a support agent who can be talked into resetting things, attackers will go after that instead.

A site with passkeys and a weak recovery flow is only as strong as the recovery flow. This is the part developers most often underestimate. The OWASP Forgot Password Cheat Sheet is a good checklist for hardening it.

A breach that exposes names, addresses, birth dates, or government ID numbers is still a breach. Criminals don’t need to log in to your accounts to open new ones in your name. They can apply for credit, file fake tax returns, or run scams with that data alone.

Passkeys do nothing about this kind of identity theft. It’s why credit monitoring, freezes, and alert services are still worth knowing about even when your logins are locked down. If you want a broader look at how those tools compare, these theft protection insights from Cybernews cover what different services actually monitor.

Someone can still call a person, pretend to be their bank, and talk them into approving something. Passkeys make that harder because there’s no code to read out loud, but scammers adapt. User education still matters.

The good news is that the browser side is fairly small. Most of the real work happens on the server. The MDN guide to the Web Authentication API covers every option in detail, and the Passkeys Developer Resources site is a good source for implementation guidance.

Here’s a stripped-down registration call. In a real app, the challenge and user ID come from your server, never from the browser.

A couple of details worth noticing. The rp.id is what ties the passkey to your domain, so set it to the domain users actually sign in on. And user.id should be a random value rather than an email, since it’s stored on the user’s device.

Passkeys feel best when they show up right in the username field’s autofill menu. This is called conditional UI, and it lets you add passkeys without changing your login form much.

Users who have a passkey see it in the dropdown. Users who don’t can keep typing a password like before. That makes a gradual rollout much easier.

Verifying WebAuthn responses means checking the challenge, the origin, the relying party ID, and the signature. It’s very easy to get one of those subtly wrong. Use a well-maintained library instead of writing it yourself. SimpleWebAuthn is a popular choice for Node.js, and most major languages have solid options.

One thing that trips people up: signature counters. Older hardware keys increment a counter on every use, and some guides tell you to reject sign-ins if it goes backward. Synced passkeys often just report zero, so a strict counter check can lock out real users.

A few practical tips from teams that have already done this:

There’s also the question of moving passkeys between platforms. Early on, passkeys stuck to one ecosystem, which annoyed a lot of people. Password managers like 1Password and Bitwarden now store and sync them across platforms, and the FIDO Alliance has published specifications for moving passkeys and other credentials between providers, the Credential Exchange Format and its companion protocol. It’s getting better, but it’s worth telling users where their passkeys live.

Yes, and by a lot, for one specific part of the problem.

Passkeys close off phishing, credential stuffing, and password database leaks, which are the most common ways criminals take over accounts. That alone makes them one of the most meaningful security upgrades the web has seen in years.

But identity theft is bigger than logins. Weak recovery flows, stolen sessions, and leaked personal data all still lead to real harm, and passkeys don’t fix any of them on their own.

If you’re building for the web, treat passkeys as a strong foundation, not the whole house. Add them, make recovery just as solid, keep your sessions protected, and collect as little personal data as you can. That combination does far more for your users than any single feature.

#10392. Vivo’s X500 series launches globally with MediaTek chips and insane cameras

Following its initial debut in China, Vivo is officially taking its flagship lineup global. The X500 series brings three new models to the international stage, including the Vivo X500, X500 Pro, and the range-topping X500 Pro Max.

What’s interesting is that the Chinese OEM has just jumped straight past the X400 to the X500, for what reason, we’re not entirely sure. This is a direct follow-on from the X300 lineup, which was released earlier this year and has been such a promising lineup.

Under the hood, both the X500 Pro and Pro Max are powerhouses, becoming the first smartphones to feature MediaTek’s new 2nm Dimensity 9600 Pro chipset. The standard X500 is powered by the MediaTek Dimensity 9600M. Display-wise, both Pro models sport 2K 144Hz BOE Q11 LTPO AMOLED displays—6.36-inch on the Pro and 6.85-inch on the Pro Max—reaching up to 4,500 nits peak brightness. The vanilla X500 sits in the middle with a 6.59-inch 144Hz AMOLED screen.

Battery capacities have taken a massive leap forward across the board thanks to high-density silicon-carbon technology: an 8,000 mAh cell inside the Pro Max, 6,510 mAh in the Pro, and 7,500 mAh in the standard X500. All three models support 90W wired FlashCharge and 40W wireless charging, carry dual IP68 & IP69 water and dust resistance, and ship running OriginOS 7 based on Android 17.

But as expected, the cameras are the (pun intended…) main focus here.

Co-engineered with Zeiss, the X500 Pro and Pro Max center around a custom 50MP 24mm-equivalent main sensor (Sony LYTIA L910, 1/1.28-inch) featuring an f/1.57 aperture, Zeiss T* coating, and 17 EV of dynamic range. The Pro Max features a “gimbal-grade” 3-degree OIS setup, while the Pro steps back slightly to 1.5 degrees.

For zoom, the X500 Pro Max packs an 85mm-equivalent 200MP periscope telephoto (Samsung ISOCELL HP0, 1/1.4-inch) backed by CIPA 7.0 pro-grade stabilization. The X500 Pro uses a custom 85mm-equivalent 64MP telephoto (Sony LYTIA 610, 1/2.0-inch) with the same stabilization rating. Both Pro variants share a 15mm-equivalent 50MP ISOCELL JN5 ultrawide lens and support an optional 400mm-equivalent ZEISS Telephoto Extender Gen 2 Ultra add-on lens. The company has banked on Pro photography kits in recent years, and this is yet another device shipping with an option.

Meanwhile, the base vivo X500 features a 23mm 50MP LYTIA 828 main camera, a 72mm 64MP telephoto, and a 50MP ISOCELL JN1 ultrawide shooter.

Video performance gets a serious upgrade, too. The X500 Pro and Pro Max offer 4K 240fps cinematic slow motion, 10-bit video capture with 422 encoding, and ACES (Academy Color Encoding System) support, while the Pro Max tops out with 8K 30fps recording and 4K 120fps Pro AF Tracking Video.

As for aesthetics, the standard Vivo X500 comes in White, Gray, Black, and Pink. The X500 Pro and X500 Pro Max are offered in White, Gray, Black, and a standout Sunset Orange finish. The White, Gray, and Orange finishes feature a subtle micro-dot shimmer, while the Black option features a pyramid-like light-shifting texture.

#10389. The Privacy Risks of Browser Extensions: What Developers Should Know

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The extension a developer installed three years ago can probably read everything they do in the browser today.

General online security advice from VPNpro and similar guides cover the network side of staying safe, but they cannot see what an extension does inside the browser. That layer is more the developer's responsibility. The risk is complicated, but the fix is simple: request fewer permissions, narrower host access, and less data, and review every update. Here is a walkthrough of each.

Think about what a developer has open on a normal day: a cloud console, a staging dashboard, a private repo, maybe webmail. One over-permissioned extension sees all of it.

The most common mistake is asking for broad access early and planning to trim it later. Later rarely arrives.

A manifest like this should raise eyebrows:

A developer who cannot justify a permission in a short sentence likely shouldn't include the permission in the build.

Host permissions deserve their own attention because they define how far the extension can reach. A few habits make a real difference:

This also helps adoption. People hesitate when an extension asks to read every site they visit. A narrow request looks, and is, more trustworthy.

Analytics and error logging are where good intentions leak. A crash report that includes the full page URL may contain a session token in the query string. A “usage event” may capture page titles from internal tools.

The most secure approach is to collect only the data a feature needs and to process the data locally if possible. When data has to be sent, it should be stripped down to the absolute minimum. Avoid sending query strings, and truncate URLs to the domain name where possible. Never send page content if it is not absolutely necessary.

Remote code is another trap. Manifest V3 restricts remotely hosted code for a good reason. A script fetched at runtime can modify its behavior after the fact, and one can’t audit what it will do in the future. Bundle everything and the code that is reviewed will be identical to the code that is run.

A good extension today can become a risky one next year. Two patterns keep repeating.

Ownership changes. Popular extensions get sold, and the new owner ships an update with tracking or worse. The Great Suspender, a widely used tab tool, ended up flagged as malware after a change of hands.

Compromised publisher accounts. If an attacker phishes the developer account, they can push a malicious update to every existing user. Cyberhaven’s extension was hit by a phished administrator account in late December 2024.

The defenses are mostly boring, which is a good sign. Hardware-key two-factor authentication on the publisher account, a short list of people allowed to publish, and a review step before each release stops most of it. Lockfiles, pinned versions and regular dependency audits cover the code side.

Teams that only install extensions can apply the same thinking. Keep an approved list, review updates that change permissions, and remove anything nobody uses. Following security news is worthwhile too, since incidents involving popular extensions tend to be reported publicly.

It is only one layer, though. A VPN protects traffic in transit. It does nothing about an add-on that reads page content inside the browser, so it never replaces a proper permission review.

Simpler habits do more here. A separate browser profile for development keeps test extensions away from personal accounts. Fake or low-privilege accounts for testing avoid exposing real credentials. And installers for any tool, VPN clients included, should come from the official site or store, never from a mirror.

Safe extensions come down to restraint. Ask for less access, collect less data, send less off the device, and explain more to the user. Developers who treat every permission as a liability end up with extensions people can trust, and teams that review what they install avoid the worst surprises.

#10385. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 still feels refreshing in all the best ways [Video]

With the iPhone Duo now a thing, it puts the Z Fold 8 in an even more interesting position. Yes, it’s an attempt by Samsung to get ahead of the curve, but, in truth, it really has worked.

So now that Apple is here, there is no doubt that the Z Fold 8 faces its fiercest competition, but to me, it highlights so much of what Samsung did so well with. I do not think that the Duo renders Samsung’s design obsolete. Apple’s entry into the market only sharpens the contrast between these competing design philosophies. It even underscores precisely why I’ve come to prefer Samsung’s passport-size footprint.

I’m not even sure it’s Samsung that has pushed the form factor forward too drastically.

I’m not alone in realizing that, for a lot of people, passport size makes the most sense. The outer screen is just enough of a taster without being too big; switch to the inner if you want a little more playroom is immediately obvious from the get-go. Everything feels reachable on the outer screen, while the inner screen is the big canvas for all other content.

Sure, the Z Fold 8’s outer display isn’t perfect. Some UI elements can feel cramped; you will need to adjust how tall your keyboard is, but it isn’t as rigid as what Apple seems to have done on the Duo. That’s a good thing as the flexibility of Android just suits the multi-sized form factor.

The thing is, you’re going to fall into one of two categories. Those of us who want a smaller, tablet-y shape, or a phone that happens to open to run more apps side-by-side.

Objectively, the “traditional” folding phone size and shape will be more useful in basically most scenarios. Subjectively, I don’t really care about those as much. I’m in the privileged position of having a decent selection of handsets on deck at any one time to test-drive, and those satisfy something else entirely.

As I was saying, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 has arguably been the most interesting and, now, my most-used phone throughout 2026. Of course, that is alongside the Pixel 10 Pro in early 2026 and now the 11 Pro. It has my backup SIM permanently tucked inside. Backup because I don’t get distracted as much with so many notifications, but that SIM has free roaming, making it my favorite travel companion…and my Balatro on-the-go handheld.

Every week, I’ll download a few movies and TV shows, like a sort of digital “prepper,” so I can be on a flight or a train and just know I have something to watch on a screen that tucks neatly into my side bag or jacket pocket. It is a mini tablet that weighs less than most other phones. You barely notice it. As I keep repeating: It feels so fresh.

One thing I have to appreciate about the slimline design is that I haven’t babied it; I have put a screen protector on the outer display for basic protection. It has handled a couple of days at the beach as an e-reader, and as yet, it’s accrued just one minor ding on the top frame edge, no major damage. That’s a good thing to me, as I haven’t found a Z Fold 8 case I really love yet. It’s super light and razor-thin; I never want to dull that sheen. It’s like the perfect realization of what the Flip ought to have been and likely won’t ever become.

I’ve seen a lot of complaints online about the inner display being both glossy and having a very visible crease. It’s not something I’ve dwelled on too much. They’re not big problems for me, in all honesty. I would like to see some enhancements, but it is what it is. That sounds like me handwaving away those complaints. I’m in agreement that we need to see Samsung make changes, especially now that Apple has a foldable with an almost-invisible crease and, frankly, a beautiful anti-reflective coating. Samsung is the undoubted king of displays, so we need more from the company in the future. That said, I still love the inner screen once I’m zipping around the system.

Against my better judgment, I have recently tried a “matte” screen protector from Amazon. So far, so good, but it’s nowhere near as matte as advertised. It does dull reflections, but I’m still on the lookout for something that might mimic the iPhone Duo display to see what the fuss is about. The screen protector I picked up does make a difference by reducing smears and fingerprints, but you might not want to put something on the inner screen, as Samsung doesn’t entirely recommend it, and it can be a bubbly mess if you get it wrong. It took me a few attempts to get the fit just right.

Back to the software: It helps that One UI is basically the perfect starting base for a foldable, and I think that is what has sealed the deal in a lot of ways. I’m a steadfast Material 3 Expressive fan, and while it’s still my preferred way to experience Android, One UI has continued to evolve in ways that Google likely doesn’t care about. I liked the Pixel 9 Pro Fold and 10 Pro Fold, but they felt less playful than the Fold 8 because I don’t think that Google does quite enough to truly optimize for the non-standard dual UI experience.

Sure, you need to use some of the Home Up and Good Lock plugins, but it opens up the door to vast customization that most other brands can’t really compete with. I like being able to make even minor adjustments to things like the volume UI or icon layout. Don’t get it twisted, I’m not doing insane things on my Z Fold 8, but even the minor adjustability holds weight on a device that acts as both a phone and a tablet, depending on which screen you’re about to use. Even the bottom dock is better placed than what I can glean from how Apple has added a right-sided dock on the iPhone Duo, while the volume buttons are just sensibly placed on the Fold, too.

The “big” apps are all well optimized. Instagram on the inner screen is awesome because you can see the comments and your feed in one instance. It feels like a tablet UI, probably because it is. There’s a little jank when running two apps side-by-side, but nothing that a little resizing can’t fix. I stick to two apps, as adding that third makes things cramped.

To expand on that a little further, I am actually really happy that Apple is about to wade into the foldable waters. For starters, it means we’ll hopefully get even better application support from the biggest developers.

From launch, the Z Fold 8 has had a few apps tuned to take better advantage of the smaller outer display, and while they’re not quite perfect, at least some work has gone into them. I’m really excited about the prospect of more apps being refined, and with so many big corps effectively relying on AI for faster ports, surely the next few years might prove to be a golden age for foldables. The truth is that most Android apps already scale reasonably well, so no major issues. The odd UI section might feel cramped here and there, but I don’t think I can complain too much.

Now, for a few of the weakest portions of the Z Fold 8 that can’t really be glossed over too much.

Yes, the camera is not quite as good as the best in the business.

At its worst, it’s purely functional, and ordinarily that would be a complete dealbreaker, but I’m at an age where a sharp, clear photo that does the job is more than good enough. Maybe I’m a glutton for this kind of punishment, as I find myself working around the limitations to get acceptable shots. It’s not ideal, but foldables compromise here more than any other devices, save the affordable options. So in that regard, you are well aware of that going in anyway.

It probably helps that I haven’t given up the Pixel 11 Pro as my mainstay photography phone. Not everyone can do that, so it’s arguably the biggest complaint you can have on the Z Fold 8 in my opinion – save the price, of course.

Battery life is another one of the weakest points. It will withstand most people’s usage patterns, but nobody should spend nearly £2,000 or $2,000 to have the Fold 8 not reach the end of the day. I think we all know by now that folding phones are going to compromise in this one area quite substantially. Luckily, it does manage to do movies and TV shows more than enough justice with longevity. I have watched multiple episodes of Widow’s Bay back-to-back on a couple of flights recently, and it doesn’t sap the battery all that much so long as you put the thing in flight mode.

In fact, I have been impressed when you’re not glued to the screen. It isn’t amazing, but I wouldn’t call the battery bad on the Z Fold 8 by any stretch. I have a super-slim Anker power bank in my bag at all times, so on a long-haul flight I’d just plug it in to be sure I can keep enjoying that lovely inner display. I can charge while watching and then just unplug, ready to go at my destination.

Now for the brief section where I will unashamedly just say some truly out-of-touch things (although this entire post is debatably out of touch): this is the most special phone I have used this year. Not even because it’s the best at any one thing. It could simply be a symptom of so much being “samey” or feeling so incremental in Western markets right now. This is a luxury product, and that instantly marks it down in most people’s eyes.

If Apple, the tastemakers of the non-tech buying public, are ready to give this form factor a real college try, then Samsung has made the right decision. The Z Fold 8 is easily the most elegant Android phone I’ve used in a while. I’m smitten with it. It’s something I want to unfold, fondle, and play with. What’s somewhat annoying is that, because I’ve enjoyed it so much, I’m giving in to a phone addiction I have tried so hard to curb over the past 18 months, since there is something refreshing here that is hard to truly quantify.

In my mind, there’s a Swiss Army Knife feel about the Z Fold 8 that no other phone has had for me for a long time. A tablet, gaming screen, reading screen, webpage dream all rolled into a passport package that respects my pockets – or the space in my sidebag.

It’s a really versatile gadget that isn’t just a siloed phone, and that is probably why it’s resonating with so many people – myself very much included.

If you’re bored with your current phone and ready to switch, the Z Fold 8 might be worth taking a look at, provided you can stomach the price.

I’ll drop some of the best deals currently available on the Z Fold 8 down below, but expect to see discounts and sales soon, now that Apple has entered the fray and we’re on the cusp of Black Friday sales in the coming weeks.

#10381. Someone is bringing Android Auto back to your phone’s screen, and it actually works [Gallery]

When Android Auto first debuted, it didn’t require a fancy display in your car. It just worked on your phone screen and, now, someone is bringing that functionality back after Google killed it.

Back in 2019, four years after Android Auto hit the scene, Google announced that it would be shutting down support for using Android Auto on your phone’s display. Its replacement, Google Assistant’s driving mode, was so unfinished at launch that it required Google to release “Android Auto for Phone Screens” as a stopgap. That later shut down in 2022 as Google made Android Auto “only available for car screens.”

And, in the time since, there haven’t been very many good alternatives to that functionality. You can get in-car displays for cheaper and cheaper – the average prices on Amazon are around $80 or so – but it’s also just conveinent to get it on the phone screen.

This new app is in development and soon to debut on the Google Play Store and lets you run Android Auto on your phone screen, just like you used to be able to. Well, almost like you used to. The difference here is in how the app gets Android Auto going, using the “head unit server” developer functionality to run Android Auto on-device. It takes a couple of added clicks but gets the job done, but it does work. You also have to tap the “quit” button in your notifications to stop the head unit server from running after you’re done. “PhoneAuto” can partially automate this if you grant it accessibility permissions.

As for actual performance and functionality, it works about as well as I’d expect from a beta.

On my Galaxy Z Fold 8, the portrait mode setting doesn’t work very well, with plenty of lag in play, but the landscape setting works just fine on both the inner and outer displays. The layout adjusts to your screen, just as it does in a car, and I found that touch is responsive enough. It doesn’t run at a full frame rate as you’ll find in some newer vehicles.

But it works. It puts Android Auto on your phone’s display, something you haven’t been able to do for years.

The beta is available for sideloading via GitHub – install at your own risk, as always – with an expiration in January. Presumably it’ll be available in the Play Store before that date.

#10378. Xbox’s first chief operating officer is out after just a few months

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Helen Chiang is leaving Microsoft after 21 years.

Helen Chiang is leaving Microsoft after 21 years.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Xbox’s first chief operating officer (COO) is leaving after the new role was created just a few months ago. Helen Chiang is leaving Microsoft today, according to an internal Xbox memo seen by The Verge. Chiang has been at Microsoft for 21 years, serving as the head of Minecraft before being promoted to Xbox chief operating officer in July.

Xbox CEO Asha Sharma announced Chiang’s departure in a surprisingly short memo today. “Helen Chiang is leaving Microsoft today. I’m grateful for her years of service and wish her well,” said Sharma. As Chiang is departing today, her immediate responsibilities are being split up between other Xbox leaders for now, and Sharma notes that “we’ll share more on COO leadership as plans are finalized.”

That sounds like the COO position isn’t going away after just a few months, but it’s not clear why Chiang is departing after such a short stint in the role. At the time of Chiang’s promotion, Sharma described the new COO role as a first for Xbox, “with end-to-end P&L responsibility across content, hardware, platform, and services.” The role was created as part of Xbox’s big “reset.”

Chiang reported directly to Sharma and was promoted to “bring our businesses together under one operating model, making sure we make clear investment decisions, learn from our successes and failures, and hold ourselves accountable for results.”

Chiang departs amid rumors that Xbox is preparing more layoffs this month, in November, and in January. The sudden and short memo from Sharma, coupled with Chiang’s responsibilities being assigned to other Xbox leaders, makes it seem like this wasn’t a resignation.

I asked Microsoft to comment on Chiang’s departure, and the company refused to discuss the circumstances of her exit. “We don’t discuss individual personnel matters,” says Kari Perez, Xbox vice president of communications. “We’re grateful for Helen’s years of service and wish her well.”

#10375. Browser Fingerprinting Explained: How Websites Identify You and How Developers Can Reduce It

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most articles on this topic stop at the scary part. This one is written for people who build websites, so we’ll go a bit further: what goes into a fingerprint, what the collection code actually looks like, and what you can change in your own projects.

Think of a fingerprint as a stack of clues rather than a single value. Your time zone is shared by millions of people. So is your screen resolution. Put twenty clues like that together, though, and the crowd shrinks fast.

Here’s roughly where the clues come from.

HTTP headers. Before any JavaScript runs, every request already tells the server a lot. The User-Agent string and the Accept-Language header give away your browser, its version, your OS, and your languages. Chromium browsers also send Client Hints.

JavaScript APIs. Plenty of everyday APIs leak device details. screen.width and screen.height, navigator.hardwareConcurrency (your CPU core count), navigator.deviceMemory (Chromium only), navigator.platform, and your time zone all add something. Plugin lists used to be a goldmine, but modern browsers now return a fixed, generic list, so that one’s mostly dead.

Rendering. This is the clever bit. Ask two computers to draw the exact same text, and the pixels won’t match perfectly. Fonts, graphics drivers, and the GPU itself all nudge the output slightly. Same browser, same version, different hardware, different result.

Network details. Your IP address, connection type, and even the way your browser sets up a TLS connection can help. IPs change, so these clues are less reliable. Trackers mostly use them to back up a match they already suspect.

How unique does this make you? Back in 2010, the EFF’s Panopticlick study tested around 470,000 browsers and found that about 84% of them had a unique fingerprint. (The project still exists, now called Cover Your Tracks.) Later studies with bigger samples found lower numbers, especially on phones, since a lot of phones share near-identical hardware. The percentages move around, but the basic idea hasn’t changed.

Canvas fingerprinting gets talked about the most. Here’s a basic version:

Read through it and you’d never guess it was tracking code. It draws an orange box, writes some text, and exports the image. The catch is that anti-aliasing and subpixel rendering vary from device to device, so that exported string varies too.

The data URL is long and clunky, so trackers usually hash it into something shorter. With the Web Crypto API, that’s a few extra lines:

WebGL fingerprinting follows the same idea with 3D graphics. A script draws a hidden scene and also reads the WEBGL_debug_renderer_info extension, which can reveal your GPU’s make and model. Audio fingerprinting is sneakier. The script generates a sound, runs it through an AudioContext, and hashes the result. Different audio stacks produce slightly different numbers.

It’s worth remembering that the Canvas API, WebGL, and the Web Audio API were built for charts, games, and music apps. Tracking is a side effect of how much they expose. The W3C’s guidance on mitigating fingerprinting in web specifications covers how standards authors try to avoid adding new signals.

You might not write a single line of fingerprinting code yourself and still end up involved. There are three common ways that happens.

Your third-party scripts can do it without asking you. Analytics tags, ad scripts, chat widgets, social buttons. Each of them can call the same APIs your own code can. If your page loads fifteen of them, that’s fifteen possible fingerprinters. Checking what each script does has quietly become part of the job.

Browsers are closing the door. Chrome froze most of the User-Agent string and moved detailed info into Client Hints. Safari has Advanced Fingerprinting Protection. Firefox blocks known fingerprinting scripts, and Brave randomizes values returned by fingerprintable APIs. If your site depends on the old, wide-open APIs, you’re building on ground that’s shrinking every release.

Most of this comes down to one rule: collect less, and know why you’re collecting what’s left.

Search your codebase for the high-entropy stuff: canvas exports, WebGL renderer lookups, font detection, and calls to navigator.userAgentData.getHighEntropyValues(). If a feature still works without them, take them out.

In many teams, the biggest win is boring. Someone added a fingerprinting library years ago for one fraud check, nobody reviewed it since, and it’s still loading on every page. Deleting it helps more than any clever fix.

The classic User-Agent string packs browser, exact version, OS, and CPU architecture into one header. Client Hints break that apart so you only get what you ask for.

By default, Chromium browsers already send three basic hints: Sec-CH-UA, Sec-CH-UA-Mobile, and Sec-CH-UA-Platform. For most sites, that’s plenty, and you don’t need to request anything extra. If a feature really does need more detail, ask for that one hint with Accept-CH:

Skip full version numbers, architecture, and device model unless you have a real reason. Each extra hint makes your visitors a little easier to tell apart.

Keep in mind that only Chromium-based browsers support User-Agent Client Hints. Firefox and Safari won’t send them, so you’ll need a fallback. Where you can, feature detection beats browser detection anyway.

Here’s an uncomfortable truth: once a script runs on your page, it can call canvas, WebGL, and audio APIs, and there’s no header that switches those off. What you can control is which scripts run and how close they get to your page.

None of these is a silver bullet. Together, they cut down how much code gets the chance to fingerprint your users at all.

If your app draws on canvas (dashboards, editors, browser games), that output can be fingerprinted too. Try not to hand raw canvas data to third-party scripts.

Fonts count as well. Load fifteen unusual web fonts and you’ve given every script on the page an easy font fingerprint. A system font stack blends in better, and for interface text, most users won’t notice the difference.

Sometimes you’ll want to keep a fingerprint, for example to flag logins from a device you haven’t seen before. That’s fine, as long as you treat it with care:

Before you ship, open your site in Firefox with Enhanced Tracking Protection set to Strict, and in Brave with fingerprinting protection turned on. If something breaks, it was relying on a signal browsers are trying to get rid of.

Fix the feature. Don’t ask users to turn their protection down. Browsers are only going to get stricter from here.

What works better is a browser with fingerprinting protection built in, such as Brave, Firefox on strict settings, Safari, or Tor Browser. These either add noise to the signals or make everyone look the same. Extensions that block canvas and font reads can help too. So can simply updating your browser, since each release tends to remove a few more leaky details.

No single tool will stop fingerprinting completely. The good news is that it doesn’t have to. Fingerprinting works on probabilities, so making the signals noisier is often enough to break the match.

#10374. This Switch 2 sequel accessory was a few millimeters away from being good

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Crkd’s $99.99 Nitro Deck 2 has some improvements, with Switch and Switch 2 compatibility, but it’s far from essential.

Crkd’s $99.99 Nitro Deck 2 has some improvements, with Switch and Switch 2 compatibility, but it’s far from essential.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Crkd Nitro Deck was a saving grace for the original Nintendo Switch. The slide-on $59.99 accessory brought Hall effect sticks to drift-plagued consoles — no need to open up your Joy-Con for a complicated repair. The Nitro Deck 2, made for both the original Switch and the larger Switch 2, continues the tradition of having better sticks than what Nintendo puts in even the newest Joy-Con (though these aren’t Hall effect or TMR). But it doesn’t feel as essential, and there’s one thing I really can’t stand about it.

It’s too difficult to use the Nitro Deck 2’s ABXY face buttons without the right stick getting in the way. The stick-to-button arrangement is a big step down from the original Nitro Deck (and especially bad compared with the symmetrical Steam Deck-like stick layout in the video output-ready Nitro Deck Plus). I’m surprised Crkd’s team thought this was an improvement. My patience with the run-based gameplay in Echo Weaver is already tested enough, and trying to quickly hammer inputs on the Deck 2 only adds to the frustration.

Your preference may differ, but I like having a little breathing room between the right stick and the face buttons. Some companies choose to space them out or, in the case of the Switch 2’s Joy-Con, make the stick small enough to allow my thumb to hover over it so I can press the buttons. The Deck 2 makes me adjust my thumb’s angle of approach outside of what’s comfortable. It’s just a few millimeters off.

The original’s superior slide-in design was traded for a vice-grip design to ensure compatibility with both the newer and older Switch consoles. The Deck 2 has a twisting mechanism on the back that you can loosen, tighten, or lock. It’s clever, though the tradeoff for broader compatibility is a design that feels inherently less secure. Crkd is asking Switch 2 owners to put a ton of faith in this mechanism staying tight around their $500 consoles. My Switch 2 didn’t feel wobbly during testing, but I’d rather not have to think about its safety at all when I’m gaming. I would have preferred a slide-in design for the Deck 2 made expressly for the Switch 2.

It’s disappointing that Crkd’s Joy-Con-replacing solution isn’t as good because I like some things about it more than the original. For instance, the accessory has adjustable tension on both sticks, so they can snap back to their original position faster or slower than the default speed. Also, the accessory’s bulkier design makes the console more comfortable in my medium-size hands to use in handheld mode. It also has a bunch of customizable buttons: four on its back and an extra near the triggers.

Not many similar accessories are out there yet, and the other one I tried (the abxylute N6) wasn’t all that good, either. I plan to test Mobapad’s latest take on Joy-Con soon, and it seems promising. Alternatively, you can add a bunch of comfort to your Switch 2 in handheld mode with either the Dbrand Killswitch 2 or the Mechanism Basecase. And with the money left over, you could buy a good wireless Switch 2 controller with long-lasting sticks and better rumble.

#10371. Gemini Notebook for Android, iOS adds built-in audio recorder

After introducing Connected App integration last week, Gemini Notebook has rolled out an audio recorder on Android and iOS.

Between the camera and “Create New” floating action button at the bottom, you’ll see a new microphone icon. This lets you “capture lectures and record your thoughts on the go,” with the audio file creating a new notebook if you launch the recorder from the homepage.

Your notes will live directly alongside your sources, meaning you can chat, cite, and build from them anytime, all while making ongoing edits.

Recordings will be saved to an existing Notebook if you tap the microphone from the Sources tab.

The recorder interface is very simple with a pause button and checkmark that starts the upload once you’re done. A Gemini Notebook-themed waveform and live count appear above. You can switch to another application or lock your screen and have recordings continue in the background. There’s a 1-hour capture limit.

Google first announced the audio recorder in September, and it’s now widely rolling out today on Android and iOS. Force stop the app if it’s not live on your device.

Meanwhile, Google this week made a small tweak wherein tapping the “Create New” FAB immediately starts a new notebook rather than sliding up the “Add a source” panel.

These updates follow the recent launch of the Live button in the Chat tab that lets you have a real-time conversation with your notebooks.

#10367. 8BitDo’s new Xbox Elite-style controller sets a high bar for customization

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The retro controller maker’s first real swing at a premium controller feels great, but it doesn’t earn its high price tag.

The retro controller maker’s first real swing at a premium controller feels great, but it doesn’t earn its high price tag.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It’s hard to make an Xbox controller that stands out these days, especially one that’s expensive, due to a wealth of great semi-affordable options. Still, 8BitDo’s $150 Ultimate 3E is a bold controller that holds its own against other feature-packed options. It’s made for Xbox and PC gamers who want a deeper level of customization and a more refined design than Microsoft’s pricier Xbox Elite Series 2 and 8BitDo’s own $60 Ultimate 2.

Basically every feature I’m looking for in a controller is here, plus some, including drift-proof TMR joysticks, extra shoulder buttons, rear paddles, motion control support, modular buttons and joysticks, a 3.5mm audio jack for chat or private listening, and a 1,000Hz polling rate over a low-latency wireless connection (only when connected to a PC). It includes a carrying case and a wireless charging dock that you can stash the USB dongle within.

8BitDo’s first premium controller competes directly with other big names, like Razer, PowerA, SteelSeries, and rising star GameSir. But the Ultimate 3E is also among the most expensive of the bunch, and I’m not sure the company delivers enough additional features to pull it off.

The Ultimate 3E’s modular design opens up more customization than a lot of other controllers I’ve tried. It includes two four-button modules (one with membrane buttons and another with clickier microswitch buttons), two directional pads, and three extra joysticks. That level of customization and included accessories should hopefully help it age more gracefully than cheaper alternatives.

Its TMR joystick mechanisms shouldn’t wear down, so you’ll only need to be concerned with replacing the joystick tops down the line, and the controller includes several to choose from. However, 8BitDo doesn’t sell replacement accessories for the Ultimate 3E, as it does for many of its other controllers, and it hasn’t responded to The Verge as to whether it ever will.

The Ultimate 3E’s modularity also helps somewhat to make up for the high price tag. 8BitDo’s Pro 3 already lets you swap individual magnetically attaching buttons to accommodate different console layouts. The Ultimate 3E doesn’t offer as much freedom of per-button placement as the Pro 3, likely due to being intended for use with Xbox and PC, but the joy of using its microswitch button module makes up for the limitation. They’re sharp and clicky, although they have a bit more travel than I’d like.

My other complaints are relatively minor. The clips that hold the face button module in place are very tight, and I found myself having to carefully use a plastic toothpick to pop them out. Similarly, swapping out joysticks requires a surprising amount of force, and I had to work up the courage to yank them off the first time. And, even when set to their higher response point, the triggers feel a bit soft and, like the membrane buttons, have more travel than I prefer, although the extra shoulder button can offset that travel distance in some situations. The soft touch grips and back gather dust and gunk pretty quickly, but you can at least clean the joystick tops by removing them.

It’s as weird of a time to be releasing a new high-end Xbox controller as it is shopping for one. Assuming you’re not content with the solid but basic Microsoft model that’s around $50, it seems unwise to commit to spending well over $100 on one given the increased frequency of Xbox Elite Series 3 leaks. What we’ve seen of that, while unofficial, seems interesting: It doesn’t appear to offer the modular customization of the Ultimate 3E, but its screen and knobs might be what some people want in their next controller.

As for how the Ultimate 3E stacks up to other great controllers, Microsoft’s Elite Series 2 is a poor value by comparison. It uses potentiometer joysticks that are likely to drift over time, which to me feels disqualifying given the price point. I don’t think anyone using 8BitDo’s Ultimate 2 should feel the urge to upgrade to the Ultimate 3E. There just aren’t enough differences to make it worthwhile. However, there’s also a non-modular 8BitDo Ultimate 3 for $99, which may end up being a sweet spot for more budget-conscious gamers who don’t need to tinker as much.

For what it’s worth, I’ll be sitting down with the GameSir G7 Pro to play GTA VI. It’s one of my favorite Xbox controllers, and it has a nearly identical set of features and specs to the 8BitDo. Better yet, it’s only $80 at full price and comes in a variety of colors, with swappable faceplates for sale.

Overall, the Ultimate 3E feels similar to use as other Elite-style controllers I’ve tried, and that’s impressive for a company that hasn’t tried to take on pricier competition before. Whether you opt for the cheaper Ultimate 3 with mostly similar specs or the pricier, more customizable Ultimate 3E, 8BitDo’s high-end gamepads make a strong impression, but they’re tough to recommend when there are cheaper alternatives that don’t slack on features.
