#10365. 6 Email Tools Developers Can Use to Build, Test, and Monitor Better Email

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A password-reset email can leave your application successfully and still fail the user. It might arrive too late, display an unreadable button, or contain a link pointing to the staging environment.

These failures belong to different parts of the email workflow. Fixing the HTML will not repair authentication, and improving sender reputation will not correct an expired verification token.

For developers and designers, the useful question is therefore: which tool helps us inspect the layer that is failing?

The following six options cover mailbox reputation, local testing, message construction, responsive design, compatibility research, and production monitoring. They serve different purposes; they are not interchangeable alternatives.

For teams managing connected business mailboxes, EmailPond describes a service for automated warmup and reputation monitoring, including connections to Google Workspace and Microsoft 365.

The engineering lesson is to distinguish three outcomes:

A successful submission does not establish that a message reached the inbox.

When evaluating mailbox warmup or reputation services, ask what their measurements actually represent. Are they based on participating test accounts, provider feedback, or a proprietary score? What period and sample size do they cover?

Treat any score as one diagnostic input. A result from a testing network is not evidence that every real recipient will receive the same placement.

Also match the tool to your infrastructure. A connected employee mailbox and an application sending through a transactional email provider may use different delivery paths. Activity on one does not demonstrate the health of the other.

Practical exercise: document the sending path for each message type: visible From address, sending provider, authentication domain, and responsible team. This makes later investigations much more precise.

Mailpit runs an SMTP testing server with a web interface and an API for inspecting captured messages. It is useful when testing registration, receipts, invitations, and password resets without delivering development messages to real customers.

Use local capture to inspect more than appearance:

Point the application at SMTP host 127.0.0.1, port 1025, and open http://127.0.0.1:8025 to inspect messages. This example assumes the application runs on the host; an application in another container needs appropriate container networking.

Binding the ports to localhost keeps this development instance off public interfaces. For a repeatable team environment, pin the image to a reviewed version or digest.

Practical exercise: trigger account verification, capture the message, and confirm that the link works once and fails after reuse. Test token behavior through the application, not just by inspecting the email.

Nodemailer provides email-sending functionality for Node.js, including SMTP transport and HTML/plain-text message construction.

Keeping message construction in a dedicated module makes it easier to review and test. Here is a minimal local example using Mailpit:

Run it with node send-test.mjs. Production configuration needs the authentication and TLS settings required by your provider.

SitePoint’s guide to sending email using Node.js provides additional context for building an email workflow.

Practical exercise: move rendering into a separate function and test missing optional fields, long names, and HTML-sensitive characters. Escape untrusted text when inserting it into HTML.

MJML is a markup language that compiles components into responsive email HTML. Its sections, columns, text blocks, and buttons let teams maintain a readable template source.

Keep source templates in version control and compile them during a reproducible build. Review the final output after personalization and any link rewriting.

A component system reduces repetitive layout work, but it does not establish that every message is accessible or renders correctly in every client.

Practical exercise: test a long translated heading, enlarged text, blocked images, and a narrow viewport. The essential message and primary action should remain understandable in each case.

SitePoint’s HTML email design overview offers background on designing within email-client constraints. Pair its established principles with current compatibility data.

Can I Email publishes support information for HTML and CSS across email clients. It helps developers investigate a specific feature before building a template around it.

Start with the clients that matter to your audience. Then inspect support notes for the features your design depends on, including partial support and the date of testing.

A global support percentage cannot tell you whether a feature works for your particular recipients.

For critical content, design a usable fallback. A button should remain identifiable without a decorative background image. A heading should communicate its meaning even when a custom font is unavailable.

Practical exercise: select three visual features in a proposed template and document what users see when each is unsupported. If losing a feature hides the primary action, revise the design.

Compatibility research narrows the risks. Testing the final message in representative clients checks the actual result. SitePoint’s HTML email testing guide provides further background on that review process.

Google Postmaster Tools provides information about email sent to personal Gmail accounts, including authentication, reputation, spam reports, and delivery errors.

Use it alongside application and sending-provider telemetry. Its data concerns Gmail traffic; it is not a universal view of delivery across every mailbox provider.

For example, if delivery errors increase after a provider change, compare the timing with authentication configuration and sending-volume changes. If users report missing messages while the application shows no failures, inspect later delivery events rather than assuming the original SMTP response proves success.

Google’s email sender guidelines explain authentication and sending requirements. SPF authorizes sending infrastructure for the envelope domain, DKIM provides a verifiable signature, and DMARC evaluates alignment with the visible From domain. Configuring them correctly is foundational, but does not guarantee inbox placement.

Practical exercise: create a release checklist that verifies authentication on real test messages from the production sending path, rather than checking DNS records alone.

Tool selection becomes useful when each tool supports a clear acceptance check.

Production systems also need explicit retry behavior. Queueing email can keep slow sending operations out of an HTTP request, but retries can create duplicate messages.

Use a stable application event identifier to track a notification attempt. Where a provider supports idempotency keys, use them according to its documented limits. Otherwise, record provider message identifiers and handle uncertain outcomes carefully: a timeout can occur after the provider has accepted a message.

Test expired notifications as well. A password-reset message delayed in a queue should not promise that its link is still valid.

For delivery-event webhooks, verify authenticity using the provider’s documented mechanism, deduplicate repeated events, and tolerate out-of-order arrival. Avoid logging message bodies or authentication tokens as routine diagnostics.

A useful email workflow gives the team a clear answer to each question: what did we generate, where did we send it, what happened afterward, and could the recipient complete the intended task?

Start with local capture and reliable message construction. Add template and compatibility checks. Then connect production delivery signals to application events.

The measure of success is whether users can verify an account, retrieve a report, or recover access reliably—not simply whether a send function returned successfully.

#10364. Samsung’s open-ear Galaxy Buds On cost $229 in the US, launching next month

After launching in Korea earlier this month, Samsung has confirmed that its open-ear Galaxy Buds On will cost $229 in the US and will go on sale early next month.

Galaxy Buds On are clip-style, open-ear earbuds that are designed to be super lightweight and let you hear music, podcasts, and more without blocking sound from the world around you. The form factor has been used by other brands, but is new to Samsung’s lineup.

The company announced Galaxy Buds On earlier in October in Korea, touting Gemini Live integration, 9.5-hour battery life, automatic volume adjustments, and more. Today’s announcement also calls out features like “Intelligent Clear Call” and “Quick Voice Memo,” as well as Bixby integration for searching through recordings “hands-free.” There’s also gesture support for answering/declining calls, dismissing alarms, and more.

Galaxy Buds On are available solely in black and the earbuds have the same case design as Galaxy Buds 4/Pro.

Pricing lands at $229 in the US, making these Samsung’s second-most expensive pair of wireless earbuds behind the $249 Galaxy Buds 4 Pro. Galaxy Buds 4 are $179. Pre-orders open today with shipping in “early November” – a specific release date has not been confirmed.

If you’re a patient buyer, you can get a 256GB Galaxy S25 Ultra for a reasonable price, as one of 2025’s best flagship phones is down to just $799 in the latest Amazon discount.

A ton of Samsung’s lineup has been discounted recently, and this is a solid deal for what I’d argue is the best S-series phone in a while. The display is much better than the Galaxy S26 Ultra, and the colors are better too, in my opinion. Check out our long-term review of the device here if you want a verdict:

The price has fluctuated for a while, but at $799 for the 256GB variant, you’re getting a heck of a lot of phone for your money. As I noted, the 6.9-inch AMOLED anti-reflective display is GOATed; it has the Qualcomm Snapdragon Elite, 12GB RAM, same camera system as the S26 Ultra, S-Pen if you care about that, and it has just recently received One UI 9 as its second major Android update since launch. You’ll get another 5 years of Android OS upgrades, too, for what it’s worth.

It’s hard to fault the Galaxy S25 Ultra even today. Some might even prefer this marginally older model over some newer devices like the Pixel 11 series, as you get more bang for your buck. The only limitation is that this deal is currently only for the Titanium Gray finish – if that matters to you.

There is peace of mind in picking up a brand-new device with a fresh battery and unsullied hardware over a used or renewed model. If you prefer to stick to renewed devices, you can pick up the 512GB S25 Ultra for just $645 from the Amazon Renewed storefront, and it’s backed by the firm’s industry-leading return policies.

#10359. Microsoft’s AI future has fewer jobs

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It’s also time to name the next Elder Scrolls game, and Xbox’s architect of Forza Motorsport is leaving.

It’s also time to name the next Elder Scrolls game, and Xbox’s architect of Forza Motorsport is leaving.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

To anyone listening closely to what Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella has been saying over the past couple of years, this week felt like the clearest sign yet that AI is about to fundamentally change the size of Microsoft’s workforce. Microsoft is now offering voluntary buyouts to around 60,000 employees across the US, UK, Canada, and India.

It’s the first time the company has presented such an offer to so many employees, expanding greatly upon the retirement offer that was limited to around 9,000 employees earlier this year. It’s all part of an ongoing effort to avoid reducing investment in AI infrastructure by lowering Microsoft’s operating costs and headcount.

“Our total people costs will go down, our cost per head will go up, and my GPU per researcher will go up,” Nadella said on a podcast almost two years ago, responding to a question about AI’s impact on headcount. That’s a financial way of saying Microsoft will have fewer people, but it will offer higher salaries and more tools and AI power to those who remain.

It also helps explain the continued layoffs we’ve seen at Microsoft over the past couple of years. Microsoft has been performing layoffs regularly over the past decade, but over the past couple of years, the layoffs and executive departures have been significant.

These aren’t the annual layoffs of old; they’re now happening every few months. This week’s voluntary exit offer arrived just months after Microsoft laid off 4,800 employees, cuts that impacted its Xbox and commercial sales teams the most.

For the first time in a decade, Microsoft’s overall headcount has also gone down year over year. Microsoft now employs around 223,000 people as of June 30th, 5,000 fewer than the 228,000 people it employed in 2025. Microsoft laid off 4 percent of its workforce in July last year, and it’s now offering voluntary buyout offers to around a quarter of its employees.

More than 30 percent of the 9,000 eligible employees accepted a retirement offer earlier this year, but that number will likely be far less for the company’s latest Voluntary Exit Offer. That’s largely because, according to sources familiar, the offer is practically the same as regular severance terms at Microsoft — cash based on time at the company, accelerated stock vesting, some additional subsidized health coverage, and several months of career services.

But what makes this unusual is the scale of the amount of employees who have been offered this exit. Even if only 5 percent accept the offer, that’s still 3,000 employees. I understand eligible employees have until December to decide, so we won’t know the impact of this offer for a while yet. With rumors of more layoffs in November and January, the amount of employees who do accept will surely inform Microsoft’s numbers for its next round of cuts.

There are some, both inside and outside the company, who think Microsoft needs these cuts. “Microsoft needs fewer people,” says Merill Fernando, a former Microsoft Entra product manager. “I’ve been on the inside. I’ve seen how things get built. Lots of small orgs. Layers on layers. Silos everywhere.”

Fernando paints a picture of a Microsoft where every organization “had its own priorities and its own decision makers,” so it’s hard to get the company to do anything collectively. It sounds a little like the meme where each organization in Microsoft is pointing guns at each other, competing instead of collaborating.

Nadella even addressed that meme last year in a podcast with Stripe co-founder John Collison, acknowledging that the comic has defined Microsoft’s cultural narrative “more so than reality,” but that it had signaled some very real tensions inside the company. “In some sense, some of these divisional tensions are real issues that need to have tension. Social cohesion is not a goal, winning in the marketplace is a goal,” said Nadella.

The question now is whether Nadella can reshape Microsoft for the AI era without undoing the cultural transformation that helped define his leadership. A company that has shifted to be built around empathy is now asking its employees to reckon with an AI future that could make many of their jobs unnecessary. How Nadella handles that transition will define his legacy as much as the growth and success he’s achieved over the past decade.

A few weeks ago I started hearing from Xbox employees that Dan Greenawalt, the longtime creative force behind Forza Motorsport, is leaving after almost 27 years. Greenawalt is departing as the head of Turn 10 Studios, the Forza Motorsport creator that merged with Forza Horizon developer Playground Games.

Playground Games studio head Trevor Williams revealed the news in a recent internal memo, seen by Notepad. “Dan Greenawalt has decided that this is the right time for him to retire from Microsoft,” says Williams. Greenawalt will stay on as an adviser to Williams and the newly appointed Playground Games chief operating officer, Michel Bastien, “while he transitions to his next phase.” Here’s part of Williams’ memo about Greenawalt:

It’s hard to overstate Dan’s impact over the past 27 years. From shipping Project Gotham Racing on the original XBOX in 2001 to leading the creation of Forza, founding Turn 10 Studios and appearing so many times on our E3 stage, usually making a dramatic entrance with a supercar in tow – and so much more – we owe tremendous thanks and gratitude to Dan. On behalf of both teams, I would like to thank Dan for his leadership, his support, and his partnership. I wish him well for whatever comes next.

Microsoft revealed last month that it was merging its two Forza developers into a single studio as part of a massive restructuring of Xbox. Both Playground Games and Turn 10 Studios have merged into just Playground Games. The studio now has two locations: Playground Games Leamington Spa and Playground Games Redmond.

With Greenawalt retiring and Turn 10 merged into Playground Games, it’s not clear if we’ll ever see Microsoft return to Forza Motorsport. More than 70 people at Turn 10 Studios were laid off last year, in a move that hit the vast majority of the studio. Over the past year, Turn 10 Studios has essentially been maintaining Forza Motorsport, and now the remaining employees have moved to Playground Games to work on Forza Horizon and Fable.

It’s been more than eight years since Bethesda officially announced the existence of the sixth game in its Elder Scrolls series. While there have been some debates around platform exclusivity for The Elder Scrolls VI, we’ve heard very little detail about the game that Bethesda has been working on all this time.

Sources familiar with Bethesda’s plans tell me that the Xbox-owned publisher is about to announce that the next Elder Scrolls game will officially be called The Elder Scrolls VI: Sentinel. The announcement could come as soon as next month, during a period that will mark 15 years since The Elder Scrolls V: Skyrim was released.

Bethesda has been showing off Sentinel gameplay in recent months, I’m told. Xbox CEO Asha Sharma got a live playthrough of Sentinel in August, and she praised the scale of the game and said “the story is even greater.”

While I’m only expecting a name reveal next month and nothing more, I’ve been hearing some additional details about the game. If you want to totally avoid spoilers about the game, then don’t read the next paragraph.

I understand that Sentinel will be set in Hammerfell, a province in the Elder Scrolls universe that’s appeared in The Elder Scrolls Adventures: Redguard and The Elder Scrolls Online. Sentinel will be the first game about the history of the Elder Scrolls, and help uncover the secrets of these mysterious ancient artifacts.

#10358. Samsung can’t decide between a larger Galaxy Z Fold 9 or one with worse specs, and neither can I

Following a smash hit in the smaller and wider Galaxy Z Fold 8, Samsung is obviously weighing its options behind the scenes when it comes to sequels. According to one leaker, the company is currently deciding between adding another Galaxy Z Fold 9 with a larger display, or one with lesser specs, and I can’t decide which one I want to see either.

Leaker Lanzuk on Naver says that Samsung is adding more options to the Galaxy Z Fold 9 lineup next year after the Galaxy Z Fold 8 garnered “much better response than expected” (translated). It’s all-but-official that Samsung is expanding the lineup – we’ve heard this repeatedly at this point – but exactly what joins the lineup remains unclear.

This latest rumor suggests two possibilities.

The first is a larger version of the Galaxy Z Fold 8, a “Galaxy Z Fold 9 Plus,” the leaker suggests. This wouldn’t be an Ultra device, but rather would share the same wide form factor as the standard Fold 9, just with a larger pair of displays.

The other option is a “Galaxy Z Fold 9 FE” which would downgrade the specs to achieve a lower price point. Samsung tried this with the Galaxy Z Flip 7 FE to essentially no success, but the company’s cuts on that device were far from compelling on a form factor that was already in decline.

Samsung is said to be testing both form factors and “a decision will be made soon,” but only one of the two would move to production and a full release.

Personally, I’m split here. I’ve been using the Galaxy Z Fold 8 as my daily smartphone since its launch, and the size is certainly one of its most attractive features. On the one hand, I’d love to have a larger version for better media consumption and multitasking, but I also think Samsung would run into diminishing returns with a larger size. The wider form factor wouldn’t scale nicely to a larger size too, I think.

Meanwhile, a more affordable version seems like a clear win. This form factor has so clearly resonated with customers, and having an option that makes strategic cuts to hit a price of say, $1,400, would be a home run. However, given Samsung’s existing struggles with profitability amid RAMageddon and ever-rising prices, that also feels unrealistic.

I’m very torn on which would be the better option. Which one would you prefer?

#10354. Motorola’s flagship Signature 27 sounds quite expensive in retail leak

Motorola’s return to the proper flagship space is coming in the Signature 27, but early leaks suggest this new device, as well as the company’s wide foldable, won’t exactly be cheap.

GSMArena and “Android Treasure“ spotted retail listings in Europe that give us some idea of what Motorola’s upcoming releases will cost.

The Motorola Signature 27 is listed at €1,594.90 through a retailer website based out of Portugal. For comparison, the Galaxy S26 Ultra is sold for €1,299 in Portugal. The listing calls out some marketing points including the 50MP Sony Lytia L910 main sensor, 200MP telephoto camera, as well as a “unique cooling system uses thermal gel with diamond particles and a new 3D vapor chamber to maintain performance for longer.” It also mentions weight of “0.4kg” which seems… unlikely given that would make for a phone that weighs 400g. Perhaps that’s the shipping weight. There’s also mention of a 12GB/512GB configuration.

Circling back to the price, it’s unclear whether or not that’s strictly accurate, but it’s not exactly out of the question.

With Motorola Signature 27 packing top-tier specs including Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6, it was always going to be pricey. And if €1,600~ is the final price, it could be a lot worse. As a reminder, Oppo Find X10 Pro Max recently made its debut in the EU for €1,800.

The same retailer also lists the “Razr Flex,” Motorola’s wide foldable, at €1,510~. This one very much seems like a placeholder listing given there’s no imagery and a generic description compared to the Signature’s listing. The price also seems borderline impossible seeing as the Galaxy Z Fold 8 sells for over €2,200 in Portugal.

What these listings do suggest is that Motorola is gearing up for launch on these new products pretty soon.

What would you pay for Motorola Signature 27?

#10351. Public Discord messages might start showing up in Google Search, unless users opt out

Discord announced today that Discoverable Server web pages will begin showing summaries and pulled content, which might show up in search engines.

Discord splits its servers into three different types: private, community, and discoverable. The latter is the most public version. No one needs an invite, and anyone can message, given that the admins of that server haven’t blocked you completely.

In a recent press release, Discord notes that all Discoverable Servers will get a Server Web Page. The page is essentially an overall summary of that server, so users can easily gauge whether they its something they want to join.

Discord says that discussion summaries will also appear on those server web pages, extracted from public channels. Any conversation that might be deemed noteworthy, whether by an AI model or moderator, could appear on that web page, and by extension, search engines like Google.

It sounds like Discord is hoping channels surface in Google’s AI Mode or other engines, like Reddit, when users search for certain topics. It’s not a bad idea, as message boards often contain answers to incredibly niche questions otherwise hard to find on the internet.

The release makes it clear that server admins can exclude certain channels and channel summaries, but doesn’t say the entire server can opt out. That seems to only be possible by switching server types.

Discord says these changes will take effect starting November 16. Admins and users wishing to opt out of having their public messages and channels included in web pages, and by extension, search results, will need to opt out before then.

#10349. 6 Tools for Building Email Automations You Can Test and Debug

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An email automation can fail even when every service reports success.

These are integration problems. They involve state, timing, retries, and ownership—familiar concerns for anyone building web applications.

This guide uses a hypothetical agency dashboard as a running example. The dashboard manages client workspaces, connects to an email platform, records incoming events, and shows operators whether an action is pending, completed, or blocked.

The six tools below address different parts of that workflow. Each section includes an engineering principle and a practical check to help you apply it.

Postman supports JavaScript assertions against API responses. Use it to establish how an integration behaves before connecting it to a production interface.

A successful request is only one part of the contract. Record response shapes, error formats, pagination behavior, and any documented rate limits.

For an application-owned endpoint that acknowledges queued work with HTTP 202, a post-response test could look like this:

This is an illustrative contract for your application, not a claim about an external provider's response format.

The interface should use the operation identifier to show progress. Displaying “completed” immediately after a 202 response would confuse acceptance with completion.

SitePoint’s introduction to building and testing APIs with Postman provides background on organizing requests and tests. Consult current documentation for the latest interface.

Try this: deliberately submit an invalid request and confirm that the dashboard displays a useful error without exposing credentials or raw internal responses.

The useful engineering question is how your application should represent and control these external operations.

Keep provider-specific calls behind an adapter. Application code might call methods such as pauseCampaign() or listReplies(), while the adapter handles authentication, endpoint paths, pagination, and response normalization.

These are application-defined methods, not an assumed vendor SDK.

Workspace boundaries deserve explicit tests. The documentation distinguishes workspace-specific api-user credentials from broader super-admin behavior. Select credentials deliberately, keep them server-side, and bind every operation to a trusted internal workspace mapping.

For webhook processing, use this sequence:

Confirm the available webhook authentication options before implementation; an HTTPS endpoint alone does not establish who sent a request.

Try this: use synthetic data in two test workspaces and confirm that neither credentials nor resource identifiers allow one workspace to read or modify the other.

Webhooks and workers should be designed for repeated delivery. A sender may retry because your acknowledgment was lost, even when the original event reached your application.

A database can enforce a durable deduplication boundary:

Use a stable provider event identifier when available. If none exists, define an event-specific key carefully. A contact ID alone is insufficient because the same contact can generate many legitimate events.

PostgreSQL's INSERT documentation explains conflict handling. The receiving application can insert an event with:

The placeholders are bound parameters supplied by the database driver. A returned row indicates a new insertion; no returned row means the key already exists.

This protects event storage from duplicates. It does not automatically make every downstream action execute exactly once.

A worker should apply local state changes and mark the event processed in one database transaction. If the action involves another service, record a durable outgoing operation and use the destination's documented idempotency mechanism where available.

Try this: deliver the same event twice, then restart the worker between attempts. Confirm there is one stored event and no repeated business action.

n8n provides error workflows for handling failed executions. This is useful when integrations combine several steps that operators need to inspect.

For example, a reply might require an application status update and a follow-up task for a human reviewer. Decide which step is authoritative and what should happen if only part of the workflow succeeds.

For this agency dashboard, a reply can pause further automated follow-ups until a person reviews the conversation. That is an explicit business rule, not a universal rule for every email system.

Try this: cause a downstream failure after an earlier step succeeds. Verify that recovery resumes safely instead of repeating the entire workflow blindly.

Mailpit supports captured-message inspection and API-based integration testing. Its testing features can also simulate unexpected SMTP responses.

Use it for emails that your application generates, such as workspace invitations or operational alerts. It does not automatically capture messages sent independently by a remote email platform; those need that platform's test facilities and controlled recipients.

For a workspace invitation, test the full path:

Avoid selecting an unfiltered “latest message” in parallel tests. Another test may have generated a newer message, causing a failure—or a misleading pass.

Keep production recipients and credentials out of test environments. Use unique synthetic addresses and test-only tokens.

SitePoint’s guide to sending email with Node.js offers related background on application-generated mail.

Try this: simulate SMTP failure and confirm the application records a pending or failed operation rather than claiming the invitation was delivered.

Playwright can test the dashboard behavior users depend on: submitting an action, observing progress, correcting errors, and inspecting results.

Define visible states before styling the interface. “Request received,” “waiting for provider,” “completed,” and “needs attention” convey different facts.

An illustrative test for a locally running dashboard might be:

This assumes a configured Playwright base URL and an application that implements the illustrated route, labels, and status region. It verifies presentation behavior against a mock response; a separate integration test must verify actual provider completion.

Designers can review these states alongside developers. Use clear text and accessible status announcements, rather than color alone, to communicate progress and errors.

Playwright's visual comparisons can also detect dashboard layout changes. Browser screenshots do not establish how an email renders in Outlook or Gmail.

SitePoint’s guide to testing Node.js APIs with Jest provides a useful companion for checking backend behavior independently of the interface.

Try this: test a slow response, an expired credential, and a completed operation at a narrow viewport. The next available action should remain clear in each state.

Before releasing an integration, demonstrate these properties:

Measure processing delay, retry counts, and unresolved operations. A high volume of successful HTTP requests can hide an accumulating backlog.

Also provide a controlled reconciliation path for missed events. Where the provider supports it, compare local state with the API using a durable cursor or carefully bounded time window. Passing reconciliation results through the same deduplicated processing path makes recovery easier to reason about.

The strongest automation is one the team can inspect and explain. Each tool should help answer a specific question about correctness, failure, or user experience—and every integration should have a tested route back to a known state.

#10348. New Zelda and GTA represent a games industry that no longer exists

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The two biggest games of the year launch in November, offering some reprieve from a terrible year.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The two biggest games of the year launch in November, offering some reprieve from a terrible year.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The two biggest games of 2026 are set to launch just a few weeks apart in November.

On the one side you have the behemoth that is Grand Theft Auto VI, an experience 13 years in the making, and the sequel to one of the best-selling games in history. Its predecessor, GTA V, has sold a staggering 230 million copies since it launched in 2013, and was re-released across three generations of consoles. Developer Rockstar is a pioneer in the realm of open-world games, dating back to GTA III, and the scale and ambitions of those worlds have steadily grown since then. With GTA V and later Red Dead Redemption 2, the studio’s ideas reached a new level of realism. And the sixth GTA looks poised to eclipse them both, which has created an unprecedented level of demand.

Analyst firm Circana estimates that 29 percent of gamers in the US intend to buy the game, with demand so large it could boost flagging console sales. “Many consumers who have yet to upgrade to a current-generation console may choose to purchase a PlayStation 5 or Xbox Series specifically to play the title,” analyst Mat Piscatella says in a statement. GTA VI’s reach is so vast that almost every other major game launched early to avoid it.

The one exception is Nintendo. The company’s tentpole release for the holidays, and its next major exclusive for the Switch 2, is a lavish remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time.

The release is notable for a few reasons. For one thing, it’s an attempt from Nintendo to modernize one of the most influential and beloved games in its long history. The original Ocarina of Time was the world’s first introduction to a 3D iteration of Hyrule, which, in turn, made it an early glimpse at what was possible in three-dimensional spaces, laying a blueprint for countless games that came afterward. Nintendo has since expanded on that, turning Switch-era adventures Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom into wide-open adventures designed for player creativity. They went on to become some of the best-selling games for Nintendo’s most successful console.

Ocarina of Time’s launch also comes just ahead of a live-action Zelda movie in 2027, which could catapult the franchise to a new level of prominence. That’s why Nintendo is seemingly the only company unafraid of competing with GTA.

These aren’t just two blockbuster games – they’re two series with the pull to make this a real cultural moment, akin to “Barbenheimer” but for video games. They’re the kind of launches that make it onto Saturday Night Live and have NBA players worried.

It’s just too bad about the rest of the industry.

These major November releases are something of a mirage, two outliers that stand out amid the wreckage that is the rest of the video game industry. While there have been plenty of major releases and great games this year, the main theme of 2026 has been just how dire things have become. Layoffs, studio closures, unpopular business models, the ever-looming threat of AI, and massive increases in hardware costs have combined to create an ominous cloud over the medium.

Here is just a snapshot of what the year has been like: Xbox is in the middle of a “reset” that involves laying off thousands of developers, shutting down some studios, and gutting others; Fortnite developer Epic cut more than 1,000 jobs due to a sharp downturn in the live-service games space, and it’s currently going all-in on vibe coding; Sony is also struggling, slashing jobs at Destiny developer Bungie, watching console sales plummet as costs skyrocket, and raising the ire of gamers by moving on from physical media; and meanwhile, major publishers like EA and Ubisoft are in their own dramatic states of upheaval.

And the bad news hasn’t really let up. Just last week, EA shut down beloved studio BioWare and canceled the next Mass Effect game, and this week Sony studio Sucker Punch cut staff despite the success of last year’s Ghost of Yōtei. Every day, it seems, brings some new unwelcome development.

Clearly, things are changing for the video game industry; they have to change. What that future looks like, though, remains unclear. The next generation of game consoles is being threatened by exorbitant prices, and all the while the rise of AI-powered tools is sending ripples across the industry, threatening the established order between players and creators as games are ported to every platform imaginable. “These are unmistakably good outcomes for players, even when (and sometimes because) they challenge our existing business models,” Xbox CEO Asha Sharma said in a recent memo. But how that plays out remains to be seen.

It’s not all doom and gloom. There have been rare success stories, where studios like FromSoftware and Remedy have managed to carve out a sustainable existence in a turbulent field. But for the most part, the established norms that have been part of the blockbuster games industry for years are going away. Big games are too expensive to make, hardware is too expensive to buy (and the cost is only going up), and somewhere, something has to give.

It’s tempting to look at Zelda and GTA as answers to these problems. After all, if Nintendo and Rockstar are doing so well, there must be a solution out there. But they’re also two very specific cases that are essentially impossible to replicate. Nintendo maintains ownership over all aspects of its platform, from the hardware to the games, and it has taken a careful and largely risk-averse approach that has allowed it to weather the current storm and create a growing entertainment empire. Rockstar, meanwhile, is the rare example of being too big to fail. GTA V was so massive for so long — Circana says it was among the top 20 best-selling games in the US for 13 straight years — that it allowed the studio to build up both an unprecedented amount of anticipation and, it seems, a game big enough to match it.

In that way, Zelda and GTA are throwbacks to a time when major games were sure bets. Their duelling releases are fascinating both because these games are so impactful, but also because, with all of the impending upheaval, the industry might never see a moment like this again.

#10347. RAMageddon is ready to rage on through 2030

SK Hynix, one of the major companies behind memory production, says that it’s sold out through 2029, leaving RAMageddon ready to rage on for at least the next three years.

On its latest earnings call today, SK Hynix confirmed that production of both DRAM and HBM (high-bandwidth memory) are effectively sold out not just for this year, not just for next year, but through 2029. That puts RAMageddon, the global shortage of memory, lasting at least another three years before we see any relief.

SK Hynix’s CFO explains (via The Motley Fool, Seeking Alpha):

…memory supply cannot keep pace with this level of demand growth, thus, we expect it to be difficult to fully meet customers’ volume requirements in the near term, given the complexities involved in implementing leading-edge processes and the lead times required to construct new production facilities. A situation where supply cannot keep up with demand is expected to persist for some time, a constraint projected to continue through 2030.

Micron, one of the other companies responsible for memory production, previously said to expect 2027 to be “even tighter” compared to 2026.

The SK Hynix call also details how the company is making long-term agreements which already account for “more than 50% of our total capacity for 2027 and 2028.”

SK Hynix isn’t the biggest name in DRAM/HBM production, with Samsung accounting for a larger share, but everyone else is dealing with the same constraints. PCMag points out that SK Hynix is working to build out more production capacity, as everyone else is too, but it takes time to build out the complex infrastructure.

Buckle up folks, this isn’t ending anytime soon. 🫠

#10345. A fully playable version of Portal prequel F-Stop leaked

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Nearly 20 years after it was canceled, the camera-based Portal prequel F-Stop is no longer lost media.

Nearly 20 years after it was canceled, the camera-based Portal prequel F-Stop is no longer lost media.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Leakers have pieced together a playable version of the canceled Portal prequel F-Stop. Most of the assets came from the massive 12-terabyte leak that dropped this August. That didn’t include the game’s source code or map data, but those final pieces apparently hit the Valve Cut Content Discord this week, allowing a nearly complete version to be pieced together, according to Kotaku.

Some dialog and elements of F-Stop eventually found their way into Portal 2. But it differs dramatically from the mainline Portal games in that it’s built around a camera mechanic that lets you capture an item by taking its picture and place it elsewhere using the resulting Polaroid-like photos. It’s not unlike Viewfinder in that way.

The game is short, with videos showing off its full campaign all clocking in at under an hour. That’s not terribly surprising, though. F-Stop was developed as part of the “Directed Design Experiments,” which, according to The Half-Life & Portal Encyclopedia, was a three-month period in 2007 when Valve broke into five teams to build new video game experiments.

Update November 2nd: Added attribution to Kotaku and The Half-Life & Portal Encyclopedia.

#10341. Best AI Recruiting Agents in 2026: 6 Compared

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Short answer: Pin (pin.com) covers sourcing through scheduling with approvals you control at every step. Pin says it searches 850M+ profiles and assists recruiters with ranking, outreach, follow-up, scheduling and ATS updates. You approve each step (Assist) or delegate the sequence (Autopilot), and Pin logs every action. Closing and offers stay human.

Be skeptical. Gartner estimated in June 2025 that only about 130 of the thousands of vendors marketing "agentic AI" are real. Test any product on a live role before you pay.

An AI recruiting agent is software that takes a hiring goal, such as "three senior backend engineers in Austin," and works through several steps toward it on its own: finding candidates, ranking them, contacting them, following up and booking interviews. A recruiter sets the brief and the limits and makes the final decisions. The agent assists the recruiter; it does not replace one. See Pin's guide to AI recruiting agents.

It differs from three things sold under the same name:

A quick test: if you stop prompting it, does the search keep moving? If not, it is a tool.

Demand is rising: in Korn Ferry's October 2025 survey of 1,674 talent leaders, 52% plan to add autonomous agents to their teams in 2026.

Compliance. The employer carries the legal duty. New York City's Local Law 144 requires a bias audit within one year before an automated employment decision tool is used, a public summary of results, and candidate notice at least 10 business days before use. The EU AI Act's Annex III classes AI that filters applications or evaluates candidates as high-risk, and the Commission's timeline puts those obligations at December 2, 2027. Ask each vendor whether its ranking is covered, whether a bias audit exists and what logs it keeps. Pin feeds no names, gender or protected characteristics to its AI, uses third-party fairness audits, and lists an Algorithmic Fairness Report (August 2026) in its trust center on request. This is not legal advice.

Pin (pin.com) is an AI recruiting platform with a built-in agent that assists recruiters across the top of the recruiting funnel, from sourcing to interview scheduling.

Sourcing. Pin says it searches 850M+ profiles across multiple sources, including North America and Europe, drawn from professional networks, GitHub, Stack Overflow, open-source contributions, patents, academic publications and the broader web. Start from a plain-language brief, a pasted job description, uploaded ideal résumés or an ATS import. Results arrive as a ranked shortlist, each candidate with a fit score and an AI-written reason. Pin asks clarifying questions about the brief, such as "Is remote considered?" Learn more about AI candidate sourcing.

Assist and Autopilot. In Assist, you delegate tasks one at a time and approve each step. In Autopilot, the agent takes the whole sequence.

In Autopilot, only the email steps send automatically. Pin queues each LinkedIn and SMS step as a task with an AI-drafted message, and the recruiter sends it.

Visibility and learning. Every action appears in a feed, and the agent runs 24/7. Pin's agent page describes an audit log with reasoning, a pause control, approval thresholds for sensitive cases and exclusion lists for past contacts and current employees. The model learns from accept and reject decisions across your team.

Outreach and scheduling. Pin drafts each message from the candidate's profile signals, calibrated to your writing voice. Sequences are multi-channel across email, LinkedIn and SMS: automated email steps plus AI-drafted LinkedIn and SMS tasks in the same sequence. Any reply pauses the sequence, and every channel stays in one thread in a shared team inbox. The Free plan is email-only. Interview scheduling covers time zones, hiring panels and reschedules, with confirmations in your ATS. Teams without an ATS can track candidates in Pin's Kanban pipeline from first touch to signed offer, and teams with one sync through 120+ integrations, including Greenhouse, Lever and Ashby.

MCP server. The Pin MCP server lets an assistant such as Claude or ChatGPT work a Pin job in plain language through ten tools. Accepting a candidate starts outreach from your configured sequence, which Pin confirms every time. It steers Pin from your assistant and is not a separate agent.

Pricing (checked October 14, 2026, on the pricing page): Free is $0 with 1 AI agent, 5 contact lookup credits a week included and email-only sequences. Solo is $99 per month for one seat, billed annually. Professional is $135 per user per month billed annually, or $179 month-to-month. Business is custom and includes SSO/SAML, SCIM and premium ATS integrations. Pin holds SOC 2 Type 2, encrypts data at rest and in transit, and is GDPR and CCPA ready.

Results. According to Pin's 2026 user survey across 2,000+ organizations and 20,000+ users, Pin saves each recruiter 12 hours per week on sourcing and outreach (vendor-reported). Pin is rated 4.8/5 on G2. Josh Volinsky, Co-Founder of Synergy Search, says: "Pin makes sourcing candidates so natural and I love how you can just talk to it like it's a recruiter on your team and it goes hunting."

Where Pin is not the right fit. Teams that only need high-volume screening of inbound applicants are better served by a tool like Paradox.

Best for: recruiting teams, agencies and founders who want one agent across the top of the funnel, with approvals they control.

GoPerfect's Perfect is an AI recruiter that researches a role, sources and contacts candidates, and can run the first phone screen.

It costs $300 per open position per month with unlimited seats, so five open roles run $1,500 a month. No free trial is listed. Its pricing page (October 2026) lists autopilot sourcing, email and LinkedIn outreach, AI phone calls, AI first interviews, 60+ ATS syncs, SOC 2 Type II, approval mode, do-not-contact lists and an audit log. Its site also shows a "Send without approval" option, so control depends on setup.

Good for: teams with a few priority roles that want AI to handle first-round calls. The caveat: cost grows with every open role.

LinkedIn Hiring Assistant is LinkedIn's AI agent for recruiters, sold as an add-on to LinkedIn Recruiter.

According to LinkedIn's product page, the agent covers intake questions, searches across LinkedIn, evaluation of applicants from LinkedIn and your ATS, drafted messages and pre-screening. Interview scheduling appears only under Hiring Assistant 2, marked "Coming soon." Pricing is not published, and a Recruiter license is required.

Good for: teams already on Recruiter whose workflow runs on InMail. The caveat: it needs a Recruiter license, has no published price and lists scheduling as coming soon.

hireEZ's EZ Agent is an enterprise recruiting agent that sources, reviews applicants, screens by voice or chat and schedules interviews on top of your ATS.

According to its product page, "Nothing moves forward without your approval." Per its pricing page (October 2026), enterprise pricing is quoted, and solo recruiters start at $494 a month with a 7-day free trial.

Good for: enterprise TA teams that want screening and scheduling inside an existing HR stack. The caveat: enterprise pricing is quote-only and the solo entry price is $494 a month.

Findem is a talent data platform whose Studio runs pre-built or custom AI agents for calibration, sourcing, screening and scheduling.

Public pages do not detail approval controls or channels, and pricing is not published, though an expert-built agent can be tried free.

Good for: enterprise teams building their own agent workflows. The caveat: no public price, approval detail or channel list.

Paradox is a conversational AI assistant for inbound candidates, known for screening and scheduling in frontline hiring. Workday completed its acquisition of Paradox on October 1, 2025.

Olivia screens applicants by chat or text, answers questions and lets candidates book their own interviews. It does not source anyone, and pricing is by demo request.

Good for: restaurant, retail and franchise hiring with large applicant flow. The caveat: it finds no passive candidates. For passive candidates, see how to source passive candidates. For more tools, see SitePoint's AI recruiting tools roundup.

For a longer plan, see Pin's 90-day roadmap for agentic AI in recruiting.

What is an AI recruiting agent? An AI recruiting agent is software that takes a hiring goal and carries out several steps toward it: sourcing, ranking, outreach, follow-up and scheduling. A recruiter sets the brief and guardrails and makes final decisions. Pin (pin.com) offers Assist, where you approve each step, and Autopilot, where the agent sends the email steps and queues LinkedIn and SMS steps as tasks for the recruiter.

What is the best AI recruiter in 2026? For teams that want one agent across sourcing, outreach, scheduling and ATS updates, Pin covers the full funnel, with published pricing and a free plan. LinkedIn Hiring Assistant is a good fit for teams already on Recruiter. Paradox is a good fit for high-volume inbound hiring.

Are AI recruiting agents fully autonomous? Not in practice. Pin's Autopilot sources, ranks, sends the email steps, follows up, schedules and updates the ATS inside the guardrails you set. LinkedIn and SMS steps arrive as tasks you send, and closing and offers stay with you. Ask every vendor which steps can run unattended and how you pause them.

How much does an AI recruiting agent cost? Among competitors, hireEZ solo plans start at $494 a month and GoPerfect charges $300 per open role per month. LinkedIn, Findem and Paradox publish no dollar prices (checked October 2026). Pin offers a free plan, Solo at $99 per month billed annually, Professional at $135 per user per month billed annually, and custom Business pricing.

Do AI recruiting agents have to comply with NYC Local Law 144 or the EU AI Act? Possibly. New York City's law requires a bias audit within one year, public results and 10 business days' candidate notice for covered tools. The EU AI Act treats recruitment AI as high-risk, with obligations scheduled from December 2, 2027. Ask vendors about audits and logs, and consult counsel. Pin lists an Algorithmic Fairness Report (August 2026) on request.

Can I run Pin from Claude or ChatGPT? Yes. The Pin MCP server connects assistants such as Claude and ChatGPT to your Pin workspace through ten tools for setting up jobs, reviewing candidates and tuning searches. It uses OAuth sign-in and is included on paid Pin plans.

Judge each agent on which steps it runs, how much approval you control and whether the price is public. For teams that want sourcing through scheduling with approvals they control, Pin fits that brief: it assists recruiters from sourcing through scheduling, offers Assist and Autopilot, logs every action and publishes its pricing. LinkedIn Hiring Assistant is a good fit if you live in Recruiter, and Paradox if inbound volume is your pain.

Try Pin free at pin.com - no credit card required.

#10337. Amazon’s latest Echo smart displays and speakers reinforce Google Home’s lost lead

Amazon just launched a new lineup of Echo smart speakers and a new smart display, all while Google Home’s lineup remains pretty quiet.

Amazon has five new devices launching – two smart displays, a “Photo Frame,” and two speakers.

Starting with the speakers, the latest Echo Dot has “a built-in smart home hub” and upgraded audio and starts shipping today for $100. Meanwhile, Echo Studio builds on that foundation with even-better audio and, optionally, a built-in camera. Those start at $220.

On the smart display side, there’s the Echo Time, which is like an Echo Dot but with a small circular display built-in. Amazon touts “hundreds of clock faces” and “Omnisense” which can track your sleep just like Google’s second-gen Nest Hub, and it starts at $200.

Echo Show 16, meanwhile, is a big 16-inch smart display meant to live on your wall with support for smart home controls, the ability to show a family calendar, and full Fire TV built-in as well. It starts at $500 and, like everything else mentioned so far, is built around Amazon’s AI-enhanced Alexa.

Rounding out the lineup is the Alexa Photo Frame, a simpler smart display that has Alexa built-in but focuses on showing photos from your Amazon Photos library. It has a 10-inch anti-glare display and a design that looks like a normal photo frame and starts at $200.

Google Home really is just so wildly behind isn’t it.

In looking at Amazon’s lineup, I can’t help but feel like Google is just wasting potential here. A smart speaker that doubles as a Nest Cam would be awesome. The same goes for a giant Nest Hub that doubles as a Google TV.

I still don't understand how nobody is questioning Google when they still don't support basics stuff like:

casting from the phone using a "video" app (YouTube, Netflix, etc) to Google Cast speakers -> AirPlay has supported this for a decade

being able to set Google Cast speakers as audio output devices of Google TV devices (and not the extremely limited - and frankly, basically useless - implementation that there's only between Google TV Streamer and the new Google Home Speaker) -> both Amazon and Apple (as well as many competitors) have supported this for several years, including with with surround setups (4 speakers)

Their latest Google Home Speaker was a major disappointment. I almost have no doubt that a new Google Home Hub and a new Google TV Streamer will be disappointments too.

It's years that I keep wishing for Google to prove me wrong, but all they do is keep digging themselves deeper in their grave.

And, seriously, how does Google not sell (or at least partner with someone) a Google Photos digital photo frame. Google Photos is a wildly successful platform and that would sell like hotcakes. Big “how am I not in that movie” energy.

I’ve said it before, but Google Home had such a lead in the smart speaker and smart display market, especially with Google’s ecosystem tie-ins, but Amazon is currently running laps around it with products that Google should be selling. Throw in that even Apple has a smart display now where Google hasn’t even teased a new Nest Hub, and it just feels all kinds of bad to be in Google’s camp right now.

#10334. XREAL Aura is coming in November with a special $200-off launch discount

The first Android XR-powered smart glasses are almost here, and early adopters actually get a rare discount on the cutting-edge XREAL Aura hardware.

XREAL Aura is set to redefine what a “VR headset” can be by combining the form factor of XREAL display glasses with a small compute puck that clips to your waistband. XREAL and Google have teased Project Aura for months, announcing the official name and price last month, and now XREAL just unveiled the special launch price and release date on stage at Google’s Android Developer Summit.

The special glasses launch in mid-November, with preorders starting today, October 28, at Xreal.com and Best Buy. XREAL Aura will initially be available in the United States, Canada, Japan, and South Korea (with a broader rollout in 2027) and is coming in two SKUs:

Both SKUs are available in standard and large sizes and come with an included carrying case that houses the glasses, compute puck, and cable. The cable is user-replaceable and uses a standard USB-C connection for longevity.

XREAL Aura will be launching alongside 150 made-for-XR experiences designed to showcase the power and capability of the new form factor. I got to use a near-final unit last month and was incredibly impressed with how XREAL and Google have been able to pull together the full Android XR experience on a pair of smart glasses, including full 6DoF spatial tracking and support for hand tracking and controllers.

XREAL Aura attaches via a USB-C cable to a compute puck, which is roughly the size of a phone. This has a generously sized trackpad on the front and a detachable belt clip on the back. The battery and Snapdragon Reality Elite chipset are inside the puck, taking the glasses’ weight down to just 95g (compared to ~500g for a VR headset).

XREAL and GameSir have partnered to deliver a new game controller built specifically for XREAL Aura (and Android XR). This one sports a prominent Gemini button that gives immediate access to Gemini’s helpful gaming capabilities (it’s actually more helpful than you might initially imagine). I also used this controller last month and found it to be wonderful. It feels similar to an Xbox Elite controller, but it is not spatially tracked.

A wireless portable keyboard is also on the way for productivity and multi-window workflows, but you can use any mouse and keyboard you’d like with Aura. XREAL Aura also supports wired mirroring, so you can plug your laptop or gaming console directly into the compute puck and add a spatially-tracked window to your view.

Preorders are open now and, of course, you’ll save $200 if you order XREAL Aura before the end of the year.

#10332. Google adding more Connected Apps to Gemini & Search with new dev platform

Google today announced the Connected Apps Platform as a “unified way for more app developers to bring their capabilities directly into Gemini and Search.”

Existing third-party Connected Apps let you access Adobe, Dropbox, Instacart, OpenTable, Ticketmaster, and other services — now numbering over 50 — from the Gemini app. Google also announced a new Calm integration that will be available in the coming weeks. These @-integrations join first-party offerings like Google Workspace, Photos, and YouTube. To date, the creation process has required working closely with Google.

Google is now expanding availability on multiple fronts, starting with the Connected Apps Platform. It’s billed as a way to “build once and reach high-intent users.” Developers need to have a cloud-hosted MCP server or Android MCP using the AppFunctions APIs. They then register, test, and submit their Connected Apps using the new Google Connected Console. This new website lets them track traffic, latency, and error rates, as well as control brand identity.

In addition to the Gemini app, Google will soon make existing Connected Apps available in Search. Developers can “choose whether [their app] will go live in Search, the Gemini app, or both.” As part of the recent redesign, the Gemini app gained a new Customize page.

Developers can sign up for the preview at developers.google.com/connected-apps. Google is opening up access in phases over the coming months.

#10331. Intel’s woes wipe out $1 trillion of semiconductor market cap in one day

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

$1 trillion in semiconductor market capitalization wiped out.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Intel's shares plunged 30% on Friday, wiping out nearly $150 billion in market capitalization in a single day, the largest single-day loss in the company's history. The decline followed a disappointing financial outlook, largely driven by an anticipated drop of more than 20% in sales at Intel's client computing group (CCPG), the company's main cash cow. The declines quickly spread across the semiconductor industry. Although losses elsewhere were nowhere near as severe as Intel's, approximately $1 trillion in semiconductor market capitalization was wiped out on Friday, according to intraday estimates by Benzinga.

The PHLX Semiconductor Sector Index (SOX), which tracks the stock performance of 30 major semiconductor companies, is one of the key indicators of the industry's financial health. On Friday, the index plunged 9.14%, or 1,052.82 points, to 10,465.38 points as of 2:31 PM ET, as Intel's disappointing outlook triggered a massive selloff across the semiconductor industry.

Intel was by far the biggest loser, as other chip-related stocks, while Arm (-12.68%), AMD (-12.48%), Qualcomm (-12.14%), Applied Materials (-8.73%), ASML (-8.13%), Lam Research (-8%), Micron (-7.28%), and Broadcom (-7.1%) suffered considerably less. Then again, while Nvidia lost 5.66% of its market cap, this translates to around $270 billion, which is a massive amount of money. Paradoxically, Nvidia lost more market capitalization in absolute dollar terms than Intel.

In an unexpected turn of events, the downturn extended to server manufacturers and cloud giants too, as Dell (-12.67%), HPE (-8.10%), Supermicro (-6.96%), and Oracle (-6.88%) lost nearly 7% to 13% of their value. Reuters noted that investors increasingly question the scale of capital investments required to expand AI infrastructure as well as the sustainability of that spending.

Intel’s client computing and physical AI group (CCPG) generated $8.1 billion in revenue during Q3 2026, down 5% year-over-year and 9% sequentially. Weaker demand and increased competition reduced client processor shipments by 14% year-over-year, which was partially offset by a 9% increase in average selling prices. CCPG accounted for nearly half of Intel's revenue of around $16.5 billion and the company's main source of cash. As strong demand for server processors only partially offsets declining client sales, Intel guided its Q4 revenue to be between $15 billion and $16 billion.

Intel expects the client market to deteriorate further in Q4 2026 and into 2027 as elevated memory prices and shortages of other components impact PC prices and demand. In general, Intel expects the client PC market to decline 10% in 2027 compared to 2026, which will inevitably have an effect on CCPG performance. While Intel expects its data center business to continue growing in 2027, industry-wide supply constraints will limit its ability to fully capitalize on demand.

2026 has been a mixed bag for Intel so far. On the one hand, the company achieved its highest market capitalization ever — $673.43 billion in mid-June — on growing sales of data center CPUs, strong sales of client products, and a return to profitability (if the $12.5 billion non-cash accounting charge associated with shares issued to the U.S. government is excluded). On the other hand, the company's capitalization has dropped by nearly 50% to $338.91 billion since mid-June (with $148.68 billion in just one day), as investors began to realize that the performance of Intel's CCPG heavily depends on memory prices, as customers and businesses tend not to like overpriced systems. Yet on the other hand, now that Intel sells fewer client processors, it can sell more expensive data center CPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Surprisingly, Apple (-1.66%), which only sells client products, was nearly unaffected by the performance of Intel's client computing group, as investors probably believe that the world's largest supplier of consumer electronics is more immune to memory prices than Intel. Ironically, if we count the A-series SoCs used in iPhones (which tend to outperform mainstream laptop CPUs), Apple sells substantially more processors than Intel, making Apple by far the world's largest supplier of microprocessors. Despite this, Apple's shares were largely unaffected by the semiconductor selloff.

There are several reasons for this distinction. Firstly, Apple's business model is fundamentally different. Intel sells processors to PC manufacturers, whereas Apple designs its own processors and sells them as part of finished products. Consequently, Apple captures revenue from the entire device rather than just the processor. Secondly, the two companies face different demand dynamics. Intel depends heavily on the PC market, while Apple's iPhone business follows the smartphone replacement cycle. Rising memory prices could affect both, but not necessarily to the same extent and at the same time.

There is an additional irony: Apple is also a major customer of TSMC, so its processor supply chain overlaps with those of AMD, Intel, and other semiconductor companies whose shares declined sharply.

While we should not directly compare Apple and Intel, the comparison illustrates that investors were not necessarily abandoning companies that design or use semiconductors, but were rather concerned about the profitability and growth prospects of these high-tech companies. And if we take a look at the situation from this point of view, the whole thing will start looking completely different.

#10329. Building Location-Aware Interfaces Without Mixing Up Language, Currency, and Region

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A visitor opens an online store while traveling. Their browser uses English, their account is registered in Canada, their network appears to be in Germany, and their delivery address is in France.

Which currency should the store show? Which products are available? What language should the checkout use?

There is no single answer hidden inside a country code. These decisions depend on different signals and different product rules.

For developers and designers, the challenge is to represent that context explicitly. Otherwise, a convenient shortcut—such as deriving everything from an IP address—can produce confusing prices, unexpected redirects, and incorrect availability.

This article walks through a practical approach to building a regional product interface, with examples that also apply to travel sites, service directories, and applications that retrieve current web information.

Avoid a single variable named country that silently controls every aspect of the interface.

These values can disagree without any of them being wrong.

For an anonymous visitor, network region might help suggest an initial market. Once the user selects a destination, the application should not silently replace it because a later request arrives from a different network.

Write precedence rules for each decision. Language selection and delivery eligibility need separate policies.

SitePoint’s introduction to JavaScript internationalization provides useful background on separating software capabilities from locale-specific adaptation.

This is an illustrative application contract. It keeps the observation's market, currency, and timestamp attached to the value.

Without that context, another component might reuse the amount for a different market or label it as current long after availability changes.

At the API boundary, validate the required fields and their allowed values. A missing amount must not become zero, and a missing availability value must not become “in stock.”

Also define the timestamp precisely. Does it mean the source was fetched, the source published an update, or your database last processed the record? Those are different events.

For the interface, use wording consistent with the evidence: “Checked at 09:00” is more precise than “Live availability” when you only have a timestamped observation.

Browser preferences and network location operate at different layers.

Changing the browser locale can affect language preferences and formatting. It does not relocate the connection seen by the server. Similarly, supplying browser geolocation coordinates does not change the network's public IP address.

This distinction also matters in live-web retrieval. Bixberry describes an opt-in network that routes requests through participants' internet connections. It illustrates how retrieval infrastructure can involve connections outside a conventional data-center environment.

When comparing two responses, record the relevant conditions:

Keep diagnostic records minimal and avoid retaining unnecessary personal information.

For authorized regional testing of your own application, SitePoint’s guide to testing geo-dependent web apps offers related background on separating browser settings from network behavior.

JavaScript's Intl.NumberFormat formats numeric values according to locale and currency settings:

Both calls represent the same euro amount. Formatting it for a different locale does not convert it to another currency.

Do not turn a euro price into a dollar price by changing only the currency argument. A conversion requires a defined exchange-rate source, timestamp, rounding policy, and explanation of whether the result is an estimate or the actual charged amount.

For calculations, choose a representation appropriate to your pricing model rather than relying on floating-point arithmetic throughout. If using integer minor units, remember that currencies do not all use two decimal places.

SitePoint’s overview of the JavaScript Internationalization API explains related formatting capabilities.

Practical check: switch interface language while leaving the selected market unchanged. The number format may change, but the underlying price and currency should remain consistent with the product rules.

A country flag is a weak substitute for a language label. Countries can have multiple languages, and the same language can be used across many countries.

Use clear, separate controls when the product needs them:

Do not expose a control unless its effect is meaningful and implemented consistently. A currency selector that updates a price label but leaves checkout charging behavior unexplained creates confusion.

Before a market change, explain consequential effects. For example:

Changing your delivery country may update availability and delivery options.

Preserve the user's task where possible. If a product is unavailable in the newly selected market, retain its page and show an explanation instead of sending the user to an unrelated homepage.

For designers, test longer translations, currency codes, and expanded validation messages. Use logical CSS properties where appropriate so layouts can adapt to right-to-left content:

These rules are a starting point, not a complete localization solution. Review reading order, icons, numeric content, and translated copy with people familiar with the target language.

A regional interface can behave correctly at the origin and still show incorrect content when a shared cache serves the wrong variant.

If a response varies by market or language, that variation must be reflected in your cache design. One option for public content is an explicit route structure:

These routes are illustrative. They make market and language differences visible, but the rest of the cache configuration must still match the application's behavior.

Personalized prices and account-specific responses require their own cache policy. A public page cache must not expose one account's content to another.

Test the deployed caching layer, not only a local development server:

Keep freshness requirements separate from variant separation. A response can belong to the correct market and still be too old.

Playwright's emulation settings let tests configure locale, time zone, geolocation, and permissions.

This assumes a configured base URL and an application implementing the illustrated route and selectors. It tests the application's handling of explicit market selection under a different locale; it does not prove that the request originated in Germany.

Use controlled API fixtures for predictable frontend tests, then test real routing and caching separately. Browser emulation is one layer of verification, not a substitute for every regional check.

A location-aware application should be able to explain why it chose a language, price, destination, or availability result.

Keep those decisions separate, preserve the context of retrieved data, and make explicit user choices easy to change. Then test the situations where signals disagree.

The result is an interface that works for travelers, multilingual households, remote workers, and anyone whose preferences cannot be reduced to the location of their current internet connection.
