Out-of-control pricing and soon completely out of stock

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

First, it was the GeForce RTX 5090, and now the GeForce RTX 5080 – two of the best graphics cards have gone dark. After the Blackwell flagship left Nvidia's marketplace, the GeForce RTX 5080 followed suit. Prices for the sub-flagship have exploded to more than 2.2X its original $999 MSRP, and the Blackwell-powered graphics card is slowly going out of stock at various U.S. retailers.

As with the recent disappearance of the GeForce RTX 5090 on Nvidia’s marketplace, the listings and filters for the GeForce RTX 5080 have also disappeared. While its absence does not necessarily mean Nvidia has discontinued the graphics card, it does raise questions about its status and future availability. This move is especially fishy because other Blackwell SKUs, such as the GeForce RTX 5070 Ti and GeForce RTX 5070, remain listed on the marketplace, albeit marked as “Out of Stock.”

The continued presence of the GeForce RTX 5070 Ti and GeForce RTX 5070 suggests that Nvidia wants to keep the products visible even when they are not immediately available so that consumers can anticipate restocks. The complete removal of the GeForce RTX 5090 and GeForce RTX 5080 breaks that pattern. It fuels rumors that Nvidia has allegedly stopped production of the GeForce RTX 5090 and possibly the GeForce RTX 5080 too, in favor of a rumored GeForce RTX 5080 24GB.

Rumors claim Nvidia allegedly stopped producing the GeForce RTX 5090 to repurpose the valuable GB202 silicon into the chipmaker’s RTX Pro Blackwell lineup, which fetches higher margins. This has a lot of credence, since many agree that Nvidia stopped being a gaming hardware vendor long ago and has transformed into an AI provider.

Rather than simply discontinuing the GeForce RTX 5090, Nvidia purportedly intends to introduce the GeForce RTX 5080 24GB as the new flagship gaming graphics card. The thing is, a 24GB variant of the GeForce RTX 5080 would solve one of the biggest consumer gripes with the graphics card: the lack of memory. While a GeForce RTX 5080 24GB would offer the same memory as the GeForce RTX 5090, it simply isn't a direct drop-in replacement. The former has 2X fewer CUDA cores, a substantial performance gap that even a 50% memory increase can't make up for.

The GeForce RTX 5080 launched in January of last year with an MSRP of $999. Demand quickly outpaced supply, and with the rapid price hikes due to the memory shortage, the market price has more than doubled its original MSRP. As of this moment, the most affordable GeForce RTX 5080 in stock, the PNY GeForce RTX 5080 ARGB Epic-X RGB OC, retails for $2,199.99, which is 2.2X over the original launch price.

While some stock remains, it’s selling out fast, and the remaining units will only get more expensive as availability dwindles. At this rate, the GeForce RTX 5080 is unlikely to be available by Black Friday. If you are set on a GeForce RTX 5080, you may have little choice but to act quickly and buy at today's elevated prices.

However, if you cheat and enable 16-bit apps in Win11, you can still enjoy REVERSI.EXE, as distributed with Windows 1.01 in 1985.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A technology enthusiast claims that the oldest GUI application that still runs on Windows 11 is CDPLAYER.EXE from November 1992. The app had to run on Microsoft’s newest consumer OS without modifications or emulation involved to be chosen as the ‘winner’ of their experiment. Blogger Enlyth expected something from the early NT era, as Windows 11 doesn’t support 16-bit apps, despite its strong backward compatibility. Finding an app that is almost 34 years old that still works with a simple double-click is a remarkable result.

At the beginning of their journey to find the oldest Windows UI app that just works on Microsoft’s newest OS, Enlyth confirmed FREECELL.EXE from February 1993 would still run without issues. This game was distributed alongside the Win32s environment, designed to allow some 32-bit applications to run on the 16-bit operating systems like Windows 3.1 and 3.11. Win32s was made available in beta in October 1992, but went mainstream in July 1993.

A tip Enlyth received via a tech forum suggested that FREECELL.EXE was a candidate for the oldest GUI app remaining compatible with Windows 11. However, the blogger wasn’t satisfied with this easy win and dug through “some early NT 3.1 pre-release versions that seemed promising.” Build 347 caught the detective’s eye, specifically CDPLAYER.EXE, linked on Nov 16, 1992. That indeed seems to be the earliest GUI app that works without modification or emulation on Windows 11. But this knowledge almost defied Enlyth’s eye as their system is, like many modern systems, optical-drive-less. Luckily, adding a small DLL (cdemu.dll) allowed Enlyth to emulate a CD drive and demonstrate that the 1992-era application still functions as intended.

I wanted to verify that CDPLAYER.EXE was still usable on the latest Windows 11 release. Unfortunately, the multi-part.ARJ archive of Windows NT 3.1 that I found on Archive.org threw up errors when I tried to pick through its contents. I have a USB-attached optical drive that plays CD audio in Windows 11, so I thought I could get CDPLAYER.EXE to run without any extra DLL.

Lastly, Enlyth wanted to push the envelope a little and see what even more ancient Windows apps might work if they got some help from the Winevdm project on 64-bit Windows. “Using otvdm does feel like cheating,” admitted the digital sleuth, and we’d agree. However, being able to run 16-bit apps in Windows 11 ratcheted back the wheels of time. The result was that Enlyth managed to enjoy “a fully playable version of REVERSI.EXE dated 15-Nov-1985.” That’s a GUI-driven game distributed as part of Windows 1.01 and is roughly 41 years old.

#10319. 3D-printed ghost guns may now have a way to be traced to their source

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

It only tests for PLA, but it's a starting point.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A study published on October 22 in the journal Forensic Science International reports that researchers from Curtin University in Perth, Australia, have developed a forensic method to analyze 3D-printed PLA parts and match them to their respective filament, correctly matching 26 out of 28 samples of PLA. This potentially provides a way to link seized 3D-printed “ghost-guns” with other seized filament, allowing police to form a clearer network for the manufacturing and distribution of the 3D-printed firearms.

3D-printed firearms have become a big problem worldwide, as they are untraceable and even compostable in the case of PLA, with tools like this AI 3D-printed gun generator making it scarily accessible. Serial numbers, registration, and a network of licensed distributors are usually the first line of investigation when it comes to crime involving firearms, but easily manufacturable, untraceable, disposable plastic firearms are a new threat with no clear way to crack down on them yet.

However, this research, by Dr. Georgina Sauzier et al. from Curtin University's School of Molecular and Life Sciences, along with forensics experts from ChemCentre, has devised a way to trace a printed object to a specific filament product, down to the brand and type.

The process of 3D printing changes the filament’s physical properties, and this change can be measured. In this research, differential scanning calorimetry (DSC) is used, a thermoanalytical technique that lets the sample go through a range of temperatures, and the instrument measures how heat flows through the sample. It measures how much energy or power is used to keep the sample at a certain temperature or to change its temperature, giving us a graph of its thermal properties.

The sample’s thermal properties can then be cross-referenced with its unprinted filament, creating an association between them. As of now, the tested PLA products are the only successfully identified material, with PETG and ABS not providing enough information to link them to their filament product. Sauzier blames the amorphous nature of those filaments, giving very similar results with no clear identifiers linking them to their respective filament products. PLA has a semi-crystalline molecular structure, which provides clearer temperature transition points under the temperature tests as the crystalline structures break down and rearrange.

However, ABS and PETG did show some promise when tested using ATR-FTIR (Attenuated Total Reflectance-Fourier Transform Infrared), which uses infrared to identify chemical bonds and material composition. The team did find differences in additives using this method, but still did not give enough information to link them back to their respective filament products.

The research is still very early, with more rigorous testing to be done, as the team printed using only one machine, printed small sample tiles cut with scissors, and left them for a year in monitored office conditions before testing. Properties could further change as cooling rates vary greatly depending on what the printer is printing: Faster cooling and slower print speeds on overhangs, or slower cooling and faster print speeds on infill sections. Different printers may also affect the physical properties, adding one more factor in the chain.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

So far, correctly identifying 26 out of 28 samples is a great start to cracking down on dangerous, untraceable illegal firearms. When the research matures and can reliably identify a broad range of materials, it could potentially trace 3D-printed firearms to the printer and filament, potentially creating a list of where they were purchased and who purchased them.

#10317. A Practical Workflow for Testing JavaScript Forms Before Deployment

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Web forms are an essential part of many applications. They allow users to register accounts, submit contact requests, update profiles, and complete purchases. However, a form that appears to work correctly during development can still fail when users enter unexpected values, lose their connection, or submit information multiple times.

Testing forms before deployment helps developers identify these problems early. Instead of relying only on a successful submission, it is useful to test validation, browser behavior, accessibility, and error handling as separate parts of the workflow.

Before writing tests, define what the form should do from the user's perspective. A registration form, for example, might require a valid email address, a password that meets specific rules, and confirmation that the user has accepted the terms.

List the expected outcomes for each field. Consider valid input, empty values, incorrect formats, and unusually long entries. This gives you a clear checklist for testing the form without depending on assumptions about how users will interact with it.

It is also worth considering what happens after submission. Does the form display a confirmation message? Are users informed when something goes wrong? Can they correct their input without entering everything again?

These details help establish the expected behavior before testing begins.

HTML provides several built-in attributes that handle common validation requirements. The required attribute prevents submission when a mandatory field is empty, while type="email" checks whether an email address follows an accepted format.

This approach reduces the amount of JavaScript needed for basic validation. It also provides a useful starting point for testing browser behavior.

However, client-side validation should not be treated as a security measure. Users can bypass browser checks, so the server must validate submitted data independently before processing or storing it.

Developers can also explore SitePoint's HTML and responsive web design guide to understand how form elements fit into layouts across different screen sizes.

JavaScript becomes useful when a form requires custom rules or more detailed feedback. For example, a password field might need to meet a minimum length, or two fields might need to contain matching values.

When testing this logic, check both successful and unsuccessful cases. A common mistake is to test only valid submissions, leaving error conditions unexamined.

Make sure that error messages explain what users need to change. A message such as "Please enter a valid email address" is more useful than a generic notification stating that the submission failed.

Also check that validation does not unexpectedly clear fields or prevent users from correcting mistakes. Error messages should be associated with the relevant inputs so that keyboard and assistive technology users can identify the problem.

Browser developer tools help identify problems that are not immediately visible in the interface. The Console panel can reveal JavaScript errors, while the Network panel helps developers inspect requests sent when a form is submitted.

For example, a form might display a loading indicator but never show a result because its API request returns an error. Inspecting the request status, response, and timing can help distinguish a frontend problem from a backend failure.

The MDN guide to browser developer tools explains how to inspect page elements, debug JavaScript, and investigate browser behavior.

Test situations such as an unavailable server, a delayed response, and invalid server-side data. The interface should communicate the problem clearly and provide a reasonable next step.

A form should remain usable across different devices and input methods. Test it with a keyboard to confirm that users can move between fields, activate buttons, and identify validation errors without a mouse.

Every input should have a clear label. Placeholder text alone is not a reliable replacement because it disappears when users begin typing.

Next, test the form at several viewport widths. Check that labels remain readable, buttons are accessible, and fields do not extend beyond their containers. Long error messages should not overlap other elements or break the page layout.

Automated accessibility checks can identify some issues, but manual testing is still important for understanding the actual user experience.

A simple checklist makes form testing more consistent across projects:

Check server errors and slow network responses.

Confirm that repeated submissions are handled appropriately.

Test keyboard navigation and accessible error messages.

Verify the layout on mobile and desktop screens.

For larger applications, these checks can be supplemented with automated unit and integration tests. Automation is particularly useful when the same validation rules must work consistently across multiple forms.

Reliable form testing involves more than confirming that a submit button works. Developers need to consider validation, server responses, accessibility, responsive layouts, and unexpected user behavior.

By combining HTML validation, targeted JavaScript tests, browser developer tools, and a repeatable checklist, teams can identify problems before deployment. The result is a more dependable interface that helps users complete tasks without unnecessary confusion or frustration.

Just like we suspected, our fellow Australian hardware examiner TrashBench is up to shenanigans again. Not too long ago, after putting a graphics card in the "wrong" place for much better cooling results, he's ... thinking bigger, let's put it that way. He took an old 4-kW outdoor radiator-fan unit from a split air-conditioning setup (sans gas) and hooked it up to a water-cooling loop to assess its performance.

He kicked off the video with his best impression of Crocodile Dundee, and set out to rearrange the unit for the task at hand, as he only needed the actual radiator, fan, and pump. Most of the fiddly guts were taken out, and casters were added for convenience. TrashBench then added a bog-standard reservoir to the main enclosure.

Moving on to the PC side, he converted a T-joint into a Y-joint with judicious application of heat and force, and, being the ever-clever craftsman, designed 3D-printed mounting brackets for the various cold plates. It was around this time that it was made certain he'd be successful, as at 02:49 you can see that his knuckles paid the requisite blood toll to the machine god.

After firing up the entire system, he set to benchmarking, enlisting both a modern RTX 5060 and a hotter, earlier RTX 3080 for the process. The 5060 is a very power-efficient GPU, and its temperature with the standard cooler was a tame 52ºC. However, the mighty power of four kilowatts' worth of cooling brought that down to just 33ºC, around the temperature of lukewarm water, and a 16ºC drop altogether.

With such a wide thermal range to work with, he set out to overclock the living daylights out of the GPU, moving the boost clock needle from 2902 MHz up to 3300 MHz. Despite the 14% clock-speed improvement, average FPS saw a mere 3% uplift, clearly highlighting how perfectly Nvidia has the power/performance curve dialed in since the RTX 2000 series.

He then moved on to the RTX 3080, a beastly, much more power-hungry card that could provide a worthy challenger for the Fujitsu radiator. It was promptly defeated, and its temperature went from 65ºC down to just 28ºC, or in other words, not much above ambient. Overclocking this card, however, also yielded a mere 3% fps, barely worth the man's time.

The final challenge was seeing how well the teeny weeny Fujitsu could handle a combined GPU and CPU load. The processor on hand was probably the worst possible consumer chip to try and keep cool, the lightning-fast and flame-hot Intel Core i9 14900K.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

TrashBench hooked it up and saw CPU temperatures drop 18ºC down to 76ºC, a strong achievement for this silicon outside of rooms with liquid nitrogen buckets. As for the graphics card, its heat sensor simply didn't move at all, suggesting that the radiator had far greater headroom than TrashBench had hardware.

#10312. Veteran Microsoft engineer makes his own RAM from scratch using AI

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Dave Plummer, former Microsoft engineer and wizard of Task Manager, Windows Pinball, and now TMOG, has an interesting project in his hands once again. After seeing exactly how many zeros were in the price tag for a megabyte of memory for his vintage PDP-11, he decided to take matters into his own hands and conjure up a new RAM board.

The PDP-11 is from 1970, and later models could fit in as much as four megabytes of memory. Plummer enlisted the help of ChatGPT and the EasyEDA online circuit simulator, though he notes that he hadn't done complicated projects like this before. As several PDP-11 models used Q-Bus boards for main system components, he designed this RAM as a standalone card, then tested it on a backplane alongside another board containing the system's M8190 CPU.

Atop it, he placed a Lattice MachXO2 FPGA serving as a memory controller (model 2000HC, if you're wondering), connected to four ISSI 1 MB memory chips. After adding a U2S I/O chip, a bank of DIP switches, comm ports, and sprinkling a handful of capacitors, he had a complete circuit. The M8190 board design has a 22-bit bus and reserves the top 256 KB for I/O and ROM.

However, it wasn't a first-time-lucky project. Plummer encountered a few abortive starts, as he'd never flash-programmed an FPGA chip before. He fixed that after a few attempts, but then he fell into what appears to be a classic PDP-11 trap.

The machine's memory bus design means it can only access entire words (two bytes) at once, and treats the lowest bit in a memory address as a "lane" selector that picks between the low or high byte in the word.

When reading, the CPU will pick out the right byte itself, but Plummer's "memory controller" still needs to return two. Similarly, on writes, the bit selector must be read and then the proper byte selected so that only that will be updated, as the bus lines will still carry an entire word that could otherwise write invalid data.

Plummer seemingly wasn't aware of this before, and one commenter joked that he'd "built 4MB of memory that turned out to be 2MB", to which he replied that the memory "only returns every second word," matching the aforementioned design pattern. Although there are no further updates, since more commenters pointed this out, we assume that he implemented an easy fix in his FPGA code.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

One person suggested that he sidestep the entire problem and implement his memory in software via a Raspberry Pi instead, wired directly to the PDP bus. Plummer replied that he'd basically done that already last week with a modern QBone DEC interface board, which in turn contains a Beaglebone Black atop it (similar to a Raspberry Pi).

#10310. AMVR’s new head strap is my favorite way to keep cool with a Meta Quest 3 or 3S

The upgraded AMVR BS1 head strap includes better ergonomics, a built-in fan, magnetic rechargeable batteries, and a new breathable mesh top support for ultimate comfort.

I don’t know how companies like AMVR and BoboVR keep doing it, but AMVR’s latest head strap is now the most comfortable cooling head strap I’ve ever used for the Meta Quest 3 and Quest 3S. If you’re still using the included cloth head strap or haven’t used a “cooling head strap” before, here’s how it works.

The AMVR BS1 uses a breathable forehead pad attached via magnets to the support bar. Inside that support bar is an adjustable-speed fan, which you can easily adjust using the recessed wheel on the right side of the head strap. This fan keeps sweat down and helps prevent lens fog, two problems that can be a constant annoyance.

The fan is powered by the magnetically detachable, rechargeable 8,800mAh battery pack that mounts on the back of the head strap, helping to even out the weight distribution across the headset. That battery pack will also charge your Quest while playing and adds around 4 hours of additional play time. You can always purchase more packs to keep things going for longer, if you need to.

Lots of padding can be found all around the headset, and the mesh support on top accepts any weight that remains. Every bit of padding is easily removable and washable, so you can keep things clean and tidy (especially if you share your headset with others). You can also get extra pads if you’d rather do it that way.

The back of the head strap cradles your skull with a mix of half supple padding and half grippy silicone. You’ll be double happy there, as the pad on the top part further cushions any headset weight, while the silicone keeps the headset from slipping forward and shifting weight onto your cheeks.

If you don’t need a fan, the BoboVR M3 Pro+ is currently $17 less than the AMVR BS1. I find the BoboVR M3 Pro+’s hybrid design to be slightly more comfortable, but the lack of a fan is a big negative for this particular BoboVR strap.

Once you’ve chosen a head strap, grab an AMVR FC4M face pad to add another layer of comfort to the headset. Like the AMVR BS1’s forehead pad, this face pad attaches to the Quest 3 (NOT the Quest 3S) via magnets and is made of breathable, easy-to-clean material. It’s perfect for staying cool year-round and makes the Quest so, so much more comfortable.

This raises a lot of ethical questions, though.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Exin Therapeutics, a biotech startup focusing on next-generation neurotherapeutics, claims it has successfully conducted brain surgery on a mouse using an AI agent. Its co-founder and CEO, Gabriel Ocana-Santero, shared the entire procedure on X, showing how the AI agent chose the gene therapy vector and dosage and the brain region where it would be applied. The AI agent also drove the robotic arms that injected the medication into the mouse’s brain. Thankfully, the operation seems to have been a success, with the mouse recovering well and recorded moving around in its enclosure.

Last night an AI agent performed brain surgery on a mouse. We gave it 19 gene therapy vectors in a spreadsheet, a mouse model of epilepsy, access to a motorize surgery rig with cameras and one goal: reduce seizures. It picked the drug. It picked the brain region. It derived the coordinates from three papers. Then it drove the stereotaxic arms into the brain and injected 250 nanolitres while watching the entry site through the rig camera for bleeding. A licensed vet gated every irreversible step and held a physical kill switch to stop the AI agent. Thread on what it decided, what it saw, and the moment it refused to let me override it. [Writing this on the spot out of excitement, but I've been 18hrs in front of my laptop coding and coordinating this, so keeping it brief and descriptive, more cohesive thoughts on this soon...]October 28, 2026

“Autonomous surgical robots exist and they are better at the mechanics than our bench rig, [to be honest]. But in every one of them, a human decided what operation to perform, and the robot executed it,” Ocana-Santero said. “Nobody told this agent what to give or where to put it. It got a phenotype and a shelf of candidates and had to work out the therapy and the coordinates itself. I am not aware of an agent previously choosing a treatment and a brain target and then delivering it into a living mammal.”

The team used Anthropic Sonnet, which received a mouse model of epilepsy, control of a motorized surgery rig, and 19 gene therapy vectors in a spreadsheet. Aside from that, it also had access to three research papers, allowing it to choose the brain region where it should apply the treatment. Nevertheless, a licensed veterinarian was purportedly overseeing the entire operation, with every irreversible step requiring approval before the AI agent could proceed. Aside from that, the entire system also had an emergency stop button that would cut the power to every motor in the surgery rig.

The only thing that the vet surgeon on duty did was to do the craniotomy procedure to expose the brain, as the AI agent wasn’t allowed to do that yet for safety reasons. There were also several safeguards that the AI agents were banned from overriding, which even the human operators couldn’t bypass, necessitating a manual fix to the agent’s code during the operation.

What’s interesting, though, is that the startup only used Anthropic’s mid-range Sonnet model for the operation. It initially tried using Opus 5.5, but it immediately refused to do any work after it detected that it was working on a biological experiment. The older Opus 5 model was able to run the entire procedure on paper but refused to do live surgery. “The block isn't the rig and isn't the inputs. It's me. My own output was stopped when I started writing the plan for this surgery... if I try, it gets cut off the same way, and the worst version of that is me cut off partway through a live procedure with a needle somewhere in the brain,” the LLM reportedly told the Exin Therapeutics team. “Starting something I can't finish on an anesthetized animal is worse than not starting.”

EARLY conclusions Autonomous surgical robots exist and they are better at the mechanics than our bench rig tbh. But in every one of them, a human decided what operation to perform, and the robot executed it. Nobody told this agent what to give or where to put it. It got a phenotype and a shelf of candidates and had to work out the therapy and the coordinates itself. I am not aware of an agent previously choosing a treatment and a brain target and then delivering it into a living mammal. This is what we mean by in vivo lab in the loop. FULLY connecting agents to our experimental capabilities. More on this soon. P.S.: Mouse recovered well! Full write-up soon on substackOctober 28, 2026

Despite the seeming success of the operation, some people are concerned about the ethics of such a procedure. While AI LLMs have been useful in medical research, adding them to sinus surgery has seen malfunctions skyrocket by more than 12 times. Aside from that, relying on an AI to make every decision in such a risky procedure puts accountability in question, especially if something goes wrong. We also haven’t even touched the long-standing ethical question of animal experimentation in the medical setting. Still, some users applauded the experiment, with one comment saying that they’re excited about the possibilities of these integrated technologies, as their son has been suffering from drug-resistant epilepsy for 19 years.

#10307. Some Pixel Watch users report a sudden decrease in battery life

A surge of online reports indicates that some users are experiencing a recent increase in battery drain on their Pixel Watch, by as much as 20% every few hours.

Sometimes new software introduces extra stress on device batteries. New processes can take a toll on phone or smartwatch energy at a higher cost than expected, and that might be what’s happening with some Pixel Watch users.

It’s nowhere near a definitive trend, but it’s intriguing to see several new posts on Reddit pop up with the same complaint – their Pixel Watch’s battery is draining faster than normal (via Android Authority). One post has 65 comments, with a healthy number sharing the same concern. One user wrote that their Pixel Watch 2 is getting 12 hours of use on a full charge, instead of the normal 48 hours with AOD off.

The other user claims their Pixel Watch 4 now lasts less than a day. Their normal usage reportedly gets them up to 2.5 days. That’s high for the Pixel Watch 4, but it’s not unheard of. Both of these users dropping by 12 or 24 hours from one charge isn’t nothing.

Interesting, because in the last week or so I have noticed shorter battery life. Typically I was getting 2 full days with my Pixel Watch 4, the last handful of cycles I've noticed the low battery alert coming on maybe 4 - 5 hours earlier.

Sporadic comments echo the same situation, but many report they haven’t noticed a difference. Timing isn’t entirely consistent across comments, either. Some say they started seeing the issue a few days ago, while others say this happened to them a while back.

Still, it might indicate a recent software update is to blame. Google’s recent September 2026 update reaches the Pixel Watch 2 and newer. It adds “Gemini Personalization,” which Google says leverages past conversations and Google apps like Gmail or Calendar to offer personalized responses. It’s also possible that the battery is affected by the recent Google Health update with automatic activity tracking, but that wouldn’t explain why the Pixel Watch 2 is having an issue.

It’s unclear if that’s playing a factor, but Google has yet to officially acknowledge the issue for these users.

#10305. 7 Ways to Keep API Changes Safe as More Teams Join a Project

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A backend team renames a response field from name to displayName. Its tests pass, the deployment succeeds, and the API continues returning HTTP 200. Minutes later, a dashboard starts showing blank user labels.

The failure is easy to explain: the server changed a contract that another application depended on. Preventing it requires more than asking teams to communicate better. The dependency needs to become visible in documentation, tests, and deployment decisions.

This article follows that field rename through seven practical safeguards. The examples apply to frontend and backend teams working in the same office, across departments, or in different countries.

Start by documenting the existing response and the intended replacement.

The two objects may look equivalent to a person, but software reads specific keys. A client using user.name will not automatically discover displayName.

An OpenAPI description can document request and response shapes alongside status codes and other interface details. Keep it in version control and update it in the same change as the implementation.

Document meaning as well as structure. Does displayName contain user-supplied text? Can it be empty? Is it intended for display only? Must consumers avoid using it as an identifier?

SitePoint’s REST API best practices provides background on interface design. For an existing API, the immediate task is to identify the promises clients already rely on.

An endpoint can serve more than the application shown in the product demo. Consumers may include an administrative dashboard, a mobile app, scheduled exports, internal scripts, and external integrations.

The point is to discover different upgrade timelines. A web application may update quickly, while an installed mobile client remains active for months.

Inspect request telemetry where available, but do not treat a short period of inactivity as proof that a consumer is unused. A quarterly export will not appear in a one-week observation window.

Also record whether consumers reject unknown fields. Adding a response property is often easier to migrate than removing one, but strict decoders can make even additive changes incompatible.

For this example, a transitional response can expose both fields:

Generate both values from the same source so they cannot drift during the migration.

The fourth step needs evidence. The server usually cannot observe which property a client reads after receiving a response. Use consumer version information, published contracts, maintained integration records, and explicit confirmations where appropriate.

For external APIs, removal may require a new version or a documented deprecation process. Do not remove a field simply because the internal dashboard has migrated.

Consider rollback before deployment. If the consumer now requires the new field, rolling the server back to an older response can break it. Either preserve compatibility during rollback or give the consumer a deliberate transition strategy.

A temporary adapter can let a consumer accept both the old and transitional response formats. Keep it narrow and document when it can be removed.

Save the following runnable example as user-label.test.mjs:

Run it with node --test user-label.test.mjs.

The fallback policy is a product decision. For a navigation label, “Unnamed user” may be acceptable. For an operation that requires a verified identity, missing required data may need to stop the workflow.

These tests exercise the adapter only. They do not prove that the live API returns the expected payload or that the application calls the adapter. Add integration coverage at the boundary where the response enters the client.

For UI rendering, insert the returned label as text or use your framework’s normal escaping. The adapter is not an HTML sanitizer.

Several tools can contribute to the review, but their responsibilities differ.

A useful pipeline can lint the API description, compare it against the released baseline, run consumer tests, and verify provider behavior.

Store the baseline deliberately. Comparing two files from the same proposed change can miss a regression against the deployed contract.

SitePoint’s Postman workflow guide explains collections and request organization. Keep shared examples free of production credentials, and include failure responses alongside successful ones.

Mocks should include old, transitional, and new payloads during a migration. This gives frontend developers and designers a way to inspect loading states, missing data, and error handling before the provider rollout finishes.

As more contributors join a project, architectural decisions need to remain understandable outside the meeting where they were made.

Poland has also become a popular choice for companies looking to expand their development teams without limiting their search to local talent.

For a distributed project, the engineering requirement is concrete: a new contributor should be able to identify who owns an interface, which consumers depend on it, and how a change reaches production.

Use actual team names and version identifiers in a real repository. Link the note from the pull request and make unresolved decisions explicit.

A reviewer should not need to infer whether a field is already deprecated or whether the mobile team has been consulted. That context should remain available when colleagues work different schedules.

Define what success looks like before shipping. For this rename, useful checks might include schema-validation failures, client exceptions around user rendering, and unexpected increases in missing-name fallbacks.

Avoid putting names or entire response bodies into diagnostic logs. A template version, consumer version, error category, and correlation identifier may provide sufficient evidence.

Test combinations that can exist during deployment:

Do not assume every combination is supported forever. Define the supported window and its rollback implications.

Once the removal condition is satisfied, retire the old field and temporary adapter through reviewed changes. Update fixtures and documentation so they describe the current contract.

A safe API migration gives teams an explicit sequence, tests the compatibility assumptions, and preserves enough evidence to decide when the transition is complete. That is what allows contributors to work independently without surprising the applications that depend on them.

Following a “coming months” teaser earlier this year, Motorola has confirmed that its flagship Signature 27 device is launching in the US in November.

A November launch date is much earlier than Motorola’s announcement initially suggested, and also lines up with this week’s retail leak. As for an exact timeline, we’ll have to wait and see, but it’s clearly coming up very soon.

#10303. Anthropic's Claude AI blocks video game cloning and reverse-engineering

Many game developers are reporting Claude's refusal to work on their own projects.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Reverse-engineering games for converting them between systems is currently a hot topic, and it's getting messier by the day. According to multiple user claims, Anthropic's Claude Code and/or Opus 5.5 model are blocking attempts at innocuous reverse-engineering, including but not limited to those using the popular Reverse Engineer Anything (REA) tool. That issue may be linked to a text rule that's now in force, which allegedly instructs the model to avoid "[recreating] a specific existing game." The updated rule appears to be a move to limit a flood of AI-cracked and cloned titles, with some game devs even recently being hit by extortion scams that threaten to clone their game demos into complete games before they have a chance to finish and release the original.According to Twitter user Chasm, the game-related textual rule is "Do build original games, games in a genre, and games from the user’s own designs and assets. Don’t build a recreation of a specific existing game, even if it is renamed or called original." There is no official or additional confirmation if this is the actual rule text, but it's plausible enough given the very clear refusals that devs have been encountering over the past week.

This situation is tricky to analyze due to the varying types of reports. First, the existence of a game-related rule is real, as Claude Code's GitHub is seeing an influx of issues complaining about refusals to do standard game work halfway through development, when replicating a game mechanic, and patching a tabletop-like game. It's possible that some of those reports have been made by users with nefarious purposes, but at least one is a bona fide developer with a real track record.

As for reverse engineering, there had already been a smattering of user reports across Reddit and other platforms, wherein Claude refused to perform tasks like (allegedly) analyzing a simple Windows crash dump, clearing up junkware on a Samsung TV, or just using REA altogether.

The simultaneous timing of both game- and cyber-security restrictions may be a coincidence, and yet there are complaints from purported game developers saying that Claude blocked their own games' anti-cheat development and security reviews, under the justification that its safeguards were triggered.

Anthropic introduced its Cyber Verification Program on October 6, essentially saying that access to its frontier models' advanced cybersecurity capabilities would be limited to entities covered by Project Glasswing, taking effect immediately.

It's possible that whichever ruleset was applied is the same one that's causing the cybersecurity-related refusals. Still, it's odd to see that 24 days have passed since then, and yet reports of bot refusal are only coming in since the last week or so.

As for possible motivations behind these restrictions, they are twofold. The first and most obvious one is that Anthropic — and, by extension, other AI players — are preemptively protecting themselves from potential lawsuits from software companies, including game studios, for offering the means to reverse-engineer their software.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

There is no official statement on these matters from Anthropic or other AI companies so far. Anthropic isn't the only one playing this game, too, as recently OpenAI deactivated the account of another person trying to clean up their TV's software.

The recent rash of AI-powered clones of application suites like Adobe's suite and Microsoft Office 365, as well as the countless recomps and decomps of console games, likely has most every software company on earth rethinking their life choices. And probably also in frequent contact with their legal teams.

The online reaction was swift, suggesting that users looking to perform reverse-engineering work simply use Chinese, open-weight, or local LLMs instead of Claude or GPT. This, at the same time, highlights the irony of frontier AI companies that copied the world's information and art for their training sets and are now concerned with copyright infringement.

And yet there are situations that merit some discussion, like the recent controversy about the Steam Next Fest being chock-full of AI slop copied from existing titles. That particular situation is getting particularly nasty, as game developers are reportedly being extorted under threat that their game demos will be cloned into complete games before they have a chance to finish and release the original.

Adding insult to injury, this is seemingly being done to small studios whose main priority is usually "having food," rather than big publishers looking to microtransaction everything into a new yacht for shareholders.

He's obsessed with detail and has a tendency to ramble on the topics he loves. When not doing that, he's usually playing games, or at live music shows and festivals.

#10301. Google had a very good, very bad Q3

Good morning! Today I’m reading about the death of the internet, deciding whether to buy a PROLED or many pro LEDs, building all my IKEA furniture with this step-by-step 3D app, trying desperately to get into the Readwise Books early access, and playing an “Enhanced 3D” Command & Conquer: Red Alert on my PC.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

Alphabet’s Q3 earnings were interesting. Despite a 25% rise in revenue and a healthy net income of $26 billion, analysts were disappointed with the company’s results, sending the stock down as much as 11% in after-hours trading.

On the plus side, the company’s cloud business is booming, doubling year-over-year revenue to $30 billion as it attracts frontier labs to its data centers. But advertising revenue didn’t meet expectations, and investors appear to be worried that its search advertising business, one of the most efficient sources of recurring revenue in history, is not keeping up with changing user behavior.

As Anthropic gets ready to soar into its IPO later this year, mature businesses like Alphabet, Microsoft, and Amazon are racing to adapt to a market where demand for AI infrastructure is outpacing compute supply. But investors are looking at the capital expenditure tied up in future data center buildouts and getting nervous that, if the bubble bursts, even these well-capitalized companies will take massive hits.

The biggest question surrounding Google’s stock performance is whether it can sufficiently monetize AI-powered search before demand for its traditional search inventory falls off a cliff. As the company sends less and less traffic to the open web, it’s touting the fact that AI Mode and AI Overviews keep people on Google for three times as long as traditional search.

So it’s no surprise that it’s expanding ads in AI Mode to 10 new markets this week, with more to come by the end of the year. While the post was only a few paragraphs long, expanding its AI ad business may end up being one of the company’s most consequential announcements of 2026.

I didn’t get to see either of the first Dune movies on IMAX, but you can bet your ass I’ve already secured opening-day tickets for the third one. Dune: Part Three debuts in December, and we now have the final trailer that showcases the impending showdown between Muad’Dib and Chani as the former realizes the consequences of his galactic war.

On the gadgety side, there’s another impending showdown brewing between XREAL’s Android XR-powered Aura glasses and Meta’s upcoming VR Glasses.

I liked Jasmine Uniza’s video below comparing the two because, until I watched it, I didn’t realize the extent of the diverging approaches to reality they’re each taking. The Aura glasses are more traditionally translucent and somewhat awkwardly project two OLED screens on top of the dimmed lenses, allowing the outside world to filter through. Meta’s VR Glasses eschew the traditional headset shape and weight of the Quest but block out far more of the outside world and use external cameras, like their predecessors, to pipe in mixed-reality footage.

Finally, some personal news of a sort: I am now a permanent host of The Sideload. If you know me at all, you know I love a podcast, and Will Sattelberg was my co-host at the Android Police podcast for three years before he left for 9to5 last year. I’m so excited to be doing this again every week with him, and I hope you’ll tune in to our most recent episode about the death of the web browser and other good news.

If you’ve been patiently waiting for Apple’s top-tier M5 Ultra-powered Mac Studio, fear not: Apple has you covered. From 9to5Mac:

When Apple announced the new M5 Max and M5 Ultra configurations of the Mac Studio in August, it said a configuration with 512GB of RAM would be available in October. Today, the company officially revealed the pricing of that option.

If you head to the Mac Studio configurator on Apple’s website, you’ll see the new 512GB upgrade option. As previously announced, the 512GB option is only available with the top-tier M5 Ultra chip that has a 36-core CPU and 80‑core GPU.

It’s a $15,500 upgrade over the base configuration of that processor, which has 96GB of unified memory.

To put it another way: you’ll pay $22,299 for the Mac Studio with the top-tier M5 Ultra, 512GB of unified memory, and 1TB of storage. You can spec it up all the way to 16TB of storage, which brings the total price to $29,799.00.

There’s FreeDOS and classic games like Doom ready to play around with, or you can use your own disk images.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Developer Jan de Jager has created a full Intel 486 PC system in a browser. On the e486-web GitHub page, de Jager boasts that the emulator is coded in 100% pure JavaScript. The project’s fuller title is the Retro486 Modular Browser PC. Claude gets a development credit, so we guess you could say e486-web was (partially) vibe-coded.

The live demo you can boot with a single click is impressive, with tabs to interact with and configure your browser-based, dependency-free Intel 80486DX2-66 PC in a browser. The first tab is dubbed the CRT monitor, and this is where you will spend the most time using the command line, apps, and games. However, it is also very appealing to be able to adjust your PC configuration in the tabs for Motherboard & Slots, Storage Bay, Custom Card Studio, and System & Diagnostics.

I couldn’t resist taking the e486-web for a spin using the live demo. The CRT monitor screen is totally charming, with various display options including color and scanlines, and tributes to classic CRTs with green phosphor, amber, or paperwhite displays. This is the screen where you will also type DOS commands and interact with whatever you get to run.

The next tab in this comprehensive 486 PC system emulator lets you pick a motherboard design and, probably more importantly, add and remove expansion cards. The default system comes with a VLB graphics card, Sound Blaster 16 ISA card, a multi-I/O controller, network card, and more. All the cards seem to be configurable. A Custom Card Studio tab lets you create and test more cards, and it looks quite advanced.

I spent quite a lot of time in the Storage Bay tab, switching through the OS and software disks inside the “1-Click bundled & Cached Disks” collection. FreeDOS started fine, but I got some errors when I wanted to test some of the games in the library, which includes Doom, Wolfenstein 3D, and more. Though tempted to find and install Syndicate on the hard disk that the emulator can create and configure, I didn't get around to it yet. However, the Bullfrog classic is one of the first games I played on an actual 486DX2 system in the 1990s, and I also played it on Amiga. This browser-based emulator features browser persistence, so e486-web settings can be maintained between sessions, and it automatically caches ROMs, floppy images, and custom uploaded .img/.iso files for your convenience.

Another tab in this extensive system emulator is called System & Diagnostics. Here you can switch the CPU to a different model, view the CPU registers, and nose around in RAM, VRAM, storage, BIOS, boot sectors, and so on.

Currently e486-web is in version 0.1, and the GitHub shows that most files were uploaded/updated about a week ago. Some files are just two days old, though, so hopefully wrinkles are busily being ironed out by de Jager and Claude. The presentation of this no-downloads live emulator, and the conveniently tabbed interface and configuration options, show lots of promise.

#10296. Intel shifts production capacity from consumer to server CPUs in face of 'unprecedented' AI demand

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Client platforms struggle to get memory anyway, right?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Although the consumer PC market is shrinking and will continue to shrink in the coming quarters, Intel is confident that the overwhelming demand from the AI industry for everything that can compute or store data will offset declines in the client space. To that end, the company is reallocating some of its capacity from client to data center CPUs, expanding its Fab 24 in Ireland to make more Xeons, has begun equipment installation into Fab 62 in Arizona ahead of schedule and vows to make 18A chips there, and is expanding its advanced packaging capacity in New Mexico and Malaysia.

"The industry continues to navigate one of the most significant supply shortages in history, driven by the unprecedented demand for Super Intelligence," said Lip-Bu Tan, chief executive of Intel, during the company's earnings call. "Against this backdrop, I am pleased by the progress we made growing our server supply in Q3. I am mindful about the ongoing challenges we face in the future quarters to keep pace with server demand."

Intel's manufacturing expansion comes as demand for server processors continues to exceed supply despite substantial production increases over the next several quarters. The company's Data Center and AI (DCAI) business generated $7.3 billion in revenue in the third quarter of 2026, up 17% sequentially and nearly 80% year-over-year. Intel expects demand for data center CPUs to remain strong in 2027 – 2028 as AI infrastructure operators deploy more CPU-intensive agentic and inference workloads.

To meet demand for Xeon CPUs in the coming years, one of Intel's immediate priorities is to increase output of Xeon 6 'Granite Rapids' processors, which are made using its Intel 3 process technology. The company recently began expanding production capacity at its Fab 24 manufacturing complex near Leixlip, Ireland, but this capacity will only come online by the end of 2027, unless the move can be accelerated.

To further increase its Intel 3 capacity, Intel plans to convert some manufacturing resources at its Oregon facilities from Intel 18A to Intel 3. These resources were originally intended to manufacture Panther Lake client processors, but weaker PC demand has allowed Intel to prioritize higher-demand data center products. The company expects the additional Intel 3 capacity in Oregon to enter production in the second half of 2027.

"What we are taking offline to put Intel 3 into Oregon is 18A wafers, which we are generally going to build Panther Lake products," said Dave Zinsner, chief financial officer of Intel. "We see the opportunity with some of that capacity freed up to go produce more data center wafers."

Granite Rapids has already become Intel's fastest ramping server processor family, but demand continues to exceed available supply, according to the company's management. Intel is also improving manufacturing yields and reducing production cycle times to extract more usable processors from its existing capacity. According to the company, Intel 3 manufacturing costs have declined by approximately 15% since the first quarter of 2026.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Intel is also accelerating the expansion of its leading-edge manufacturing operations in Arizona. The company is ramping Intel 18A production at Fab 52 and is accelerating equipment installation into the adjacent Fab 62, which is set to become the company's second high-volume manufacturing facility for 18A, 18A-P, and derivative process technologies.

These investments will support future Intel 18A/18A-P-based products, including server CPUs, although the company has not disclosed the precise allocation of manufacturing capacity between client and data center products at the facility. While the latter is natural, Intel said during the conference call that it expects to win some market share in the client space with its Core Ultra 400-series 'Nova Lake' CPUs, which will require the company to produce these parts in high volumes.

To support its capacity expansion, Intel aggressively procured new tools in the third quarter.

While expanding production of Intel 3 and Intel 18A/18A-P wafers is a must to increase output of data center CPUs, producing more wafers will not necessarily solve Intel's supply problems. The company also faces shortages of advanced organic substrates required to package its multi-chiplet processors (or rather system-in-packages, or SiPs?) that rely on EMIB and/or Foveros 3D packaging technologies. Intel has been working with substrate suppliers to expand their production capacity and expects approximately 40% additional substrate capacity to become available in the second half of 2027.

"We are not only constrained on the wafer side, we are also constrained on the substrate side," Zinsner said. "So, it is important we get the substrate piece of this ramped up to be able to meet the demand."

In addition, Intel is expanding its advanced packaging operations in New Mexico and Malaysia to support growing demand from internal product groups and external customers. The company already uses its EMIB technology to connect multiple compute dies in Granite Rapids processors, while its newer Clearwater Forest Xeons combine EMIB with Foveros Direct 3D and hybrid bonding to stack Intel 18A compute dies on Intel 3 base dies. Additional packaging capacity will be essential as Intel increases production of these processors and introduces more complex multi-die products as well as assembles advanced system-in-packages using EMIB, EMIB-T, Foveros 3D, and other technologies for external clients.

"On Advanced Packaging, we are pleased by the progress we are making with EMIB and EMIB-T for our internal products," Tan said. "Both continue to see significant external customer traction as well. We continue to improve on execution, working with our supply chain to secure the capacity to meet the needs of our Advanced Packaging customers. We are also seeing robust progress in the readiness of Hybrid Bonding Interconnect (HBI), which is an enabler of 3D chip stacking for our upcoming server products."

Intel's expansions will require substantial capital. Intel expects its capital expenditure (CapEx) budget to reach approximately $20 billion in 2026 and increase significantly in 2027 (though it did not reveal any firm numbers). The company said firm purchase orders to semiconductor equipment suppliers increased by nearly 40% sequentially as it secured tools for its manufacturing expansion. Intel is also evaluating opportunities to accelerate construction of its semiconductor manufacturing complex in Ohio, where the first major structural truss was recently installed.

"We have made good progress securing the equipment required to support our growth plans with firm purchase orders to equipment suppliers increasing nearly 40% sequentially," Zinsner said. "We are also accelerating clean room build-outs, including Fab 62 in Arizona and exploring opportunities to accelerate in Ohio.

Yet Intel's capacity expansion will take time to produce results. New equipment must be installed and qualified, additional wafers must pass through lengthy manufacturing cycles, and substrate suppliers must increase their output (which will likely require them to procure new tools and install them) before Intel can ship more finished processors. Consequently, the company expects supply constraints to remain particularly severe during the first half of 2027, even as the client PC market is set to continue contracting.

Nevertheless, Intel expects its data center business to continue growing and believes that additional manufacturing capacity, higher yields, and improved substrate availability will enable it to capitalize on demand for AI infrastructure in the second half of 2027 and beyond.

#10293. Samsung’s advanced sleep apnea detection is coming to these Galaxy wearables

Samsung announced a new, advanced version of its AI-powered sleep apnea detection, and which devices it will appear on in November.

Sleep apnea detection was first introduced to Galaxy devices in 2024, after Samsung gained approval from the FDA in the US. The tool was offered as a baseline detection feature, so users who might be suffering from frequent breathing interruptions during sleep would be aware and seek further medical help.

Samsung says its new and improved version uses an AI algorithm to analyze breathing disturbances per hour (via GSMArena). It goes beyond detection and breaks those disturbances down into more granular detail – closer to something a dedicated sleep study would be equipped to do.

Users will need to record at least five hours of sleep twice within 10 days for the sleep apnea detection tool to properly get results on their Galaxy Watch or Ring.

Samsung is still adamant that this tool does not replace professional diagnosis; it’s simply better at making the user aware of their sleep habits.

Selling the exact same model worldwide at the same price while disabling features under the guise of country restriction is unfair. For example, keeping health features inactive in certain regions under the name of FDA approval. If Samsung wanted to, they could easily enable features like blood pressure and ECG for non-medical use for Bangladesh—or at least in countries where these features aren't legally prohibited—for health awareness. Not doing so feels like a form of deception toward customers.

Samsung says the new sleep apnea detection feature will roll out in November, starting in Korea, after the tool was approved by the Ministry of Food and Drug Safety. It’s FDA-approved in the US, as well, so it should follow shortly.

So far, Samsung has detailed these wearables:

After the initial rollout, the Galaxy Watch 7 and Watch 8 will get an update as long as they’re running Wear OS 7.0 or later.

There are no plans to dismantle the current sleep apnea detection tool available on the Galaxy Watch 4 and later.

#10289. 7 Checks That Make Form Bugs Easier to Reproduce Across Teams

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A designer reports that a form’s error message overlaps its submit button. The developer opens the same page and sees no problem. Both are using the same code, but one has a narrow viewport, a translated interface, and an unusually long validation message.

A screenshot records what appeared on screen. A reproducible example also preserves the inputs, responses, permissions, and environment that produced it. That distinction matters when people review work at different times or join a project without knowing its undocumented conventions.

This article uses a workspace-invitation form as an example. The form accepts an email address and a role, submits a request, and displays either a confirmation or an error. The seven checks below turn its awkward states into something developers and designers can inspect together.

“The form looks broken” leaves too much for the next person to discover.

A useful report specifies the starting state, action, observed result, and expected behavior:

Include the browser, application revision, and relevant account permissions. Explain whether “360 pixels” means the browser’s layout viewport or the dimensions of an exported screenshot.

Avoid using real customer addresses in shared fixtures. A synthetic address such as member@example.test is sufficient to exercise the interface.

This report gives a designer a clear layout requirement and gives a developer a precise state to recreate.

A form tested only with successful submissions is missing most of its interesting behavior.

Create a small set of fixtures that cover the product’s actual rules:

Keep fixture values deterministic. Random addresses or constantly changing timestamps make visual comparisons harder to interpret.

Record expected outcomes alongside the data. A response containing a message does not establish where the interface should display it, whether focus should move, or which fields should remain editable.

Treat those as product decisions and test them explicitly.

Some failures can be reproduced by passing props to a component. Others depend on the request lifecycle.

Storybook args can describe inputs for isolated component states. An error-state story might supply a message, current field values, and a pending flag to a presentational form component.

However, passing pending: true does not prove that the real application enters or exits that state correctly.

For network-dependent behavior, use Mock Service Worker or your test runner’s request interception to supply a controlled response. Preserve the distinction between these two checks:

Avoid mocking the same endpoint in several overlapping layers. When a response is unexpected, the team should be able to identify which fixture produced it.

Also keep at least some integration coverage against the real API. A frontend mock that returns an outdated error shape can make the interface look reliable while hiding a broken contract.

Open the failing state at the recorded viewport. Inspect the error container, its parent layout, and the submit button.

Look for constraints such as fixed heights, absolute positioning, hidden overflow, and a flex or grid item that refuses to shrink.

A possible layout for a message beside an action is:

This pattern allows the message column to shrink and stacks the elements at a narrow width. It is not a universal fix: choose a breakpoint and arrangement that suit the surrounding form.

If the error belongs to a particular field, placing it beside the submit button may be the wrong design regardless of how well it wraps.

SitePoint’s browser-based CSS debugging guide explains how to inspect applied rules and layout behavior. Its guide to troubleshooting CSS with Inspect Element provides additional background.

Use DevTools experiments to identify the cause, then make the lasting change in the source. Check a wide viewport afterward so a narrow-screen fix does not introduce a desktop regression.

A screenshot can show that an error is present. It cannot, by itself, prove that the form preserved the email address or prevented an unintended navigation.

The following Playwright example assumes an existing application route, configured baseURL, and accessible form labels. Adapt the endpoint, response schema, and selectors to your project.

The example expects a native select for the role and a named form landmark. A custom role picker requires different interactions.

The behavior assertions precede the visual comparison. The screenshot then helps reviewers spot overlap, clipping, or unintended spacing changes.

Playwright’s visual comparison documentation explains baseline screenshots. Generate and compare them in a consistent environment; operating-system and font differences can affect rendering.

Review the first baseline manually. A screenshot test can faithfully preserve a bug if the initial image is approved without inspection. Disabling animation for a snapshot also does not test the accessibility of motion itself.

As a project grows, a component’s behavior should remain discoverable without a private walkthrough.

Poland has also become a popular choice for companies looking to expand their development teams without limiting their search to local talent.

The practical requirement for distributed contributors is a review process that carries its own context. Attach the exact fixture, preview location, reproduction command, and expected behavior to the issue or pull request.

Keep the open decision separate from the implemented behavior. This prevents reviewers from assuming that a visual change has already resolved a product question.

Name the person or team responsible for the component, and identify where design decisions are recorded. A stable fixture is useful only if someone maintains it as the component evolves.

A form that no longer overlaps can still be difficult to use.

Verify visible labels, keyboard navigation, focus visibility, and the relationship between errors and their fields. For field-level errors, consider aria-invalid and aria-describedby where appropriate.

The alert in the test example assumes a newly displayed form-level error. Do not add several competing live regions or announce every keystroke. Check the resulting experience with assistive technology.

SitePoint’s accessible form layout guide is relevant background for the relationship between structure and usability.

A focused toolset can support the workflow:

Use the smallest set that answers the current question. Adding another tool is useful when it closes a testing gap, not merely because it produces another report.

A good handoff lets someone unfamiliar with the original conversation recreate the failure, understand the expected behavior, and verify the fix. When the fixture and regression check remain with the code, that work continues to help the next contributor.

OpenRouter has also started prioritizing speed over cost.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Darkbloom, a decentralized AI inference network that aggregates and sells spare compute capacity from Macs, told its members to expect revenues to fall as token prices continue to drop. According to The Register, the price per million tokens for Anthropic dropped by 77% in under five months, falling to $0.62 in October 2026 from $2.65 in May.

“Data centers have become cheaper. Output per NVIDIA cluster is up ~20x this year, and their margins are wide enough to cut prices 90 percent and still profit at 50 percent,” said Kydo, a researcher from Eigen Labs, the developer of the compute platform. “The market caught up to our price. We could go lower, but then most of you would earn even less [than] nothing.”

It’s estimated that someone running an M5 Pro Mac Mini with 64GB of Unified Memory could earn as much as $192 per month. This is the best-case scenario, though; the median earnings of the top 50 operators on Darkbloom’s leaderboard are about $159.86 per month. This translates to about $5.26 daily or $1,918.50 annually. However, if we account for Anthropic’s 77% token price drop, earnings will fall to a measly $36.77 per month, or about $1.23 per day.

AI token prices are falling so fast that they've even outpaced Moore’s Law, with some reports saying AI is becoming 50% cheaper every quarter and up to 13 times more affordable every year. News of companies blowing through their annual AI budgets in months has less to do with per-token price and more with the number of tokens, as agentic AI use outpaces token price drops. Aside from that, OpenRouter, Darkbloom’s primary client, has started prioritizing decode speed, quantization, and prefill speed, putting users without a top-of-the-line Max or Ultra Apple chip at a disadvantage.

Despite that, Darkbloom says it will not sit idly by and let some users lose out because of these changes. The network is analyzing model demand to advise members on which models are in higher demand right now. It’s also looking at other kinds of workload beyond inference, including distributed CI/CD jobs, improving network performance to better compete against other computer providers/aggregators, and finding other clients outside of OpenRouter.
