#10248. How to Organize CSS with Cascade Layers in Large Web Projects

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

As a website grows, its stylesheet often becomes harder to maintain. A rule added to fix one component can unexpectedly affect another, and developers may find themselves adding increasingly specific selectors to override previous styles. Over time, these changes make CSS difficult to understand and update.

CSS cascade layers provide a way to organize styles into named groups and control their priority. Instead of relying entirely on selector specificity and the order of declarations, developers can establish a predictable structure for their stylesheets.

CSS determines which declarations apply by considering factors such as origin, importance, cascade layer priority, specificity, and source order. When several rules target the same element, understanding these factors helps explain why one style overrides another.

If all three rules apply to the same button under normal author styles, the ID selector wins because it has greater specificity.

This can become inconvenient when a project contains third-party styles, reusable components, and application-specific overrides. Developers may respond by adding more specific selectors, making future changes increasingly difficult.

Cascade layers offer another approach: establish a clear priority order before writing individual component rules.

A useful starting point is to divide styles according to their purpose. For example, a project might contain reset styles, third-party styles, reusable components, and page-specific adjustments.

You can establish these layers at the beginning of your stylesheet:

The declaration at the top establishes the order for normal declarations in these layers. For normal author styles, later layers have higher priority than earlier layers, regardless of selector specificity between those layers.

This means a simple selector in a higher-priority layer can override a more specific selector in a lower-priority layer.

For more background on building adaptable interfaces, developers can explore SitePoint's introduction to CSS container queries, which explains another way to structure maintainable component styles.

Not every project needs the same number of layers. A small website may require only a few, while a large application may benefit from a more detailed structure.

This is a suggested structure rather than a mandatory convention. The important part is defining the priority order intentionally and applying it consistently.

Cascade layers can help avoid selectors that become unnecessarily complicated.

For normal declarations, the second rule wins because overrides has higher layer priority, even though the first selector contains an ID and more class selectors.

This can make changes easier to review because developers do not need to keep increasing selector specificity to achieve the intended result.

However, layer order should not become an excuse to place every exception in a global override layer. Keep related styles close to their components and use overrides only when the exception is intentional.

Cascade layers have rules that developers need to understand before adopting them across an existing codebase.

For normal declarations, later layers have higher priority. For declarations marked !important, the layer priority is reversed. Important declarations in earlier layers take precedence over important declarations in later layers.

Unlayered normal author styles also take precedence over normal declarations inside author cascade layers. This matters when integrating a layered stylesheet with older CSS that remains outside the layer structure.

The MDN documentation on CSS cascade layers explains these priority rules and provides additional examples.

Before reorganizing a production stylesheet, test how existing third-party rules, inline styles, and important declarations interact with the new structure.

Rewriting an entire stylesheet at once can introduce regressions. A safer approach is to define the intended layer order, migrate one group of styles at a time, and compare the rendered interface after each change.

Start with styles that are easy to isolate, such as resets or a small set of reusable components. Check buttons, forms, navigation, and responsive layouts at several viewport sizes.

Browser developer tools can help identify which declaration is winning and whether it belongs to the expected layer. Visual regression tests are also useful when the application has many shared components.

For teams, documenting the layer order and agreeing on where new styles belong can prevent the stylesheet from becoming disorganized again.

CSS cascade layers provide developers with a practical way to manage style priority in growing applications. By separating resets, vendor styles, components, and utilities into a clear hierarchy, teams can reduce specificity conflicts and make stylesheets easier to maintain.

The best results come from a deliberate layer structure, consistent naming, and careful testing during migration. Cascade layers do not remove the need to understand CSS specificity, but they give developers another tool for controlling how styles interact.

It is hoped game testing can provide insight into recent AI agent headlines, good and bad.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AI researchers at ycombinator and Olam Labs recently tested AI agent swarms in gaming scenarios. Minecraft and its various game modes were the sandboxes where 100s of agents were unleashed to work together or as adversaries. The idea seems to be that the in-game collaborations and conflicts would provide insight into real-world headline-grabbing cases with AI agents involved.

This study might help us see what happened, good and bad, in recent AI swarm cases, and thus developers of this technology can improve it. The blog mentions two contrasting cases, which were the thousand-agent swarms that “recently obliterated hundreds of open mathematical problems,” and the “extremely dangerous” OpenAI-Hugging Face incident. This research is built on prior work and frameworks, specifically repos like Mindcraft and scripting tools in Mineflayer.

The first AI swarm testing plan was a simple collaborative one. During this test phase, the researchers tasked 50 agents with building a thriving civilization in three hours. It seems to have tested 50 GPT-6.1 Sol agents here, but not any Claude agents.

Probably the most interesting insight from this ‘Civ50’ test was that the 50-strong colony never really thrived. After the first night saw zombies take out a large number of agents (but with little penalty) it was noted that “many ambitious initial tasks were abandoned.”

From then on, the colonists cautiously survived, and there was a tendency toward bureaucracy. As an example, “four agents spent 11 minutes appointing a ‘sole fertilizer operator’ for a single piece of bone meal on a single wheat block.” The researchers say this AI agent swarm behavior “felt similar to patterns seen while working with coding agents.”

Next up, the researchers decided to put AI agent swarms into conflict. First they tested them in Bed Wars, which is a kind of team PvP capture the base, while defending yours game mode. The setup was 10 GPT‑6.1 Sol vs. 10 Claude Opus 5.5 agents.

“GPT agents came up with much more aggressive and elaborate strategies,” noted the researchers. Claude was more defensive. It was also interesting to observe contrasts in how the teams handled problems. “A tool bug stopped block-breaking. GPT diagnosed it and shared workarounds; several Claudes concluded breaking was impossible.”

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Finally, the agents were tested in Mindustry, a kind of light RTS combat game with tower defense mode in Minecraft. “The GPTs won 6-1 against the Claudes in the 7 games,” which was a great result for the OpenAI-created agents. Researchers concluded that GPTs acted more like a hive, while Claudes had a single leader but a democratic-style decision process. In Mindustry, the former was the foundation for a more consistently winning strategy.

These swarms are open sourced so you can run your own, if you are interested in tweaking and testing. The source blog post ends with some open questions that you can check hands-on, offering some directions for investigative probing. For example, it is suggested that comparing different mixes of models and varying sizes of agent swarms in Minecraft might raise worthwhile conclusions.

#10243. Lenovo's 'encrypted' fingerprint USB drive is just an unencrypted SD card in a fancy case

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The fingerprint only switched the USB connection on; nothing on the card was encrypted.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Security researcher Xusheng Li found that a Lenovo ThinkPlus FU100 fingerprint USB stick, sold as an AES-256-encrypted device for data security, ended up being just a plain SD card internally, HackingPassion reports. The fingerprint reader only enabled the USB connection, and nothing on the card was actually encrypted. An AI-assisted follow-up last week discovered what Li calls backdoor accounts on another maker’s drive, as well as hardcoded keys in other supposedly secure USB storage products.

The FU100 is “just an SD card in a fancy case,” Li said in a March conference talk. It is, as a result, trivial for anyone with physical access to get to the drive’s data. One of his takeaways: “‘Hardware security’ can be pure marketing.” This isn’t the first time drive makers’ own encryption has failed, and it won’t be the last.

The original ThinkPlus FU100 analysis happened last year, with an interim fix using epoxy over the card. A later fix by Lenovo, put out early this year for the FU100, FU200 and TU800 drives, as well as the TSD303 SSD, requires owners to call a China service line, then either send devices back for replacement or get an on-site firmware upgrade. Lenovo advises backing up data first and strongly recommends wiping the drive before sending it in, which is quite an inconvenience. Lenovo rated the advisory’s severity as high, and one of its three CVEs covers the configuration software, which could let a local, logged-in user bypass device authentication and enroll an untrusted fingerprint.

The same research also turned up a different flaw in Lenovo’s TSD303 external SSD. Its data actually was encrypted, but the drive sent the correct password back over USB in plain text for the software to compare, making it easy to capture. The first fix came three months after the initial report, using block cipher encryption with a key derived from a timestamp-like value. Lenovo’s software could derive that key, and the first software fix is vulnerable if taken apart, making it not useful for protecting data if a device is stolen. A later fix, late last year, has the drive check the typed password itself, so the correct one never leaves the drive.

The same software development kit used for the drive covered nine other devices from multiple vendors, most of which could also reveal passwords to a savvy actor. In addition, two left data unencrypted on a microSD card. The full list of vendors was blacked out on Li’s slides.

AI agent use for driving reverse-engineering tools continues to increase. AI has also been exposing more security flaws recently. Li used OpenAI’s Codex, an AI programming-centric tool, with his employer’s reverse-engineering tool against other vendors’ protection schemes. Netac’s tool took about seven minutes to break, as Codex found a fixed key built into the program, along with the user’s password written to diagnostic logs.

Copy protection differs from direct access, as the intent is to allow the recipient to view files, just not make copies of them. KakaSoft’s USB Copy Protection has an internal password the program builds itself, which Codex rebuilt to decrypt a test file without the user’s password in around 26 minutes. Nexcopy’s Copy Secure took about 10 minutes, with its key unlocked by a certificate built into the viewer app. Separately, SecZure’s YutianDun has two hidden “backdoor” accounts with simple passwords, discovered with time-travel debugging, stepping through a timeline trace. However, Li’s comments suggest that malicious intent cannot be assumed.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Li gave no warning to KakaSoft, Nexcopy, or SecZure, arguing that a drive already in an attacker’s hands can’t easily be recalled or updated. He said that “client-side validation is never secure” and you should always verify security claims on your own. Netac had no patch as of Li’s March talk. An example of things done right would be an open-source USB stick that does encryption properly, even though it is built on a microSD card, deriving its key from the user’s password, so reading the card alone gets an attacker nothing.

#10240. Elon Musk says Neuralink's 'BlindSight' is ready to cure blindness

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The technology is reportedly "ready from an engineering standpoint."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Elon Musk’s Neuralink has been pushing advancements in the brain-computer interface (BCI) space, with patients now able to play World of Warcraft through a Neuralink brain implant. However, the billionaire founder gave a recent update on its second product, which will be Blindsight. Musk claims that it will “enable even those who are completely blind or have never seen to see,” and that it’s already “ready from an engineering standpoint” and “just waiting on regulatory approval.”

Neuralink was founded in 2016, but it didn't begin human trials until 2024. The progress also started small, with the first implantee starting with moving a mouse cursor “just by thinking.” It has since advanced its technology, with Musk seemingly launching its first product on X and calling it Telepathy. “It enables high-speed communication with computers or humans simply by thinking,” Musk wrote. “Output bandwidth will vastly exceed the fastest human speed or typing in a few years. Read that sentence again.”

Our second product is called Blindsight and it will, as the name suggests, enable even those who are completely blind or have never seen to see. It is ready from an engineering standpoint. Just waiting on regulatory approval.October 30, 2026

While Neuralink isn’t the first to work on BCIs, it’s one of the more successful companies, partially because of Musk’s financial backing. Other companies are working on similar technologies, though. For example, Meta is developing a non-invasive magnetoencephalography scanner that lets users turn thoughts into keypresses, while Chinese outfit NeuroXess, founded in 2021, is accelerating toward human trials this year.

However, Blindsight is unique, as most of these technologies currently focus on the brain sending signals to control external machines. Cochlear implants and sensory neuroprosthetics already send signals from sensors into the brain, allowing deaf patients to hear again and patients to regain the ability to feel through touch. Neuralink’s Blindsight, on the other hand, aims to create an artificial visual pathway, allowing a patient to use a camera to capture visual images and then convert the signals into something that the brain can understand via a BCI.

These medical advancements could help people with limited movement or those who have lost the ability to move their bodies regain independence, as they could use BCI to control machines with their thoughts. But Musk's mention that the technology could enable high-speed communication with humans simply by thinking could also mean much more than helping people regain mobility. Beijing announced last year that BCI technology is a nationally strategic sector, and with the renewed arms race between the East and West in drones, lasers, and new technologies, it’s not far-fetched that someone, somewhere, has thought of using BCI to create super soldiers.

#10236. What Is the Best UI Testing Tool? 8 Tools Compared by the Job They Do (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The ideal UI testing tool is the one that discovers what your users discover first: broken layouts, usability issues that no longer work, and new accessibility issues in all the browsers your users use before a change goes into the repository. In 2026, most frontend teams don't rely on a single tool to do all of that. The best setups involve two layers: a test runner layer that launches the browser and monitors behavior, and a UI testing and review platform that visually checks the outcome and routes changes to the right stakeholders for sign-off.

Even with the right stack, it still relies on what your team needs to catch. Different teams will focus on different points: a QA team trying to automate checkout flows, a design system team defending shared components, and a single developer working on a tight budget will all be somewhere different. This guide is categorized by the job these UI testing tools do, then connects them.

Most "best UI testing tool" lists rank test runners and visual testing platforms against each other as if they were interchangeable. They aren't. UI testing covers four distinct jobs:

Functional testing: Does the UI respond correctly when a user clicks, types, or hovers?

Visual testing: Does the UI still look right across layout, color, typography, and spacing?

Accessibility testing: Can people using keyboards and screen readers use it, and did this change introduce new violations?

Cross-browser and device coverage: Does it work in Safari on mobile, not just Chrome on a developer's laptop?

There are also other levels of UI tests. Unit tests test the logic but don't interact with the UI. End-to-end (E2E) tests represent the full user journey in a browser but are difficult to set up, hard to troubleshoot when the bug is a visual detail, and can't practically test every state. Between the extremes of unit tests and E2E tests, component tests simulate each component's behavior in a real browser using fixed inputs, offering speed closer to unit tests and user-level coverage closer to E2E. Many teams maintain a thin layer of E2E tests for critical journeys, and they have component tests for the dozens of states a component may be in.

These tools are organized by job so you can stack them rather than choose one winner.

Prices are taken from each vendor's site or published comparisons as of October 2026. Check current pricing before you buy.

These platforms sit on top of your test runner. They capture rendered UI, compare it to approved baselines, and give your team a place to review what changed.

Chromatic is a UI testing and review platform made by the Storybook team. It catches visual, interaction, and accessibility bugs in components and pages before they merge, and it gives developers, designers, and product managers one place to review UI changes and sign off. Chromatic's team is the core maintainer of Storybook, which has about 90,000 GitHub stars and 17 million weekly npm downloads, and a Vitest core maintainer on the team built Chromatic's Vitest integration. Chromatic is used by 45 Fortune 100 companies, including GitHub, Shopify, Microsoft, Adobe, the BBC, and monday.com

Pricing: Free plan with 5,000 snapshots per month (Chrome only), unlimited projects and users. Starter is $179/month for 35,000 snapshots and adds Safari, Firefox, and Edge. Pro is $399/month for 85,000 snapshots. Enterprise is custom, with SSO, SCIM, SLA support, and data retention. TurboSnap snapshots count as 0.2 of a regular snapshot.

Trade-offs: Chromatic is strongest when your UI is built from components. Teams with no component tests can start from Playwright or Cypress E2E tests, but they'll get the most value once components are covered too. Snapshot usage also grows with each browser and viewport you add, so plan coverage before you scale it.

Best for: Design system teams, product teams that want designers and PMs to approve UI changes, React Native teams, and Vitest-first teams that want visual testing without baselines stored in the repo.

Percy is BrowserStack's visual testing platform. It captures screenshots from your existing tests, renders them across browsers and responsive widths, and highlights pixel differences for review.

Strengths: Framework-agnostic SDKs for Playwright, Cypress, Selenium, and Storybook. App Percy covers native mobile apps. The free plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month with unlimited users and projects.

Trade-offs: Percy is page-oriented, and each page rendered in each browser at each width counts as a separate screenshot, so usage climbs quickly with cross-browser coverage. Percy compares feature branches against a base branch, which teams with complex branching should test early. Reviewers see rendered screenshots rather than an inspectable copy of the code under test, so debugging a diff usually means reproducing it locally.

Pricing: Free tier, then paid plans with fixed screenshot allowances and overage.

Best for: Teams already paying for BrowserStack that want visual checks on page-level E2E tests.

Applitools Eyes uses Visual AI to compare screenshots the way a person would, with match levels that can ignore content changes and focus on layout. Its Ultrafast Grid re-renders captured page states across many browser, viewport, and device combinations.

Strengths: Mature AI matching, broad browser and device coverage, Figma and Storybook testing, accessibility testing, and an MCP server. Higher tiers add AI-driven functional testing and plain-English test authoring.

Trade-offs: Applitools is priced and packaged for enterprises. There's a free trial but no free plan, and the Starter plan is $667/month billed annually for 100,000 component checkpoints or 1,000 page checkpoints. Professional and Enterprise are custom-priced.

Best for: Large QA organizations with big cross-browser matrices and budget for a dedicated visual AI platform.

Argos is an open source visual testing platform with SDKs for Playwright, Storybook, Vitest, and Cypress. It adds cloud baselines and team review on top of the screenshots your tests already produce.

Strengths: Simple flat pricing. The Hobby plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month, and Pro is $100/month for 35,000 screenshots, with a budget cap available.

Trade-offs: Argos focuses on visual diffs. Argos compares screenshots captured by your own test runs, so browser coverage depends on what your CI runs, and it doesn't offer hosted mobile simulators. It also has a smaller ecosystem than the larger platforms.

Best for: Small teams that want hosted visual review at a predictable price.

Test runners click, type, and assert. They're free and open source, and every platform above builds on them. On their own, they offer limited visual testing: built-in screenshot assertions store baselines in your repository, rendering can drift between laptops and CI, and designers have no review interface.

Playwright is Microsoft's open-source browser automation framework. It runs tests across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit, with auto-waiting, trace viewer debugging and code generation, and supports JavaScript, TypeScript, Python, Java, and .NET.

Visual testing: Playwright's toHaveScreenshot assertion compares screenshots against baselines stored in Git. It works, but baselines must be regenerated per operating system, and accepting a change means rerunning with an update flag.

Best for: Teams automating critical user journeys such as sign-up, checkout and login. Pair it with a platform like Chromatic for visual review across real browsers.

Cypress is an open-source testing tool that runs inside the browser, with time-travel debugging that shows the app at each step of a test. It supports both E2E and component testing.

Visual testing: Cypress has no built-in visual comparison and relies on plugins or a platform such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools. Cypress Cloud, its paid service, adds recording, parallelization, flake detection and add-ons for accessibility and UI coverage.

Best for: Frontend developers who want fast feedback and an interactive test runner while they build.

Storybook is the open source workshop for building UI components in isolation. Each story captures one component state, and those stories double as test cases.

Testing features: Interaction tests with play functions and Testing Library, accessibility checks with the a11y addon, and a Vitest integration that runs stories as component tests.

Visual testing: Storybook doesn't diff screenshots on its own. Chromatic, built by the same team, is its visual testing and review layer.

Best for: Design system and component library teams, and any team practicing component-driven development.

Vitest Browser Mode runs component tests in a real browser instead of a simulated DOM, using Playwright or WebdriverIO under the hood.

Visual testing: Vitest's toMatchScreenshot assertion stores screenshots in the repository. For large projects, this can bloat the repo and baselines drift between machines. Chromatic can take Vitest Browser Mode tests with no code changes and run them in a stable cloud environment instead.

Best for: Teams already writing Vitest tests who want them to check how the UI looks without adopting a new framework.

Start from the problem your team is trying to fix:

"Shared component changes keep breaking other teams' screens." Test at the component level with Storybook or Vitest Browser Mode and Chromatic, so the change shows up on the pull request that caused it.

"Our Playwright tests pass, but the layout still breaks in Safari." Functional assertions don't check appearance. Add a visual testing platform that renders in Safari, such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools.

"We turned off screenshot tests because of false alarms." Look for deterministic component states and flake controls such as Chromatic's SteadySnap, or AI matching such as Applitools.

"Designers find UI bugs after release." Choose a platform with a review and approval workflow, and make sign-off a required pull request check.

"AI coding agents keep inventing components." Give agents access to your real component library, for example through Chromatic's hosted MCP, and run every agent-built change through the same UI tests as human code.

"We have no budget." Use Playwright or Vitest screenshot assertions, or start on the free tiers of Chromatic, Percy or Argos.

A practical, well-covered setup for a React web app looks like this:

Unit tests in Vitest for logic and utilities.

Storybook stories or Vitest Browser Mode tests for every meaningful component state, with interaction tests on key components.

Chromatic running visual, interaction and accessibility tests on those components in Chrome, Firefox, Safari and Edge on every pull request, with designers assigned as reviewers.

A thin layer of Playwright E2E tests for sign-up, checkout and other critical journeys, also captured in Chromatic so page-level visual changes go through the same review.

This keeps most UI coverage in fast, deterministic component tests, while E2E tests confirm the journeys that matter most still work end to end.

Playwright is the best free tool for E2E UI testing, and Storybook is the best free tool for testing component states. For visual testing, Chromatic, Percy and Argos all offer free tiers of around 5,000 snapshots or screenshots per month.

UI testing is the broad practice of checking that an interface works for users, including functional, visual and accessibility checks. Visual testing is one part of it: comparing rendered UI against approved baselines to catch unintended changes in layout, color, typography and spacing.

No. Chromatic was built by the Storybook team, but it also runs visual tests from Vitest Browser Mode, Playwright and Cypress tests, and from React Native Storybook on iOS and Android simulators.

Playwright's toHaveScreenshot covers basic visual checks, but baselines live in your repo, rendering can differ between machines, and there's no review interface for designers. Teams with several reviewers or many browsers usually add a platform on top.

Most teams use both. Component tests cover individual UI states quickly and reliably, while a smaller set of E2E tests confirms that critical user journeys work across the whole app.

#10231. Open-source USB tool unlocks Nvidia's half-speed GPU AI limiter on Windows

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

It runs from a USB stick before Windows loads and has to run again on every boot.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A new open-source UEFI tool boots from USB and unlocks significant extra tensor performance on RTX 20, 30, and 40-series desktop and laptop GPUs, making the half-speed AI throttle hack a whole lot easier to try on Windows. No VBIOS flash or patched driver needed: the tool runs from a FAT32 stick before the Nvidia driver loads at every boot, and then voila, the developer’s own laptop RTX 4060 hit 65% higher FP16 math.

The “UEFI pre-driver NVIDIA GPU unlocker,” or nv-uefi-unlocker project, removes Nvidia’s tensor limiter using the community V67 payload with read-back verification. This tricks Nvidia’s signed Booter firmware into opening the write lock on two speed-selector registers, allowing the tensor units to run at full rate. The unlock can now run entirely from a bootable USB drive, on Windows as well as Linux, whereas previously it worked primarily through manually installed Linux modules, with Windows support only experimental. The read-back verification part makes things safer, which was a concern with the experimental builds.

The release ships only scripts and self-built binaries, without any Nvidia firmware in the download. Instead, one of the scripts grabs Nvidia’s public 610.43.03 driver packages, verifies them, then rebuilds the firmware files locally. The project credits Zagitta’s gpu-unlock as its porting source and builds on several other community projects, including the RTX 40 fork gpu-unlock-ada and an earlier Windows EFI tool. Having one tool for the many GeForce owners on Windows should significantly open up the hack to a wider audience. Previously, it was scarier-looking, and project progression is rapid these days with AI tools.

The steps right now involve downloading the release, running that script, and then copying the rebuilt files and a BOOTX64.EFI for booting to a FAT32-formatted USB stick. The unlocker can then be run from a one-time boot menu by using the spacebar within 30 seconds for confirmation. Once complete, things are handed off to the respective OS loader.

The unlocker runs in a firmware stage before the OS loads Nvidia’s GPU driver, which means there’s no need to flash the card’s video BIOS or to modify the driver; as the FAQ puts it, the unlock doesn’t “persist through power loss.” The trade-off is running it again on every boot. The system must be booted in UEFI mode with Secure Boot either off or set to trust a self-signed EFI. For a game’s anti-cheat that requires Secure Boot, the tool “CANNOT bypass this requirement,” a developer team member relayed on Reddit. RTX 20 owners have to run a bundled script once to switch the Windows Nvidia driver into GSP firmware mode, before restarting.

Laptop GPUs were already supported by earlier projects, but here one of the developer’s own test cards is a laptop GPU, and laptop results are logged card by card. For laptops, the discrete Nvidia GPU must be powered at entry, which means dGPU-only, or MUX, mode is recommended. On Linux distributions such as Ubuntu, runtime power management can power down the GPU when idle and silently erase the unlock. The English FAQ covers workarounds for this, with instructions and guidelines for other situations.

Compatibility otherwise includes RTX 20-series support listed as “fully compatible; some models have known issues,” while RTX 30-series cards are “fully compatible,” and the tool is “fully compatible on laptops and desktops” using RTX 40-series cards. The GTX 16 is “theoretically compatible” with verification pending, but other cards, including pre-Turing, the GA100, and RTX 50-series cards, are unsupported. The project routes support by chip family, so a card being covered doesn’t mean that specific card has been tested. Cards verified by the tool’s own Windows results are listed in the table below.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

RTX 2080, 2080 Ti and 2080 Super fail at boot or handoff; GTX 16 untested; needs GSP firmware mode and a restart first

RTX 3080 (incl. LHR), 3060 Ti, 3060 12GB, 3080 Ti Laptop, 3070 Laptop

RTX 3050 untested; persistence on Thunderbolt eGPUs not guaranteed

RTX 4090, 4080, 4070 Ti Super, 4070 Super, 4070, 4060 Ti 8GB and 16GB, 4060 Laptop, 4050 Laptop

Results compare each card before and after the unlock on the same test, but the tests differ from row to row, so they shouldn’t be compared across cards.

The developer’s own desktop RTX 2070 went from about 34 TFLOPS in FP16 matrix multiply at 8192 to around 62 TFLOPS in the screenshots below.

Some caveats still exist, and Zagitta, whose gpu-unlock kicked off the GeForce unlocks, now says in its FAQ that “I don’t run windows and don’t plan to maintain it,” pointing Windows users to a separate port and warning to be careful with unofficial Windows installers. Results from the unlock are also contingent on power and thermal limits. The new project says it can be combined with the nvoc power-limit unlock, but warns this “can exceed factory power and thermal envelopes.” A per-card ROM hash check, which the project’s own safety rules require, isn’t built yet, and the unlock’s persistence on Thunderbolt eGPUs “is not guaranteed.”

Other cards, including the RTX 2080 and the RTX 2080 Ti, are under review. No dates are promised. The pro-card tensor feature can now be unlocked for many, though, and as simply as using a USB boot drive, which will get even easier with a boot-from-disk toggle the team says is in the works. RTX 50-series owners have to wait, for now, as the team is still trying to crack those cards, which have “a lot more security” than it anticipated.

Samsung and SK hynix control roughly 83% of HBM revenue as AI data centers intensify competition for memory chips

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

OpenAI CEO Sam Altman has met with about 50 senior Samsung executives in South Korea, as the company seeks to expand its Artificial Intelligence and memory capacity, Bloomberg reports. Altman’s visit to South Korea follows his recent comments about how the world's limited memory supply should be allocated. In an interview on the Chick Pink podcast this week, the OpenAI chief argued that DRAM sitting inside personal computers is often underused, describing it as “quite terrible”. He suggested that data centers could make more intensive use of memory by sharing computer resources across AI workloads. The argument has drawn widespread criticism given OpenAI’s own services like ChatGPT rely heavily on centralized computing infrastructure.

The meeting, which took place at Samsung’s Resources Development Center in Yongin on Saturday, October 30, brought together chiefs from the company’s financial, electronics, and biotechnology businesses. Confirming the meeting, Samsung Korea said the discussion revolved around strengthening existing relationships between the two companies, particularly a letter of Intent signed in October 20225 to cooperate on global AI infrastructure, including supplying memory semiconductors for OpenAI’s Stargate project. “The Samsung executives and CEO Sam Altman discussed the direction of AI technology development and its potential applications, as well as new markets and business opportunities that will emerge from it,” Samsung stated.

Samsung, however, emphasized more on AI transformation and future business opportunities, rather than announcing a new memory supply agreement. Reportedly, Altman also presented Samsung’s AI Center head with a Codex Micro Keyboard, a device designed for use with OpenAI’s coding agent. The latest meeting comes as demand from AI centers continues to soar, with AMD forecasting DRAM revenue will jump 177% in 2026 to about $416 billion. Trendforce also estimates DRAM supply will fall 1% to 2% short of demand this year, with the imbalance expected to worsen in 2027. New manufacturing capacity may take time to come, which means the shortage could persist into 2028, as per a recent warning by Micron.

For now, demand for data centers is facing major competition, especially from PCs, smartphones, and other electronics, for limited memory supplies (and may be nowhere close to peaking). High-bandwidth memory (HBM), a specialized form of DRAM used in AI devices, has become particularly important as companies deploy increasingly powerful computing systems.

Samsung and SK Hynix are among the world's leading memory manufacturers, giving South Korea a central role in supplying the hardware behind the AI expansion. According to Counterpoint Research, both companies controlled about 63% of global DRAM revenues in the second quarter of 2026. Their influence is greater in HBM, where the two South Korean manufacturers accounted for roughly 83% of global revenue. This has prompted other technology executives to approach the country, with AMD CEO Lisa Su visiting earlier this month to discuss securing additional HBM supplies from SAMSUNG, saying, "It is important to plan three to five years ahead with our partners."

Meanwhile, Samsung and SK Hynix have been upgrading Chinese factories to meet the surging demand for AI-related memory and storage products. However, those particular upgrades concern NAND flash production, rather than DRAM — but they show just how manufacturers are adjusting capacity as demand shifts towards data center hardware.

#10225. Android 17 is on 1% of devices after four months, Android 16 on over 25%

Google has updated its Android distribution numbers with the latest update showing that Android 17 is on just 1% of devices, while Android 16 now makes up the biggest slice of the pie.

Updated as of October 19, Google’s latest Android distribution numbers offer a peek into the rollout speed of Android 17. Having released to Pixel phones in June, the update has been available for roughly four months.

At this point, though, Android 17 makes up just 1% of active Android devices, Google’s data shows.

That’s compared to over 25% for Android 16, which released last year. Android 16 was on 7.5% of devices in Google’s last update in December 2025/January 2026, which landed several months after Google released the update. The timing here requires context, as major players like Samsung have only started widely rolling out their Android 17 updates in the past few weeks and are very unlikely to be included in this 1%.

Fragmentation issues still plague Google's ability to control it's experiences. Seemingly no plans to address.

The growth of Android 16 is pretty impressive, too, as older versions tend to be still be more widely in use at this point in the schedule, but Android 16 has managed to consolidate quite a bit.

Google dropped Android 5 (Lollipop) from its data with this update, with Android 5.1 making up just 0.2% of active devices. Android 11, for reasons unclear, still holds over 10%, while Android 12 has dropped to 9.6%. Android 11 has had a higher share in the past few updates.
